Categorical Anomaly Matching
Andrea Antinucci, Christian Copetti, Yuhan Gai, Sakura Schäfer-Nameki

Abstract
Matching ’t Hooft anomalies is a powerful tool for constraining the low-energy dynamics of quantum systems and their allowed renormalization group (RG) flows. For non-invertible (or categorical) symmetries, however, a key challenge has been the lack of a precise framework to characterize and quantify anomalies. We address this by identifying tensor functors between UV and IR symmetry categories as central to capturing these constraints. To this end, we introduce Anomalous Simple Categories (ASCies) as fundamental building blocks of categorical anomalies. A given symmetry category may support multiple ASCies, each encoding distinct anomalous features. These structures naturally arise in the context of the Symmetry Topological Field Theory (SymTFT), where tensor functors correspond to RG-interfaces between UV and IR SymTFTs, and ASCies are realized as particular such interfaces satisfying simple, universal criteria. We demonstrate the utility of this framework through examples involving anomalous 0-form, higher-form, and crucially, non-invertible symmetries in various spacetime dimensions.



I
Introduction

What is the maximal amount of dynamical information that can be extracted from symmetries and anomalies? In studying RG-flows, one typically states that microscopic symmetries must be realized at long distances, and their anomalies must be reproduced by the infrared effective theory. This principle strongly constrains the possible IR scenarios and
can even provide a criterion for whether two UV models flow to the same theory in the IR, i.e. are IR-dual.

Anomaly matching is particularly important, and its full implications are yet to be uncovered, when applied to the vast generalization to higher-form symmetries
Gaiotto:2014kfa
and most recently to non-invertible, or equivalently categorical, symmetries
Chang:2018iay
;
Kaidi:2021xfk
;
Choi:2021kmx
;
Bhardwaj:2022yxj
(for reviews see
Schafer-Nameki:2023jdn
;
Shao:2023gho
).

In practice, implementing anomaly constraints can be challenging. For instance, some symmetries of the UV theory may become trivial at low energies, while new, emergent symmetries can arise that match UV anomalies via fractionalization
Senthil:1999czm
;
Essin:2013rca
;
Barkeshli:2014cna
;
Chen:2014wse
, or more recently, transmutation
Seiberg:2025bqy
.
As a result, the space of possible IR theories is much richer than naive expectations suggest, and it becomes a nontrivial task to systematically
organize
and
classify
the full set of anomaly constraints.

At the same time, the very notion of an
anomaly for a non-invertible symmetry
is considerably more subtle. Progress so far has largely focused on determining whether a given symmetry is anomalous or not
Thorngren:2019iar
;
Inamura:2021wuo
;
Kaidi:2023maf
;
Zhang:2023wlu
;
Cordova:2023bja
;
Antinucci:2023ezl
. However, an explicit
quantification of categorical anomalies
– a necessary ingredient for anomaly matching – has until now remained out of reach.

It is this challenge that we will address in the current work.
Consider a theory in
d
spacetime dimensions.
Our starting point is the observation that a complete formulation of the anomaly matching problem requires viewing symmetries as tensor
(d{-}1)
-categories
\mathcal{C}
.
The categorical structure is not merely mathematical embellishment (even in the case of invertible symmetries), as it encodes not only the symmetry generators and their composition, but also, through the (higher) associativity constraints
DouglasReutter
;
Copetti:2023mcq
;
Carqueville:2023jhb
, the ’t Hooft anomalies.

Let us denote the UV and IR symmetry categories by
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
, respectively. Our goal is to formulate a criterion that determines when a given pair of such symmetries is compatible with ’t Hooft anomaly matching. More precisely, we seek to characterize the class of maps that preserve both the symmetry structure and the anomaly data along an RG-flow.
The appropriate notion describing such a map is that of a
tensor functor

F:\quad\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}\,.

(I.1)

The composition of symmetry generators is encoded in the tensor structure
\otimes
of the symmetry category. A tensor functor is, by definition, a functor between categories that is compatible with this structure. Specifically, it satisfies

F(\mathsf{D}_{1}\otimes\mathsf{D}_{2})\cong F(\mathsf{D}_{1})\otimes F(\mathsf{D}_{2})\,,

(I.2)

as well as compatibility with (higher) associativity constraints.

In the simplest case of invertible 0-form symmetries, described by groups
G_{\text{UV}}
and
G_{\text{IR}}
with anomalies
\omega_{i}\in H^{d+1}(BG_{i},U(1))
, a tensor functor corresponds to a group homomorphism
\varphi:G_{\text{UV}}\rightarrow G_{\text{IR}}
, together with an identification

[\varphi^{*}\omega_{\text{IR}}]=[\omega_{\text{UV}}]\,.

(I.3)

As we will see in several examples, even when only invertible symmetries are present – but of different degrees – a much more subtle structure can arise. In such cases, tensor functors may encode anomaly matching via symmetry fractionalization.

When non-invertible symmetries are also involved, the situation becomes dramatically more intricate – and more interesting.
The existence or absence of a tensor functor between two symmetry categories encodes a remarkable amount of physical information. It is therefore essential to develop this concept in detail.
Suppose a microscopic model has symmetry
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
.
Then, we will show that compatibility with a low-energy effective field theory possessing symmetry
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
requires the existence of a tensor functor (
I.1
).
That is, a low-energy theory is consistent with the UV symmetry only if such a functor exists. This provides the most general and modern formulation of anomaly matching: it not only recovers the textbook criteria in standard cases, but also encompasses the categorical statement that
a symmetry is anomalous if it does not admit an
SPT phase
, meaning it lacks a fiber functor – i.e., a tensor functor to the trivial category
(d{-}1)\mathsf{Vec}
.

What has so far been missing is a
quantitative measure of anomalies for non-invertible symmetries
. One of the central insights of the present work is that anomalous building blocks of a categorical symmetry
\mathcal{C}
can be identified by considering short exact sequences of tensor functors injecting into and projecting from
\mathcal{C}
.
This highlights the fundamental role of tensor functors in capturing the anomaly structure and constraining the possible IR physics.

Although tensor functors mathematically capture the full anomaly structure of categorical symmetries, it is often far more elegant – and physically transparent – to reformulate the problem in terms of the associated Symmetry Topological Field Theory (SymTFT). In fact, our main results and examples will be developed within this framework, which we will review below.

This reformulation becomes essential in higher-dimensions. For fusion higher-categories, the notion of a tensor functor is not always well-defined. For example, in (2+1)d theories with fusion 2-category symmetries, a notion of monoidal (or tensor) 2-functor has been defined in
baez1996higher
, but a general framework remains elusive. In contrast, the SymTFT approach admits natural extensions to higher categories. We will adopt this perspective to generalize our measure of anomalies to spacetime dimensions
d>2
.

\mathcal{T}_{\text{UV}}
\mathcal{T}_{\text{UV}}+\lambda\int\mathcal{O}
\leadsto
RG
\mathcal{T}_{\text{UV}}
\mathcal{T}_{\text{IR}}
\mathcal{I}

Figure 1:

RG-interface: At the top we start with the UV:
the left half-space is the
UV theory
\mathcal{T}_{\text{UV}}
, including the interface to the right half-space, which is
\mathcal{T}_{\text{UV}}
with the
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
-symmetric relevant deformation (shown in light blue). Along the RG-flow the deformed theory flows to the
IR theory
\mathcal{T}_{\text{IR}}
, separated by the RG-interface
\mathcal{I}
.
Such an interface defines a tensor functor between the symmetries of the UV and IR theories.

RG-interfaces and Tensor Functors.

To physically motivate the relevance of tensor functors, we connect them to the concept of RG-interfaces.
Consider a UV theory
\mathcal{T}_{\text{UV}}
with symmetry
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
, which flows under a symmetric relevant deformation
\mathcal{O}
to a low-energy effective theory
\mathcal{T}_{\text{IR}}
with faithfully acting symmetry
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
. A natural construction in this setting is the
RG-interface

Brunner:2007ur
;
Gaiotto:2012np
, obtained by turning on the deformation
\mathcal{O}
only in half of spacetime and flowing to the IR. See figure
1
.
The interface
\mathcal{I}
is
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
-symmetric, meaning that the
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
symmetry defects can pass through it topologically. In particular, it encodes a tensor functor (
I.1
), which captures the symmetry aspects of the RG-interface. The existence of such a functor imposes strong constraints on the IR theory. A key goal of this work is to isolate this kinematical problem – encoded in
F
– from the more dynamical aspects of RG-flows.

Anomalous Simple Categories.

While the ’t Hooft anomaly matching conditions are elegantly implemented by the mathematics of tensor functors, a refinement of this concept is needed in order to better quantify ’t Hooft anomalies, especially for non-invertible symmetries.
Intuitively, from a symmetry
\mathcal{C}
we would like to quotient out the largest possible anomaly-free subsymmetry, leaving behind a simpler generalized symmetry
\mathcal{S}
to express the ’t Hooft anomaly of the original symmetry
\mathcal{C}
.
Mathematically, this concept is encoded in the notion of a short exact sequence of tensor categories
bruguieres2011exact
:

\mathcal{N}\overset{I}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{C}\overset{P}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{S}\,,

(I.4)

where
I
is an embedding, and
P
is surjective. The interpretation of the sequence is the following:

•

\mathcal{N}
is chosen to be maximal (i.e. such that there is no other category that contains it and fits into this sequences) and corresponds to an anomaly-free sub-symmetry. The composition
f=I\circ P
provides a map which “forgets” the symmetry
\mathcal{N}
, i.e. a fiber functor.

•

\mathcal{S}
is the “quotient” symmetry and represents the anomalous part of the symmetry
\mathcal{C}
.

We will refer to a symmetry that has no non-anomalous sub-categories
\mathcal{N}
satisfying (
I.4
) as
anomalous simple category (ASCy)
– these are the simple building blocks of the anomalous parts of symmetries.

In turn any sequence (
I.4
) with
\mathcal{N}
maximal implies that
\mathcal{S}
is an ASCy and thereby captures the anomalous part of the symmetry
\mathcal{C}
.
Important to note is that a given symmetry
\mathcal{C}
may have several inequivalent pairs
(\mathcal{N}_{i},\mathcal{S}_{i})
associated to it that fit into a sequence such as (
I.4
). Each
\mathcal{N}_{i}
is a (maximal) non-anomalous sub-symmetry, whereas each ASCy
\mathcal{S}_{i}
captures different aspects of the anomaly of
\mathcal{C}
.

When applied to group-like symmetries the ASCies become precisely the anomalous quotient groups as required, however, the framework is far more general and applicable to any non-invertible/categorical symmetry.

SymTFT Realization.

While the characterization of anomalies via tensor functors – and the notion of ASCies through short exact sequences of such functors – offers a conceptual framework for quantifying anomalies of categorical symmetries, determining the relevant tensor functors in practice can be subtle, particularly in the case of general (i.e., non-invertible) categorical symmetries.
We show that recasting the problem in terms of the Symmetry Topological Field Theory (SymTFT) not only provides a precise reformulation of the above concepts, but also renders the entire framework more amenable to explicit computations.

The SymTFT
Ji:2019jhk
;
Gaiotto:2020iye
;
Apruzzi:2021nmk
;
Freed:2022qnc
, in a nutshell, separates the symmetry aspects from the dynamics of a theory. For a symmetry
\mathcal{C}
of a
d
-dimensional theory
\mathcal{T}
the SymTFT
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C})
is a
(d+1)
-dimensional TQFT that lives on an interval with two boundary conditions: one that encodes the symmetries –
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}
– which is a topological boundary condition. The other encodes the dynamics:
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{phys}}
.
As the SymTFT is topological the size of the interval is immaterial and can be collapsed at no cost to regain the original theory.

We will show that a tensor functor
F:\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\to\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
is equivalent to a configuration, where two SymTFTs
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})
and
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}})
are separated by a particular topological interface
\mathcal{I}_{F}
, which satisfies

\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{UV}}\times\mathcal{I}_{F}=\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{IR}}\,.

(I.5)

This means when we fuse the interface
\mathcal{I}_{F}
onto the symmetry boundary of the UV theory, we obtain the IR-symmetry boundary.
The situation is depicted in figure
2
.
The condition (
I.5
) can be formulated entirely in terms of the topological defects of the SymTFT and is computationally much easier to check than the conditions on tensor functors.

When applied to short exact sequences of tensor functors and ASCies in the context of higher dimensions and non-invertible symmetries, the SymTFT approach proves significantly easier to implement.

Applications.

We present numerous applications of this general framework.
For 1+1d theories it can be applied to any fusion category symmetry.
In this spacetime dimension, the analysis is completely general and systematic, thanks to the by now well-known results on condensable algebras. In turn, this connects to many of the mathematical results on tensor functors.
In terms of examples, we will recover first some of the known results on anomalous invertible (group) symmetries, corroborating the validity of our general approach. Then we extend the analysis to non-invertible symmetries, including the Tambara-Yamagami categories.

Although to our knowledge, tensor functors still need to be rigorously defined in higher dimensions, our SymTFT approach is easily extendable and we will apply these to examples in 2+1d and 3+1d with invertible and non-invertible symmetries.
For fusion 2-categories, that are symmetries of 2+1d theories, we can show many properties of tensor 2-functors and their realization in the SymTFT. Emboldened by this, in higher dimensions, we simply use the SymTFT approach as the defining framework for anomaly matching.
In terms of examples, we find e.g. generalizations of the Wang-Wen-Witten results
Wang:2017loc
to non-invertible symmetries.

z
x

\mathfrak{B}^{\text{phys}}_{\text{IR}}

\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}})

\mathcal{I}_{\text{RG}}

\mathcal{I}_{F}
between the two topological orders
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})
and

. The gapped symmetry boundary conditions
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{UV}}

\mathcal{I}_{\text{RG}}
separating the UV theory
\mathcal{T}_{\text{UV}}

. This type of figure will be shown in section

when discussing the RG-interfaces and tensor functors.

x

-plane, that we show frequently in the following as a cartoon of the 3d picture. This is shown in the lower part of the figure. Here
\mathcal{I}_{F}
is the SymTFT interface, and only after collapsing further the
x
-interval do we get
\mathcal{I}_{\text{RG}}
. Finally in the lower right hand corner, we show the projection onto a 1d picture.

LSM Anomalies for (Non-)Invertible Symmetries.

Finally, discrete spacetime symmetries can be incorporated into the framework, by extending the SymTFT to a symmetry enriched (SE) TQFT: the SESymTFT. We discuss Lieb-Schultz-Mattis (LSM) anomalies and discuss the UV-IR symmetry matching, using such an SESymTFT. The SE is by translations and an automorphism of the Drinfeld center of
\mathcal{C}
, i.e. the topological defects of the SymTFT. The existence of an LSM-anomaly is a simple criterion in the SymTFT – namely the absence of an automorphism invariant SPT phase. The anomaly matching uses the exact sequence of categories applied to the SESymTFT. We reproduce known invertible examples and give a non-invertible example, where the UV symmetry is
\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8})
and we consider the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}
triality symmetry as automorphism.

Plan of the Paper.

This paper is organized as follows. In section
II
, we explain how RG-interfaces between QFTs naturally lead to the mathematical notion of tensor functors. We introduce various structural properties of tensor functors, and use them to define the concept of Anomalous Simple Categories (ASCies), from short exact sequences of symmetry categories. These serve as concrete invariants to detect and quantify anomalies of categorical symmetries.
Section
III
begins with a review of key aspects of SymTFT needed for our construction. We then reformulate the existence of a tensor functor between symmetry categories within the SymTFT framework, leading to the Matching Equation. We further reinterpret the previously introduced properties of tensor functors in terms of physical constraints on interfaces.
In section
IV
, we study ASCies in more detail as tools to quantify anomalies for non-invertible symmetries, both from the tensor functor perspective and using the SymTFT formulation.
We substantiate and illustrate the general framework with numerous examples.
Section
V

studies higher dimensional systems. After a brief review of symmetry fractionalization and transmutation, we apply our framework to study anomaly matching in spacetime dimension
d>2
, and connect to symmetry-preserving gapped phases, relating to results of
Wang:2017loc
.
In section
VI
, we apply our discussion to discrete spacetime symmetries and study LSM-type anomaly matching.
We conclude in section
VII
with a summary and outlook on future directions.

II
RG-Interfaces and Tensor Functors

To motivate the role of tensor functors, we begin by examining their physical significance through a close analogy with RG-interfaces between QFTs. However, it is important to emphasize at the outset that tensor functors are a more general concept and need not arise solely from RG-interfaces.
A tensor functor encodes necessary conditions for UV and IR symmetries to be compatible, serving as a generalized version of the ’t Hooft anomaly matching condition.
We will also introduce the notion of Anomalous Simple Categories (ASCies), which provide a natural measure of ’t Hooft anomalies for non-invertible symmetries; this discussion will be expanded further in section
IV
.

An important point to keep in mind when generalizing to higher dimensions is that many of the concepts we discuss should extend to arbitrary spacetime dimension. However, mathematically precise statements about tensor functors are currently limited to 1+1d, i.e., to the setting of fusion categories. As mentioned in the introduction, we will employ the SymTFT reformulation presented in section
III
to extend our ideas to higher dimensions.

II.1
From RG-Interfaces to Tensor Functors

\mathcal{T}_{\text{UV}}
\mathcal{T}_{\text{UV}}+\lambda\int\mathcal{O}
\leadsto
RG
\mathsf{D}
\mathsf{D}
\mathcal{T}_{\text{UV}}
\mathcal{T}_{\text{IR}}
\mathcal{I}_{RG}
\mathsf{D}
F(\mathsf{D})

Figure 3:
The RG-interface with symmetry defects: in addition to figure
1
the topological defects
\mathsf{D}
that generate the symmetry in the UV are shown. After the RG-flow, the defects in the IR theory are the image
F(\mathsf{D})
under a tensor functor
F:\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\to\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
between the UV and IR symmetries.

Let us start by providing a clear, physical argument for the mathematical structures we will encounter throughout this section.
The idea is that a UV-IR map on symmetries

F:\ \mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\to\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}

(II.1)

can be encoded in an
RG-interface

Gaiotto:2012np
.
This can be described by turning on a
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
-symmetric relevant deformation in half space and flowing to its IR fixed point,
where the symmetry acting faithfully can generically be quite different from the one in the UV. For example, a subsector of the UV symmetry generators might act trivially, meaning that all of its corresponding (generalized) charges are screened. On the other end, new symmetries can emerge in the IR, giving rise to further selection rules which are absent in the UV theory. This means that certain IR symmetry charges must be identified under the UV symmetry action.

The complete UV/IR mapping process is far richer, but its key properties may be derived by physical consistency alone.
Indeed, as the process is continuous, topological defects
\mathsf{D}
of
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
remain topological throughout the half space RG-flow. This gives rise to a map
F
between UV and IR symmetry defects, which is compatible with the fusion and the structure of topological junctions of defects – i.e. a tensor functor. This is shown in figure
3
.

A few obvious identifications are in place:

UV symmetries can be screened
in the IR, i.e. the associated defects become trivial after the RG-flow. This means the

F
can have a kernel
,
\ker(F)\not=1
. On the other hand the IR can have
emergent symmetries
, i.e. not all symmetries are images of UV symmetry generators. This means that
F
is not surjective
.

A dual perspective can be gained by discussing the fate of charged operators. In this discussion, we will only consider genuine, possibly extended, operators.
Operators at the IR fixed point always have a description in terms of their UV counterparts
Zamolodchikov:1987ti
and their charge
q
corresponds to a well-defined representation
F^{*}(q)
under the UV symmetry:

\mathcal{I}_{\text{RG}}
q
\leadsto
\mathcal{I}_{\text{RG}}
F^{*}(q)

(II.2)

On the other hand, UV operators can decouple along the RG and can hence be absent from the low energy Hilbert space. Their charges can still exist as long as they are confined on the RG-interface though. This makes clear that, while the map
F
between symmetries goes
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\to\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
, the representations of genuine operators are instead pulled back in the opposite direction. The significance of the dual map
F^{*}
will be apparent once we switch gears to the SymTFT.

The observation that UV symmetry defects
\mathsf{D}
remain topological throughout the RG-flow furthermore allows us to conclude that the RG-interface
\mathcal{I}
induces a map between the fusion structures in the UV and IR, see figure
4
.

\mathsf{D}_{1}
\mathsf{D}_{2}
\mathsf{D}_{3}
\mathsf{D}_{3}
\leadsto
=
\mathsf{D}_{1}
\mathsf{D}_{2}
\mathsf{D}_{3}
\leadsto
\mathsf{D}_{1}
\mathsf{D}_{2}
\mathsf{D}_{3}
F(\mathsf{D}_{3})
=
\mathsf{D}_{1}
\mathsf{D}_{2}
F(\mathsf{D}_{1})
F(\mathsf{D}_{2})
F(\mathsf{D}_{3})
\times J(\mathsf{D}_{1},\mathsf{D}_{2},\mathsf{D}_{3})

Figure 4:
Passing a UV topological junction through the RG-interface
\mathcal{I}
gives rise to a linear map
J(\mathsf{D}_{1},\mathsf{D}_{2},\mathsf{D}_{3})
between the spaces of UV and IR topological junctions. For fusion categories these are vector spaces, and the map
J(\mathsf{D}_{1},\mathsf{D}_{2},\mathsf{D}_{3})
is implemented by matrix multiplication.

It is by now established that the correct mathematical structure that describes symmetries in QFTs is that of the (higher)
tensor categories
.
The fusion of topological defects is modeled by the tensor product, while higher-data associated fusion of the topological junctions are encoded in the (higher) morphisms of the category.
For instance, for 1-categories the associativity condition on point junctions give rise to the
F-symbols
that are analogs of ’t Hooft anomalies. In that context
J(\mathsf{D}_{1},\mathsf{D}_{2},\mathsf{D}_{3})
are unitary matrices implementing a linear map between vector spaces

V_{\mathsf{D}_{1},\mathsf{D}_{2}}^{\mathsf{D}_{3}}=\Hom(\mathsf{D}_{1}\otimes\mathsf{D}_{2},\mathsf{D}_{3})
and
V_{F(\mathsf{D}_{1}),F(D_{2})}^{F(\mathsf{D}_{3})}=\text{Hom}(F(\mathsf{D}_{1})\otimes F(\mathsf{D}_{2}),F(D_{3}))

J(\mathsf{D}_{1},\mathsf{D}_{2},\mathsf{D}_{3}):\qquad V_{\mathsf{D}_{1},\mathsf{D}_{2}}^{\mathsf{D}_{3}}\to V_{F(\mathsf{D}_{1}),F(D_{2})}^{F(\mathsf{D}_{3})}\,,

(II.3)

that satisfies certain compatibility conditions (see equation (
A.6
)). Different
J
s give rise to IR symmetries with the same fusion algebras, but with different F-symbols
\mathcal{F}
:

\displaystyle{{\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\text{IR}}}^{F(\mathsf{D}_{1}),F(\mathsf{D}_{2}),F(\mathsf{D}_{3})}_{\ F(\mathsf{D}_{4})}}_{F(\mathsf{D}_{5}),F(\mathsf{D}_{6})}

(II.4)

\displaystyle={{\mathcal{F}_{\text{UV}}}^{\mathsf{D}_{1},\mathsf{D}_{2},\mathsf{D}_{3}}_{\ \mathsf{D}_{4}}}_{\mathsf{D}_{5},\mathsf{D}_{6}}\,\left[J(\mathsf{D}_{1},\mathsf{D}_{2},\mathsf{D}_{5})\right.

\displaystyle\left.J(\mathsf{D}_{3},\mathsf{D}_{5},\mathsf{D}_{4})J^{-1}(\mathsf{D}_{1},\mathsf{D}_{6},\mathsf{D}_{4})J^{-1}(\mathsf{D}_{2},\mathsf{D}_{3},\mathsf{D}_{6})\right]\,,

where
\mathsf{D}_{1}^{\prime}=F(D_{1})
etc.
Physically, this often coincides with the emergence of a ’t Hooft anomaly for the IR symmetry.
On the other hand, from the point of view of the UV symmetry, the linear maps
J(\mathsf{D}_{1},\mathsf{D}_{2},\mathsf{D}_{3})
correspond to a gauge transformation. Thus, the UV ’t Hooft anomalies are preserved by the tensor functor, and the IR
F
-symbols can always be pulled back into consistent UV
F
-symbols by pre-composing then with
F
. This is the essence of the ’t Hooft anomaly-matching condition.

The above properties of
\mathcal{I}
(together with the appropriate extension to higher-codimensional junctions for higher categories) define the mathematical structure behind a
tensor functor

EGNO
. Mathematically these are the natural maps between tensor categories that preserve their structure. Physically, this represents the intuitive idea that any topological operation (fusing, braiding) on UV symmetry defects must be mapped into an analogous operation in the IR. Tensor functors represent the most basic such map, that is compatible with the fusion.

Clearly, not every interface
\mathcal{I}
between
\mathcal{T}_{\text{UV}}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\text{IR}}
provides us with a tensor functor. As we have noticed, these interfaces must be
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
-symmetric. Symmetric interfaces and boundary conditions, and how they are described, have been studied in several recent works
Bhardwaj:2024igy
;
Copetti:2024onh
;
Antinucci:2024izg
;
Antinucci:2025uvj
;
Choi:2023xjw
;
Choi:2024tri
. In such a formulation, symmetry breaking on an interface can be understood in terms of topological order parameters, much in the same way as the SSB breaking of a global symmetry is encoded in the IR through topological bulk order parameters
Bhardwaj:2023fca
;
Bhardwaj:2023idu
. With this intuition in mind, a tensor functor is implemented by an interface which hosts no nontrivial topological charges under
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
on its worldvolume.

II.2
Tensor Functors

Having motivated the relevance of tensor functors from a physics point of view, we will now turn to a more thorough mathematical investigation of their properties, and their interpretations in the context of generalized symmetries.
Mathematically, this is standard material in the theory of fusion categories. We invite the interested reader to consult either the textbook
EGNO
, or the growing set of physics-oriented lecture notes
Schafer-Nameki:2023jdn
;
Shao:2023gho
for an introduction to categorical symmetries.

The present section is entirely formulated in terms of symmetry categories and tensor functors between them. A physically more intuitive perspective on the same concepts will be presented in sections
III
and
IV
in terms of a SymTFT realization. This will aid in developing physical intuition, but more importantly, will provide a natural extension from fusion categories to fusion higher-categories.

A
tensor functor
between two tensor categories

F:\ \mathcal{C}_{1}\to\mathcal{C}_{2}

(II.5)

is a functor, i.e. a map on objects and morphisms, satisfying certain conditions (the full mathematical definition is given in appendix
A.1
).
The most important property is that it is compatible with the tensor structure, i.e. the fusion product: there is an isomorphism

J_{\mathsf{D}_{1},\mathsf{D}_{2}}:\quad F(\mathsf{D}_{1})\otimes F(\mathsf{D}_{2})\cong F(\mathsf{D}_{1}\otimes\mathsf{D}_{2})

(II.6)

for all
\mathsf{D}_{1},\mathsf{D}_{2}\in\mathcal{C}_{1}
, satisfying compatibility condition (
A.6
).
Intuitively this is a map on symmetry generators, that is compatible with the fusion product.
A tensor functor can have various additional properties, which all will have a physical relevance in the following.

Injective Functors.
A
tensor functor

I:\ \mathcal{C}_{1}\to\mathcal{C}_{2}
is injective if

\mathsf{D}_{1}\not\cong\mathsf{D}_{2}\Rightarrow I(\mathsf{D}_{1})\not\cong I(\mathsf{D}_{2})\,.

(II.7)

If furthermore,
I
is faithful, i.e., injective on morphisms, then it is called an
embedding
.
This means the symmetry
\mathcal{C}_{1}
can be embedded into the symmetry
\mathcal{C}_{2}
.
For example, if we think of
\mathcal{C}_{1}=\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}=\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
, then an embedding functor that is not an isomorphism or an equivalence of categories, indicates emergent symmetries in the IR.

Surjective Functors.
On the other hand we can have surjective functors: a tensor functor

P:\ \mathcal{C}_{1}\to\mathcal{C}_{2}

(II.8)

is surjective if any object
\mathsf{D}_{2}
in
\mathcal{C}_{2}
appears in the image, i.e.
\mathsf{D}_{2}\subset P(\mathsf{D}_{1})
for some
\mathsf{D}_{1}\in\mathcal{C}_{1}
.
The notation
\mathsf{D}_{2}\subset P(\mathsf{D}_{1})
means that
\mathsf{D}_{2}
can be written in terms of the simple objects of
P(\mathsf{D}_{1})
.
Surjective tensor functors describe a situation in which the UV symmetry may act non-faithfully in the IR.

Normal Functors.
Another concept often appearing in the literature is that of a normal functor.
A functor

F:\mathcal{C}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{2}

(II.9)

is said to be normal if for every object
\mathsf{D}_{1}
of
\mathcal{C}_{1}
, there is a sub-object
\mathsf{D}_{1}^{\prime}\subset\mathsf{D}_{1}
such that
F(\mathsf{D}_{1}^{\prime})
is the largest trivial sub-object of
F(\mathsf{D}_{1})
, i.e.

F(\mathsf{D}_{1})=n\,1\oplus\cdots\,,\quad F(\mathsf{D}_{1}^{\prime})=n\,1\,,

(II.10)

for some
n
, where
1
is the identity object (i.e. the identity symmetry generator) and
\cdots
does not contain further copies of
1
. If
\mathsf{D}_{1}
is simple, either
F(\mathsf{D}_{1})=n\,1
or
1\not\subset F(\mathsf{D}_{1})
.
For normal functors between fusion categories, injectivity on objects is equivalent to having trivial kernels, thus such functors naturally appear in defining the analogue of a short exact sequence of groups for a fusion category
bruguieres2011exact
.
In our applications we will not find any relevant non-normal functors. Thus we will safely assume that all functors are normal throughout the paper.
More details on normal functors and examples of non-normal functors can be found in appendix
A.4
.

Examples.

A simple class of examples of tensor functors arise from group homomorphisms
G\to H
which lift to tensor functors of
\mathsf{Vec}_{G}\to\mathsf{Vec}_{H}
.
1
1

1

When dealing with discrete groups, we will use
G
to indicate the group, while
\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega}
stands for the fusion category based on the group
G
, with ’t Hooft anomaly
\omega\in H^{3}(G,U(1))
. This will be important as group homomorphisms will not always extend to interesting tensor functors on the category
\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega}
.
This becomes more interesting in the presence of anomalies.
For example, the surjective functor

F:\quad\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{4}}\to\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}^{\omega=1}\,,

(II.11)

maps a
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
symmetry to a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
symmetry with nontrivial anomaly
\omega\in H^{3}({\mathbb{Z}}_{2},U(1))\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
. In this case the map on the objects is simply the projection
p:\mathbb{Z}_{4}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
taking
\mod 2
, however, the action on junctions is nontrivial:
J(2,1,3)=-J(1,2,3)=i
.
Notice that the anomaly also maps correctly, as
p^{*}(\omega)=0
, as required. This functor has been used extensively in the description of intrinsically gapless SPTs in the condensed matter literature
Verresen:2019igf
;
Thorngren:2020wet
;
Wen:2022tkg
;
Bhardwaj:2024qrf
.
On the other hand the functor
\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{4}}^{\omega\not=0}\to\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}^{\omega=1}
is not a tensor functor, as it does not correctly pull-back the anomaly.

More generally, for any group homomorphism
\varphi:G\rightarrow H
, and anomalies
[\alpha]\in H^{3}(G,U(1))

[\omega]\in H^{3}(H,U(1))
, there is a tensor functor

\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\alpha}\rightarrow\mathsf{Vec}_{H}^{\omega}\,,

(II.12)

as long as
[\varphi^{*}\omega]=[\alpha]
in
H^{3}(G,U(1))
.

On the other hand, for any normal subgroup
N\triangleleft G
of a finite group
G
, there is also a normal tensor functor
bruguieres2011exact

\mathsf{Rep}(G)\rightarrow\mathsf{Rep}(N)\,,

(II.13)

given by restriction.

II.3
Anomalous Simple Categories (ASCies)

Tensor functors provide a key step toward extending familiar concepts – such as ’t Hooft anomalies – from symmetry groups to fusion category symmetries. We will show that a special class of surjective tensor functors, which give rise to
short exact sequences
of tensor categories, can significantly deepen our understanding of ’t Hooft anomalies in this broader setting. Intuitively, such short exact sequences describe how anomaly-free (normal) subcategories can be consistently “forgotten” along an RG-flow. A short exact sequence associated with the maximal such subcategory defines an
Anomalous Simple Category
(ASCy), which provides a distilled representation of the original ’t Hooft anomaly.

To properly define ASCies, we will need to make a brief detour. Consider a surjective (normal) functor
P
and denote by
\ker(P)
its kernel. This is a fusion category
\mathcal{N}
, generated by objects
\mathsf{D}
such that
F(\mathsf{D})=n\,1
.
This allows us to define a
short exact sequence
of tensor categories
bruguieres2011exact
:

\mathcal{N}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle I}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{C}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{S}\,,

(II.14)

where
I
is injective and
P
is surjective (and normal), and the exactness meaning

\text{im}(I)=\ker(P)\,.

(II.15)

Furthermore

P\circ I=f

(II.16)

where
f
is a fiber functor, i.e. a functor to the trivial category
\mathsf{Vec}

f:\quad\mathcal{N}\to\mathsf{Vec}\,.

(II.17)

Whenever such an exact sequence exists, we call
\mathcal{N}
a
normal subcategory
of
\mathcal{C}
. Notice that for fusion categories, the dimensions of categories involved in the short exact sequence must satisfy
bruguieres2011exact
:

\text{dim}(\mathcal{C})=\text{dim}(\mathcal{N})\,\text{dim}(\mathcal{S})\,,

(II.18)

and
\text{dim}(\mathcal{N})
must be an integer.

The physical interpretation is the following: any normal subcategory
\mathcal{N}
is an
anomaly free sub-category
, as it has a fiber functor by (
II.16
), and moreover it can be forgotten consistently in the IR, leaving behind a self-consistent symmetry structure
\mathcal{S}
.
We emphasize that not all anomaly free subcategories are normal, as we will see in examples. We will amply discuss the physical relevance of this.
Note that
not
every normal subgroup
N\triangleleft G
gives rise to a normal subcategory
\mathsf{Vec}_{N}
of
\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega}
even if
\omega|_{N}=0
.
An example of this is

\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}\to\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{4}}^{\omega=2}\,,

(II.19)

where
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\triangleleft\mathbb{Z}_{4}
is non-anomalous but there is no surjective tensor functor from
\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{4}}^{\omega=2}
to any category whose kernel is
\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}
.

If
\mathcal{N}
is the
maximal normal subcategory
of
\mathcal{C}
, the image of
P
,
\mathcal{S}
, is the simplest fusion category satisfying the ’t Hooft anomaly matching conditions of
\mathcal{C}
and it captures the anomaly of the original symmetry
\mathcal{C}
.
We will discuss this in more detail in section
IV
. Here maximal means: there does not exist
\mathcal{M}
, another normal subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
, with
\mathcal{N}\subset\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.

We define a category that does not have a non-trivial normal subcategory to be an
anomalous simple category (ASCy)
. Any category
\mathcal{S}
that fits into an exact sequence (
II.14
) with
\mathcal{N}
maximal is an ASCy, and captures aspects of the anomaly of
\mathcal{C}
.
Notably, a given category
\mathcal{C}
may admit multiple exact sequences of the form (
II.14
) with the same normal subcategories
\mathcal{N}_{i}
, but different ACSies
\mathcal{S}_{i}
.

Note that maximality is crucial. E.g. relaxing maximality we have the exact sequence of categories

\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}\to\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{4}}\to\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}^{\omega=1}\,,

(II.20)

where
\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}
is normal but not maximal. Indeed, in this case
\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{4}}
is itself its maximal normal subcategory.

As we have already mentioned, the pullback operation implements ’t Hooft anomaly matching at the level of fusion categories. In the case of ASCies, the pullback
P^{*}
can be used to describe the UV anomaly in terms of the anomalies of the ASCies
\mathcal{S}_{i}
. Furthermore, as the pullback operation also naturally acts on symmetric gapped phases, described as module categories
\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathcal{C}}
over the UV symmetry:

P^{*}_{i}:\quad\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathcal{S}_{i}}\longrightarrow\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathcal{C}}\,.

(II.21)

ASCies describe the minimal ways in which the UV symmetry
must
be broken due to its ’t Hooft anomaly. We will see detailed realizations of this mechanism in section
IV
.

Some low rank ASCies.

Some examples of low rank ASCies are:

•

\mathsf{Vec}
,

•

\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}^{\omega=1}
,

•

the Fibonacci category
\mathsf{Fib}
,

•

the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
Tambara-Yamagami categories
\mathsf{TY}({\mathbb{Z}}_{2},\pm)=\mathsf{Ising}_{\pm}
,

•

Haagerup category
\mathcal{H}_{3}
.

Notice that in several of these cases, while an invertible, anomaly-free subcategory exists – for example
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
in
\mathsf{Ising}
or
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
in
\mathcal{H}_{3}
– it is in fact not normal.

III
Tensor Functors are SymTFT Interfaces

We have seen that (normal) tensor functors have an important physical relevance.
However a comprehensive characterization can be challenging. This is when the SymTFT comes to the rescue. We can map the problem of finding tensor functors, normal categories, and ASCies in terms of the SymTFT, where they become compatibility conditions on certain boundary conditions and interfaces, which are relatively straightforward to check and can be systematically explored.

In this section, after a brief review of the necessary SymTFT concepts, we expand RG-interfaces into a one-dimensional higher system with topological bulk (RG-sandwiches) and then isolate the kinematical part, that is encoded in the so-called SymTFT club quiche
Bhardwaj:2023bbf
. This is comprised of two SymTFTs, separated by a topological interface and a gapped symmetry boundary for each. The condition on the existence of a tensor functor is recast as a “Matching Equation”, between the interface and the symmetry boundaries. This equivalence provides a dictionary between properties of tensor functors and of SymTFT-quiches.

The use of SymTFTs is not just a change of language—it provides a framework that readily extends to higher dimensions, where a direct categorical approach via tensor
(d{-}1)
-functors is still lacking.
The SymTFT approach is therefore a vital tool to quantify anomalies in higher-dimensional theories.

III.1
SymTFT Sandwiches and Quiches

In this section we will give a very brief summary of the SymTFT.
The basic premise is to separate the kinematical symmetry aspects, from dynamics. This is done by extending a
d
-dimensional theory with symmetry
\mathcal{C}
to
d+1
spacetime dimensions. The theory in
(d+1)
-dimensions is topological, and is obtained as a flat gauging of
\mathcal{C}
. For abelian group-like symmetries the resulting theory is simply a Dijkgraaf-Witten theory for the abelian symmetry (with twist if the group was anomalous), which for any fusion category symmetries becomes the Turaev-Viro TQFT.
More generally, we will refer to it as the SymTFT and denote it by
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C})
.

The most salient features of the SymTFT are as follows:
A theory
\mathcal{T}
with symmetry category
\mathcal{C}
is equivalent to the interval compactification of
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C})
with

•

Symmetry boundary:
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}
that is gapped and on which the symmetry category is realized.

•

Physical boundary:
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{phys}}
, which contains all the dynamics.

This is usually depicted in terms of the SymTFT “Sandwich”:

(III.1)

Every gapped boundary condition of
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C})
can act as a symmetry boundary
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}
. Different choices of
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}
give rise to symmetry categories that are related by generalized gauging, i.e. stacking with symmetric TQFTs before gauging. Two symmetries that are related by such a generalized gauging on the other hand have the same SymTFT and the same center. For any fixed
\mathcal{C}
,
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C})
has a canonical Dirichlet gapped boundary condition that realizes the symmetry
\mathcal{C}
.

For our considerations, the genuine topological defects of the SymTFT play a central role. Mathematically they form the Drinfeld center:

\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C})=\text{genuine topological defects of}\ \mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C})\,.

(III.2)

Its topological defects of (spacetime) dimension
p
will be denoted by
\bm{Q}_{p}
. Those that end on both symmetry and physical boundaries, as shown in (
III.1
) correspond to (generalized) charges and give rise to operators
\mathcal{O}_{p-1}
in the theory
\mathcal{T}
.

Gapped boundary conditions have a characterization in terms of Lagrangian algebras of the Drinfeld center
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C})
. For 1+1d, i.e. fusion category symmetries, these have a simple description in terms of non-negative linear combinations of anyons

\mathcal{L}=\bigoplus_{i}n_{i}a_{i}\,,

(III.3)

summing over simple anyons
a_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C})
,
satisfying a set of compatibility conditions

davydov2013witt
;
Kong:2013aya
;
EGNO
;
cong2017hamiltonian
.
2
2

2

The algebra structure is important as well, and can be different or the same set of anyons. The subsequent analysis can easily be extended to incorporate this situation.

Notably, a Lagrangian algebra has maximal dimension, i.e.

\text{dim}(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}))=\text{dim}(\mathcal{L})\,.

(III.4)

Here
d_{a}
are the quantum dimensions of the anyons,
\text{dim}(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}))=\sqrt{\sum d_{a_{i}}^{2}}
and
\text{dim}(\mathcal{L}):=\sum_{i}n_{i}d_{a_{i}}
.
Lagrangian algebras classify the interfaces from the
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C})
topological order to the trivial topological order.

More generally one can define condensable algebras
\mathcal{A}
, by relaxing the maximality condition. A condensable algebra means the anyons in that algebra are condensed, those that braid non-trivially with the anyons in the algebra confine, and the remaining ones pass through to a reduced topological order, denoted by
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C})/\mathcal{A}
. Again we can write
\mathcal{A}=\oplus_{i}n_{i}a_{i}
. The dimension of the reduced topological order is then

\text{dim}(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C})/\mathcal{A})=\text{dim}(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}))/\text{dim}(\mathcal{A})\,.

(III.5)

The resulting SymTFT takes the form of a
club-sandwich
can be depicted as in figure
5
. We will often project this to a 2d picture from the top.

\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{1}
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{2}
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{phys}}_{1}
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{phys}}_{2}
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C})
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C})/\mathcal{A}
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}}

Figure 5:
The club sandwich: the 3d figure shows the interface
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}}
between the two topological orders
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C})
and
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C})/\mathcal{A}
that are related by condensing an algebra
\mathcal{A}
in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C})
. The gapped symmetry boundary conditions
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{i}
are shown in the front, the physical boundary conditions
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{phys}}_{i}
in the back. The projections will be denoted as in figure
2
.

Note this is a slight modification of the standard SymTFT club sandwich in
Bhardwaj:2023bbf
, used to study gapless phases, in that as for the SymTFT we rotated the diagram by 90 degrees but also we introduced interfaces that separate two choices of gapped boundary conditions
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{2}
, as well as two physical boundary conditions. The mathematics of the club sandwich is the same, but the physical application is rather distinct.

The interface
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}}
between the two topological orders is entirely fixed in terms of the condensable algebra. An equivalent, very useful way to think about this is in terms of a gapped boundary condition of the folded theory

\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}}}\subset\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C})\boxtimes\overline{\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C})/\mathcal{A}}\,.

(III.6)

We will use both formulations interchangeably in the following.

Much of the information we require can be obtained from the SymTFT without specifying necessarily a physical boundary
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{phys}}
.
Such a SymTFT sandwich without a
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{phys}}
is a
quiche
or for the club-sandwich, a club quiche.

In 1+1d for fusion categories, this can be systematically and comprehensively explored.
For higher dimensions, a similarly comprehensive analysis exists in 2+1d for gapped boundary conditions have recently been classified in
Bhardwaj:2024qiv
;
Wen:2024qsg
;
Bhardwaj:2025piv
and non-maximal condensable algebras in
Bhardwaj:2025jtf
;
Wen:2025thg
. In higher dimensions, there is more circumstantial results for condensable algebras, though many results are known from e.g. (generalized) gauging.

Generalized Charges and the Dual Tensor Functor.

Before introducing the SymTFT construction of UV/IR maps, we can already use the mathematical properties of the Drinfeld center to gain further insight on its structure. Recall that the Drinfeld center
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C})
can be presented as pairs
(\mathsf{D},b^{\alpha}_{\mathsf{D}})
, where
\mathsf{D}\in\mathcal{C}
while
b^{\alpha}_{\mathsf{D}}
is the collection of
half-braiding
(
\alpha
labels the various ones): for any other object
\mathsf{D}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
there is an isomorphisms

b_{\mathsf{D},\mathsf{D}^{\prime}}^{\alpha}:\quad\mathsf{D}\otimes\mathsf{D}^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathsf{D}^{\prime}\otimes\mathsf{D}

(III.7)

satisfying certain compatibility conditions (see
(
EGNO
, Definition 7.13.1)
). The projection map

\pi:\quad\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}

(III.8)

is a tensor functor that forgets the half-braiding, namely
\pi(\mathsf{D},b_{\mathsf{D}}^{\alpha})=\mathsf{D}
. The charges
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{C}}
are the simple objects in the kernel of
\pi
:

\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{C}}=\ker(\pi)\subset\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C})\ .

(III.9)

Simple objects in
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{C}}
are of the form
(n\,1,b_{1}^{\alpha})\equiv(n\,1,b^{\alpha})
, and are the simple objects in the Lagrangian algebra
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{C}}
. Mathematically a charge
q=(n\,1,b^{\alpha})
can be evaluated on symmetry generators
\mathsf{D}\in\mathcal{C}
:
q(\mathsf{D})=b^{\alpha}_{n\,1,\mathsf{D}}
and encodes the quantum numbers of the objects charged under the symmetry.

Any tensor functor
F:\mathcal{C}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{2}
, whose tensor structure is compatible with the charge (i.e., satisfying equation (
A.7
)) gives a dual map of charges
F^{*}:\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{C}_{2}}\rightarrow\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{C}_{1}}
, defined by precomposition
3
3

3

Under identifications of vector spaces
\Hom_{\mathcal{C}_{1}}(n\,1_{\mathcal{C}_{1}}\otimes\mathsf{D}_{1},X_{1}\otimes n\,1_{\mathcal{C}_{1}})\cong\Hom_{\mathcal{C}_{2}}(n\,1_{\mathcal{C}_{2}}\otimes F(X_{1}),F(X_{1})\otimes n\,1_{\mathcal{C}_{2}}).

F^{*}(q_{2})(\mathsf{D}_{1})=q_{2}(F(\mathsf{D}_{1}))\ .

(III.10)

The physical meaning of this map is clear: while the tensor functor
F:\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
says that the UV symmetry still acts in the IR,
F^{*}
explains how the operators and states of the IR, naturally classified in representations of
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
, transform under the UV symmetry.

III.2
RG-Quiches

The problem of determining the existence and properties of a tensor functor
F
between two given categories
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
can be dramatically simplified using the SymTFT. A first natural guess would be to expand the RG-interface figure
1
into a
(d+1)
-dimensional system: see (
III.1
).

The
UV
and
IR
SymTFTs are now separated by a topological interface
\mathcal{I}_{F}
that terminates on the
d
-dimensional RG-interface. The interface
\mathcal{I}_{F}
intersects along
\mathfrak{I}_{F}
with the symmetry boundary, and separates the two boundary conditions
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{UV}}
and
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{IR}}
.
We assume that all defects can end on the physical boundary condition. Subject to this, all symmetry properties are independent of the physical boundaries, and are completely captured by the
RG-quiche
:

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{split}

(III.11)

The SymTFTs with the symmetry boundary, but no physical boundary is the quiche, which is what will be key to our analysis.

Notice that a generic interface will not in general describe a tensor functor. The reason is simple: while tensor functors are maps with a precise direction – which follows the physical RG-flow – a topological interface
\mathcal{I}
in the SymTFT can be equally well interpreted as an interface between
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})
and
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}})
or the other way around. Furthermore, notice that, while two symmetries may not be connected via any tensor functor, their centers always have interfaces between them, which are described by factorized products of gapped boundary conditions on the two sides. This tension resonates with a simple observation we have made earlier: the RG-interface should not break
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
.

The SymTFT Matching Equation.
These two observations come together in a simple equation relating the topological interface
\mathcal{I}_{F}
between
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})
and
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}})
to the existence of a tensor functor

F:\ \mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\to\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}\,.

(III.12)

We will dub this the
SymTFT Matching Equation (ME)
. It takes the form:

\text{(ME)}:\quad\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{UV}}\times\mathcal{I}_{F}=\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{IR}}\,.

(III.13)

This simple looking equation is highly constraining, and at the same time straightforward to check in concrete examples.
For fusion categories,
(
III.13
) can be recast as a simple matrix equation, which makes it computationally straightforward to handle. Consider first the interface
\mathcal{I}_{F}
alone (without gapped boundary conditions). This is equivalent, by folding along the interface to the topological order
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})\boxtimes\overline{\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}})}
, with a gapped boundary condition:

{}{}
{}{}
{}{}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{220.75642pt}{61.93852pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{split}

(III.14)

The boundary condition
\mathfrak{B}_{F}
is defined by a folded Lagrangian algebra

\mathcal{L}_{F}\subset\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})\boxtimes\overline{\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}})}\,.

(III.15)

As the 2+1d bulk SymTFTs is a theory of anyons, we can
expand
\mathcal{L}_{F}
in terms of the simple anyons
a_{\text{UV}}^{i}
and
b_{\text{IR}}^{k}
of the two factors:

\mathcal{L}_{F}=\bigoplus_{i,k}n_{i,k}\,a_{\text{UV}}^{i}\overline{b^{k}_{\text{IR}}}\,,\quad n_{i,k}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\,.

(III.16)

For instance, if
\mathcal{I}_{F}
is obtained by condensing an algebra
\mathcal{A}_{F}\subset\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})
, then

\mathcal{L}_{F}=\mathcal{A}_{F}\otimes 1\oplus\cdots\,.

(III.17)

However general RG-interfaces may not be given by condensation of algebras.

The folded Lagrangian defines a map

\phi_{F}:\ \mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})\to\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}})\,,

(III.18)

given by

\phi_{F}:\quad a^{i}_{\text{UV}}\mapsto\bigoplus_{k}\,n_{i,k}\,b^{k}_{\text{IR}}\,.

(III.19)

The anyons that do not appear in
\mathcal{L}_{F}
are confined by the interface, and are mapped to the zero object. This map implements the action of passing an anyon across the interface
\mathcal{I}_{F}
.
Denoting by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{UV}}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{i}\,v_{i}\,a_{\text{UV}}^{i}\subset\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})

(III.20)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{IR}}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{k}\,w_{k}\,b_{\text{IR}}^{k}\subset\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}})

the Lagrangian algebras corresponding to the
UV
and
IR
symmetry boundaries, respectively, the Matching Equation (
III.13
) becomes simply

\text{(ME)}:\quad\phi_{F}(\mathcal{L}_{\text{UV}})=\mathcal{L}_{\text{IR}}\,,

(III.21)

which, at the level of objects, becomes the matrix relation:

\text{(ME)}:\quad\sum_{i}n_{i,k}v_{i}=w_{k}\,.

(III.22)

The equivalence between the Matching Equation (
III.13
) and the existence of a tensor functor
F:\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
is proven in appendix
A.2
for fusion 1-categories and fusion 2-categories (i.e., finite symmetries in two dimensions and in three dimensions). In a broader context (e.g. higher dimensions and/or continuous symmetries), already giving a precise definition of a tensor functor is extremely complicated, and while our proof can probably be extended in a rigorous way once all the notions will be rigorously defined, we do not pursue this direction and instead we take (
III.13
) as a definition.

Let us comment now on higher dimensions. Gapped boundary conditions for
d+1
dimensional TOs are well defined concepts. A topological interface
\mathcal{I}_{F}
defines a Lagrangian algebra of the folded TO. This should then also provide a map between the objects and (higher) morphisms of the centers
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})
and
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}})
. For 2+1d theories with fusion 2-categories such interfaces were in fact classified in
Bhardwaj:2024qiv
;
Wen:2025thg
;
Bhardwaj:2025piv
;
Bhardwaj:2025jtf
. We will use this perspective to define a tensor functor for higher categories, using (
III.21
) in appendix
A.6
.

\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{UV}}
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{IR}}
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}})
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}}
\pi_{\text{UV}}
\pi_{\text{IR}}
\mathsf{D}
F(\mathsf{D})
a^{\text{UV}}
b^{\text{IR}}
\mathfrak{I}_{F}

Figure 6:
Extracting a tensor functor
F
from the folded Lagrangian algebra
\mathcal{L}_{F}
: unfolding it, results in lines
(a^{\text{UV}},b^{\text{IR}})
inside the
\mathcal{L}_{F}
algebra, which can be projected onto the boundary. If (
III.13
) is satisfied, these will give rise to a pair
(\mathsf{D},F(\mathsf{D}))
related by the functor
F
.

Tensor Functor Dictionary and Dual Map.
We will now explain how the matching equation (
III.13
) implements the salient physical properties of RG-interfaces/tensor functors: the map between symmetry generators and the dual map on generalized charges.

First, let us spell out the dictionary between the bulk data, encoded in the algebra
\mathcal{L}_{F}
and the structure of the functor
F
. The interface algebra is a pair
(\mathcal{L}_{F},m_{F})
of an object in the folded theory
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})\boxtimes\overline{\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}})}
and a multiplication map
m_{F}:\mathcal{L}_{F}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{F}\to\mathcal{L}_{F}
satisfying some well known properties
davydov2013witt
;
Kong:2013aya
;
EGNO
;
cong2017hamiltonian
. The boundary projection:

\pi(\mathcal{L}_{F})\equiv\left(\pi_{\text{UV}}\boxtimes\overline{\pi}_{\text{IR}}\right)(\mathcal{L}_{F})\,,

(III.23)

defines an object in
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\boxtimes\overline{\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}}
. It decomposes into simple objects as:

\pi(\mathcal{L}_{F})=\bigoplus_{\mathsf{D}\in\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}}(\mathsf{D},\,\overline{F(\mathsf{D})})\,,

(III.24)

where
F(\mathsf{D})
will define the tensor functor. Pictorially this is shown in figure
6
.
The object
\pi(\mathcal{L}_{F})
has the structure of a Frobenius algebra
Fuchs:2002cm
, and
\mathfrak{I}_{F}
is a module over such an algebra. This means that there is a notion of product
J_{F}
on the objects
(\mathsf{D},\overline{F(\mathsf{D})})
composing
\pi(\mathcal{L}_{F})
, which will eventually implement the isomorphism (
II.6
). The module structure implies that
pairs
(\mathsf{D},\overline{F(\mathsf{D})})
of boundary topological defects are allowed to end topologically on the interface
\mathfrak{I}_{F}
, in a way that is consistent with the product
J_{F}
, see e.g.
Choi:2023xjw
.

Thus, the algebra structure will precisely give rise to the tensor functor
F
.
To justify this claim consider a bulk anyon
(a^{\text{UV}},b^{\text{IR}})\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})\boxtimes\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}})
belonging to the algebra
\mathcal{L}_{F}
which remains nontrivial under
\pi
. This is mapped to a pair
(\mathsf{D}_{\text{UV}},\mathsf{D}_{\text{IR}})
of boundary lines joining at
\mathfrak{I}_{F}
. We now bend the bulk interface
\mathcal{I}
onto
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{UV}}
. From the Matching Equation, the topological line
\mathsf{D}_{\text{UV}}
is mapped into a topological line
F(\mathsf{D}_{\text{UV}})
– this is a map between the gapped boundary conditions:

(III.25)

we conclude that
\mathsf{D}_{\text{IR}}
are the simple objects belonging to
F(\mathsf{D}_{\text{UV}})
.

Furthermore, all of the simple objects
\mathsf{D}_{\text{UV}}
must appear in
\pi(\mathcal{L}_{F})
as otherwise we would not have a consistent map between the UV and IR topological boundary conditions, as implied by the matching equation (
III.13
).
As the maps
\pi_{\text{UV}}
and
\pi_{\text{IR}}
are themselves tensor functors, the multiplication
m_{F}
on
\mathcal{L}_{F}
gives rise to a multiplication map
J_{F}
on
\pi(\mathcal{L}_{F})
. This is part of the general correspondence between Frobenius algebras in
\mathcal{C}
and Lagrangian algebras in the center
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C})

Fuchs:2002cm
;
davydov2013witt

(III.26)

This defines a map

\displaystyle J_{F}:

\displaystyle(\mathsf{D},\,F(\mathsf{D}))\otimes(\mathsf{D}^{\prime},\,F(\mathsf{D}^{\prime}))

(III.27)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow(\mathsf{D}\otimes\mathsf{D}^{\prime},F(\mathsf{D})\otimes F(\mathsf{D}^{\prime}))\,.

Upon unfolding the picture,
J_{F}
provides the map in figure
4
on topological junctions, see figure
7
.

Figure 7:
The map on topological junctions, shown in terms of the black lines, as they pass through the interface
\mathcal{I}_{F}
. The map is defined by
J_{F}
in (
III.27
). To be precise, after unfolding we still need to compose the picture from the right with a 3-valent junction in
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
.

\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{UV}}
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{IR}}
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}})
\mathcal{I}_{F}
\mathsf{D}
q
=
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{UV}}
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{IR}}
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}})
\mathcal{I}_{F}
\mathsf{D}
q
\times\,q(\mathsf{D})

Figure 8:
If the matching equation (
III.13
) is not satisfied, some UV charges
q
give rise to topological edge modes on the interface
\mathfrak{I}_{F}
. The half-brading ensures that the UV symmetry
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
then is broken by the interface.

The matching equation (
III.13
), furthermore, implements correctly the dual map
F^{*}
on charges. Consider a charge
q^{\text{IR}}
in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}})
. This is an anyon
b^{\text{IR}}
inside the Lagrangian algebra
\mathcal{L}_{\text{IR}}
. Then
F^{*}(b^{\text{IR}})
must now be a – possibly non-simple – UV generalized charge, as a consequence of (
III.22
).

The dictionary we have just proposed provides a convenient algorithm to extract the data of the tensor functor
F
from those of a SymTFT interface
\mathcal{I}_{F}
satisfying the matching equation. We will make use of it in several instances throughout the rest of the paper.

Symmetric Interfaces from SymTFT.
Let us now come to the interpretation of the tensor functor as a
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
-symmetric interface. Notice that the product on the LHS of (
III.13
) defines a map between gapped boundary conditions of
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})
and
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}})
. In general, the resulting gapped BC is not indecomposable, as noticed for example in
Bhardwaj:2023bbf
. This translates into the existence of nontrivial charges
q\,\in\,\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})
which can terminate on both
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{UV}}
and
\mathcal{I}_{F}
. According to
Bhardwaj:2024igy
these describe topological order parameters for the interface
\mathfrak{I}_{F}
, which are half-braidings on
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
, see figure
8
.

Interfaces for which
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{UV}}\times\mathcal{I}
is not indecomposable thus break the
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
symmetry and cannot define a tensor functor between
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
.

This is elegantly avoided in the case of a tensor functor through the dual map on charges. Consider an IR charge
b^{\text{IR}}
and its image
F^{*}(b^{\text{IR}})
. Simple objects in
F^{*}(b^{\text{IR}})\otimes b^{\text{IR}}
define the topological edge modes on the interface
\mathfrak{I}_{F}
.
However, these edge modes are neutral with respect to the
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
symmetry, since both
F^{*}(b^{\text{IR}})
and
b^{\text{IR}}
are contained within the same interface Lagrangian algebra
\mathcal{L}_{F}
, indicating that the
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
symmetry remains unbroken in the presence of the interface.

III.3
From Tensor Functors to RG-Interfaces

The physical relevance of tensor functors becomes apparent when considering specific properties, such as injectivity, surjectivity and normal functors.
We will identify these properties in the SymTFT RG-Interface picture, using the definition (
III.21
).
We establish a dictionary between basic properties of tensor functors that were discussed in section
II.2
and their counterparts in terms of interfaces. We furthermore provide the physical interpretation, which will be put to use in the next sections.

III.3.1
Injective Functors

We defined an injective functor
F
(or embedding) in (
II.7
). Recall that this captures the case when the IR symmetry
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
is larger than the UV symmetry
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
, allowing for
emergent symmetries
, while the UV symmetry still acts faithfully in the IR.
Dually,

F^{*}:\ \mathcal{Q}_{\text{IR}}\rightarrow\mathcal{Q}_{\text{UV}}

(III.28)

is surjective, with kernel given by the set of IR charges that are neutral under the UV symmetry.

An injective functor
I
is realized by a
condensation interface

\mathcal{I}_{I}
, specified by a condensable algebra

\mathcal{A}_{I}\subset\mathcal{L}_{\text{IR}}\subset\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}})\,,

(III.29)

where
\mathcal{L}_{\text{IR}}
denotes the symmetry boundary. Condensing the anyons in
\mathcal{A}_{I}
within
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}})
yields the new theory
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})=\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}})/\mathcal{A}_{F}
. We refer to a condensable algebra
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{L}_{\text{IR}}
as an
electric algebra
(with respect to
\mathcal{L}_{\text{IR}})
.

This algebra has a simple physical interpretation:

I:\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
is an embedding if and only if the dual functor on the charges
I^{*}:\mathcal{Q}_{\text{IR}}\rightarrow\mathcal{Q}_{\text{UV}}
is surjective. The algebra
\mathcal{A}_{I}\subset\mathcal{L}_{\text{IR}}
is the kernel of this map, i.e. the set of IR charges that are neutral under the UV symmetry. The SymTFT depiction is as follows:

(III.30)

All defects in
\mathcal{A}_{I}
become trivial in
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})
, while those that braid non-trivially with
\mathcal{A}_{I}
are projected out (or confined) by the interface.

The left-hand side of (
III.21
) corresponds to a boundary condition for
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})
, whose Lagrangian algebra is obtained by
sequentially gauging

\mathcal{A}_{I}
and then
\mathcal{L}_{\text{UV}}
. By the Matching Equation, this must coincide with the condensation of
\mathcal{L}_{\text{IR}}
. This is only possible if
\mathcal{A}_{I}
is a subalgebra of
\mathcal{L}_{\text{IR}}
. That is,
\mathcal{A}_{I}
must be an electric condensable algebra (with respect to
\mathcal{L}_{\text{IR}}
).

We conclude by noticing that our discussion gives a simple strategy to derive several examples of injective tensor functors: given an IR SymTFT
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}})
, we condense an algebra
\mathcal{A}_{I}
of our choosing. This gives rise to a UV SymTFT
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})
, for which we choose a symmetry boundary
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{UV}}
. As the condensation happened on the right,
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{UV}}\times\mathcal{I}_{I}
must be an indecomposable IR boundary condition
\mathfrak{B}_{\text{IR}}
, corresponding to a symmetry
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
. Choosing
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{IR}}=\mathfrak{B}_{\text{IR}}
we will always define an injective tensor functor
I:\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\to\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
. We will apply this logic e.g. in section
V
.

III.3.2
Surjective Functors

In the absence of emergent symmetries, the tensor functor between UV and IR symmetries

P:\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}

(III.31)

is surjective, see (
II.8
). It may have a kernel
\ker(P)\subset\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
, encoding the UV symmetries that act trivially at long distances.
The corresponding interface is a condensation interface
\mathcal{I}_{P}
from
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})
to
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}})
, specified by a condensable algebra

\mathcal{A}_{P}\subset\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}).

This setup contrasts with the previous (injective) case: here the algebra
\mathcal{A}_{P}
lives in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})
, rather than in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}})
. However, the situation is not symmetric. Equation (
III.13
) implies that the composed boundary
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{phys}}_{\text{UV}}\times\mathcal{I}_{P}
must define a simple boundary condition of
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}})
, which imposes the constraint

\mathcal{L}_{\text{UV}}\cap\mathcal{A}_{P}=\{1\}\,,

(III.32)

i.e., the condensable algebra must intersect the UV Lagrangian algebra only in the trivial object. We refer to such
\mathcal{A}_{P}
as a
magnetic algebra
(with respect to
\mathcal{L}_{\text{UV}}
). The SymTFT setup describing surjective functors is depicted as follows:
