(III.33)

The intuition is that the magnetic algebra
\mathcal{A}_{P}
represents the set of symmetries that become trivial in the IR. More precisely, once projected to the boundary
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{UV}}
using
\pi_{\text{UV}}
,
\mathcal{A}_{P}
becomes
\ker(P)
.

A crucial fact is that, specifying what subsymmetry becomes trivial in the IR does not uniquely specify
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
. There can be different magnetic algebras
\mathcal{A}
with the same projection onto
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{UV}}
, which give rise to inequivalent TQFTs after condensation. This has an important physical interpretation: the remaining, ‘‘quotient’’ symmetry, can have emergent anomalies
4
4

4

Emergent anomalies recently found many applications in characterizing intrinsically gapless topological phases
Verresen:2019igf
;
Li:2023mmw
;
Bhardwaj:2024qrf
;
Antinucci:2024ltv
.
.

Example: Emergent Anomalies in (1+1)d.

Let us illustrate the above remark with the simplest example of a
(1+1)
d theory with

\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}=\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}\,,

(III.34)

where only the quotient
\mathbb{Z}_{4}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
acts faithfully in the IR.
The dynamical condition for this symmetry reduction is that all excitations created by operators with
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
charge
1
are heavy, while those with charge
2
are light. There are, however, two distinct ways in which this can occur: either with or without an emergent anomaly for the IR
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry.
This example has already been discussed in section
II
, we now revisit it from the perspective of the SymTFT.

The UV SymTFT
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}})
is the untwisted
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
Dijkgraaf-Witten (DW) theory in
2+1
dimensions:

S=\frac{2\pi i}{4}\int a\cup\,db\ ,

(III.35)

whose topological lines are

\bm{Q}_{(n_{e},n_{m})}=\exp\left(\frac{2\pi in_{e}}{4}\int a+\frac{2\pi in_{m}}{4}\int b\right)\,,

(III.36)

with
\ n_{e},n_{m}\in\mathbb{Z}_{4}
. They have
\mathbb{Z}_{4}\times\mathbb{Z}_{4}
fusion rules
\bm{Q}_{n_{e},n_{m}}\otimes\bm{Q}_{n_{e}^{\prime},n_{m}^{\prime}}=\bm{Q}_{n_{e}+n_{e}^{\prime},n_{m}+n_{m}^{\prime}}
and spins

\theta_{n_{e},n_{m}}=\exp{\left(\frac{2\pi in_{e}n_{m}}{4}\right)}\,.

(III.37)

They are generated by the electric and magnetic lines

e:=\bm{Q}_{(1,0)}\ ,\ \ \ \ \ m:=\bm{Q}_{(0,1)}\ .

(III.38)

The canonical Dirichlet boundary
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{UV}}
corresponds to the Lagrangian algebra
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}=1\oplus e\oplus e^{2}\oplus e^{3}
.

Denoting by
\mathsf{D}^{n}
,
n=0,1,2,3
the symmetry generators on
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{UV}}
, we have
\mathsf{D}^{n}=\pi_{\text{UV}}(m^{n}e^{n_{e}})
, for any
n_{e}
. The
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
subgroup is generated by

\mathsf{D}^{2}=\pi_{\text{UV}}(m^{2})=\pi_{\text{UV}}(m^{2}e^{n_{e}})\ .

(III.39)

There are two choices for the RG-interface:

•

\mathcal{A}_{0}=1\oplus m^{2}
. The lines in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}})
are generated by

1\sim m^{2},e^{\prime}:=e^{2}\sim e^{2}m^{2},m^{\prime}=m\sim m^{3},e^{\prime}m^{\prime}:=e^{2}m\sim e^{2}m^{3}
and form the center of a non-anomalous
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, namely
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}})
.

•

\mathcal{A}_{1}=1\oplus m^{2}e^{2}
.
Now
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}})
is generated by

1\sim m^{2}e^{2},e^{\prime}=e^{2}\sim m^{2},s=em\sim e^{3}m^{3},\overline{s}=em^{3}\sim e^{3}m
. These form the so-called double semion TQFT – or twisted
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
DW theory – and describe the center of an anomalous
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, namely
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}^{1})
.

Using (
III.13
)
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{IR}}
must be in both cases the canonical Dirichlet boundary corresponding to
1\oplus e^{\prime}
.

The upshot of our analysis is that there are two different surjective functors

P_{\omega=0,1}:\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}\rightarrow\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}^{\omega=0,1}

(III.40)

with same kernel
\ker(P_{\omega})=\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
, but different IR symmetry: in the first case the IR SymTFT describes an anomaly free
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, while in the second case there is an emergent anomaly for
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
We will see in section
IV.3
that, if
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
involves non-invertible symmetries, not only the anomaly, but even the symmetry group can be different in the IR, while the trivialized subsymmetry stays the same.

III.3.3
Fiber Functors

A fiber functor of a
(d-1)
-category
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
is a tensor functor
F:\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\rightarrow(d-1)\,\mathsf{Vec}
into the “trivial” category
(d-1)\mathsf{Vec}
. Physically, it encodes the mapping of a UV symmetry along an RG that ends in a trivially gapped theory, that is, an SPT.

For this reason the absence of a fiber functor is often taken as a definition of an anomalous symmetry
Thorngren:2019iar
.
While our goal is to give a more fine-grained description of anomalies, (
III.13
) should certainly recover this coarse characterization.

As the SymTFT for the trivial symmetry
(d-1)\mathsf{Vec}
is a trivial invertible topological order, the interface
\mathcal{I}_{F}
now becomes a topological boundary condition for
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})
, given by a Lagrangian algebra
\mathcal{L}_{F}
.
Equation (
III.13
) then states that Lagrangian algebras
\mathcal{L}_{\text{UV}}
and
\mathcal{L}_{F}
must intersect trivially:

\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{S}}
\mathsf{Vec}
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C})
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathsf{Vec})
\mathcal{I}_{F}
1

(III.41)

This implies that a fiber functor is described by a magnetic Lagrangian algebra in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})
. The absence of a magnetic Lagrangian algebra in the center has recently been used in several works
Antinucci:2022vyk
;
Kaidi:2023maf
;
Zhang:2023wlu
;
Antinucci:2023ezl
;
Cordova:2023bja
as a characterization of anomalous categorical symmetries.

III.3.4
Normal Subcategories

We now formulate the SymTFT realization of normal subcategories as defined in section
II.3
, i.e.
\mathcal{N}
that fits into the short exact sequence

\mathcal{N}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle I}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{C}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{S}\,.

(III.42)

A normal subcategory is the kernel of a surjective (normal) functor
P
. To each functor
I
and
P
we can associate algebras
\mathcal{A}_{I}
and
\mathcal{A}_{P}
in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C})
, which realize them in the SymTFT in terms of interfaces.
Following our previous remarks
\mathcal{A}_{I}
is an electric algebra,
\mathcal{A}_{I}\subset\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{C}}
, while
\mathcal{A}_{P}
is magnetic,
\mathcal{A}_{P}\cap\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{C}}=\left\{1\right\}
. The requirement that
I
and
P
correctly combine into a short exact sequence translates into the condition that the algebra

\mathcal{A}_{I}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{P}\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C})

(III.43)

is a Lagrangian algebra, see figure
9
.
Maximality of
\mathcal{A}_{I}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{P}
follows from the relation (
II.18
) between quantum dimensions in a short exact sequence.

\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{N}}
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{C}}
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{S}}
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{N})=\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C})/\mathcal{A}_{I}
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C})
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{S})=\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C})/\mathcal{A}_{P}
\mathcal{I}_{I}
\mathcal{I}_{P}
a\in\mathcal{A}_{I}
1
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{N}}
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{S}}
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{N})=\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C})/\mathcal{A}_{I}
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{S})=\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C})/\mathcal{A}_{P}
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{N},\text{mag}}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{S}}
1
\Downarrow

Figure 9:
SymTFT representation of the short exact sequence of tensor functors (
III.42
). The topological order on the left describes the center of
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{N})
, i.e. of the normal subcategory. The exactness is shown at the top: any topological defect in
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{N})
ends on the symmetry boundary of
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{S}}
. The other two sets of lines simply indicate injectivity and surjectivity.
Due to the exactness, collapsing the middle section,
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C})
,
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{N})
and
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{S})
are separated by the trivial topological order, i.e. the interface between them is a product of two gapped boundary conditions:
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{N},\text{mag}}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{S}}
, respectively.

On the other hand, fusing the two interfaces
\mathcal{I}_{I}
and
\mathcal{I}_{P}
together, we find a factorized interface between
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{N})
and
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{S})
, described by condensation of the algebras
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{N},\text{mag}}\boxtimes\overline{\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{S}}}
.
Here
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{N},\text{mag}}
is a magnetic Lagrangian algebra –
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{N}}\cap\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{N},\text{mag}}=\{1\}
– describing a fiber functor for
\mathcal{N}
.

The corresponding functors fit into a short exact sequence (
III.42
).
The exactness
\text{im}(I)=\ker(P)
has the following pictorial interpretation (see the above figure):
each anyon that can end on
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{S}}
is the image of some anyon in
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{N})
, which cannot end on
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{N}}
.

Due to the existence of the fiber functor,
normal subcategories are anomaly-free
. However it is important to emphasize that not all anomaly-free subcategories are normal, as we will see in an example shortly. The physical interpretation of a normal subcategory is as a part of the symmetry that we can gap-out while preserving the full symmetry
\mathcal{C}
. On the other hand, to gap out an anomaly free but not normal subcategory one needs to break explicitly the remaining symmetry. This fact provides strong predictions: it forbids the existence of
\mathcal{C}
-symmetric relevant deformations that gap a non-normal but anomaly-free subsymmetry.

Non-Example:
{\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\subset\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}^{\omega=2}}
.

In section
II
we have described an anomaly-free subcategory that is not a normal subcategory:
the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
subgroup of a
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
symmetry in (1+1)d with anomaly
2\in H^{3}(B\mathbb{Z}_{4},U(1))=\mathbb{Z}_{4}
:
\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}^{\omega=2}
.

It is useful to recall how to see that there is a non-anomalous
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-subgroup. For this consider the inflow action

S_{\text{inflow}}=\frac{4\pi i}{4}\int A\cup\beta(A)

(III.44)

with
A\in H^{1}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{4})
the background field, and
\beta(A)=\frac{\delta A}{4}
the Bockstein map. The subgroup
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\subset\mathbb{Z}_{4}
is anomaly free:
S_{\text{inflow}}
becomes trivial if
A=2A^{\prime}
. Let us recast it via the SymTFT.

\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}^{\omega=2})
has lines
e^{n_{e}}m^{n_{m}}
,
n_{e},n_{m}\in\mathbb{Z}_{4}
, with spin

\theta_{n_{e},n_{m}}=\exp{\left(-\frac{2\pi i}{4}\left(n_{e}n_{m}-\frac{1}{2}n_{m}^{2}\right)\right)}

(III.45)

and the canonical Lagrangian algebra for the
\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}^{\omega=2}
symmetry is

\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}^{\omega=2}}=1\oplus e\oplus e^{2}\oplus e^{3}\,.

(III.46)

This contains as a subalgebra
\mathcal{A}_{I}=1\oplus e^{2}
and it is easy to check that

\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}^{\omega=2})/\mathcal{A}_{I}=\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}})\ .

(III.47)

Thus we have an injective functor
I:\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\rightarrow\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}^{\omega=2}
that embeds an anomaly-free
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
inside
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
with anomaly
2
.
However there are no non-trivial condensable algebras of
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}^{\omega=2})
that intersect trivially with
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}^{\omega=2}}
, hence
\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}^{\omega=2}
does not have surjective functors with kernel
\text{im}(I)=\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
.

This shows that while
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is anomaly-free, it is not a normal subcategory.
While this category has a fiber functor, it is not of the form
f=P\circ I
. The physical meaning is that
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
can be gapped, but it cannot be gapped if it is embedded inside
\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}^{\omega=2}
. In other words, gapping out
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
requires breaking explicitly
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
down to
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

IV
ASCies as a Measure of Anomalies

Defining and quantifying anomalies for non-invertible symmetries has been a challenge thus far. We will now make propose that there is a simple characterization in terms of ASCies, which are obtained from short exact sequences of tensor functors, which will provide a characterization of all anomalous aspects, in terms of categories
\mathcal{S}_{i}
, of a given category
\mathcal{C}
.

We will first discuss this in the realm of tensor functors, and then use the reformulation in terms of the SymTFT, that allows exploring things systematically and concretely, for any categorical symmetry.
We conclude with some interesting examples of anomaly matching through ASCies.

IV.1
ASCies and Anomalies of Fusion Category Symmetries

Our mathematical description of tensor functors and categorical exact sequences via the SymTFT gives interesting insights into the classification of ’t Hooft anomalies for non-invertible symmetries.

Let us briefly recall the salient features of the problem. In the case of (higher) groups
G
, anomalies are captured by
G
-symmetric
(d+1)
-dimensional Symmetry Protected Topological (SPT) phases, which are invertible TQFT and can be composed by stacking. Thus such anomalies form a group and they can be compared by making use of the group structure. Such an observation was implicitly used ever since the first instances of ’t Hooft anomaly matching
tHooft:1979rat
.

In the case of non-invertible symmetries, the closest analogue of an anomaly theory is the SymTFT—a
non-invertible

(d+1)
-dimensional topological order. However, the absence of a group structure on the space of such TQFTs prevents a direct extension of anomaly matching
Kong:2020cie
.
Thus, while an obstruction theory to the existence of a trivially gapped phase has been developed in several instances
Thorngren:2019iar
;
Apte:2022xtu
;
Damia:2023ses
;
Antinucci:2023ezl
;
Cordova:2023bja
;
Hsin:2025ria
, there is currently no obvious way to relate these obstructions, nor to understand the physical process leading to their cancellation.

This problem is of great physical relevance: two anomalous symmetries are expected to have kinematical obstructions in being connected by an RG-flow if their anomalies do not match. Thus, defining precisely the meaning of this is of crucial importance to extract the maximal amount of constraints from symmetries.

Tensor functors, as discussed in the previous sections, will play a role in addressing this important issue.
On a mundane level, if we have a surjective tensor functor

P:\quad\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\to\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}\,,

(IV.1)

then the pullback
P^{*}
implements the ’t Hooft anomaly matching conditions, see section
II
.
More generally, we will see that short exact sequences of tensor functors

\mathcal{N}\overset{I}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{C}\overset{P}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{S}

(IV.2)

will allow us to quantify anomalies for any categorical symmetry
\mathcal{C}
.
The idea is that an anomaly-free sub-symmetry that is also normal,
\mathcal{N}
in the above sequence, can be consistently gapped out at low energy, and thus forgotten.

It is then natural to propose that an anomaly of the symmetry
\mathcal{C}
is encoded in the image of
P
with “maximal” kernel, denoted by
\mathcal{N}_{\text{max}}
.
For given
\mathcal{C}
, there can be several choices of maps
P_{i}
with maximal kernel
\mathcal{N}_{\text{max},i}
and
\mathcal{S}_{i}
:

\mathcal{N}_{\text{max},i}\overset{I_{i}}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{C}\overset{P_{i}}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{S}_{i}\,.

(IV.3)

However each sequence results in a simple category
\mathcal{S}_{i}
, which is fully anomalous, i.e. is an
ASCy
.
Notice that any category which is not simple, will inevitably fit into one (or more) short exact sequences, and thus eventually lead to an ASCy.

The collection of ASCies
\{\mathcal{S}_{i}\}
should be thought of as “building blocks” for anomalies of the category
\mathcal{C}
:

\mathfrak{A}(\mathcal{C})=\{\mathcal{S}_{i}\text{ satisfying (\ref{Huskiemax})}\}\,.

(IV.4)

Their symmetric gapped phases – described by
\mathcal{S}_{i}
-module categories
\mathcal{M}\in\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathcal{S}_{i}}
– can be pulled back by pre-composition along the tensor functor:

\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathcal{C}}\ \overset{P_{i}^{*}}{\longleftarrow}\ \mathsf{Mod}_{\mathcal{S}_{i}}\,.

(IV.5)

In practice, this provides a map between
\mathcal{S}_{i}
-symmetric phases – in which all the
\mathcal{S}_{i}
symmetry is broken – and
\mathcal{C}
-symmetric phases whose symmetry breaking pattern is enforced by the anomaly.

For example, if a symmetry is anomaly-free, a fiber functor
f:\mathcal{C}\to\mathsf{Vec}
singles out a specific
\mathcal{C}
-preserving gapped phase by pulling back the unique trivially gapped phase for
\mathsf{Vec}
along
f^{*}
:

f^{*}
\mathcal{C}

(IV.6)

If a category
\mathcal{C}
, admits multiple inequivalent ACSies
\mathcal{S}_{i}
, this is the natural generalization of the fact that a symmetry category can admit multiple inequivalent fiber functors.
In practice, the pullbacks along
P_{i}
of the
\mathcal{S}_{i}
-symmetric gapped phases will describe all the maximally symmetry-preserving gapped phases for
\mathcal{C}
.

We will use this characterization of ASCies as measures of anomalies, and in practice the reformulation of short exact sequences using the SymTFT of section
III
, to determine for given symmetry
\mathcal{C}
the set of ASCies
\mathfrak{A}(\mathcal{C})
.

ASCies from the SymTFT.
In section
II.3
we defined Anomalous Simple Categories (ASCies) as categories without any non-trivial normal subcategory.
In the SymTFT we can characterize ASCies as being categories
\mathcal{C}
such that
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C})
do not have non-trivial magnetic algebras
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{C}}=\left\{1\right\}
.
This criterion makes it straightforward to determine if a symmetry category also is an ASCy.

The SymTFT framework provides a computationally powerful tool to determine the possible maximal short exact sequences, and we demonstrate the effectiveness of this approach in several examples in the next section.

IV.2
Example: Anomalous Groups

The first examples are anomalous group symmetries
\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega}
. We will now state some general results and then specialize to cyclic groups to give concrete examples.

Given a short exact sequence of finite groups (here
N\triangleleft G
is a normal subgroup)

1\rightarrow N\rightarrow G\xrightarrow{p}G/N\rightarrow 1\,,

(IV.7)

one could wonder whether it always induces an exact sequence of fusion categories based on
\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega}
for any
\omega\in H^{3}(G,U(1))
. The answer is negative, as we show below.
However, if
\omega
is such that
\omega=p^{*}(\omega^{\prime})
for some
\omega^{\prime}\in H^{3}(G/N,U(1))
, then there is (at least) one surjective functor
P:\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega}\rightarrow\mathsf{Vec}_{G/N}^{\omega^{\prime}}
and a short exact sequence of fusion categories
bruguieres2011exact

\mathsf{Vec}_{N}\rightarrow\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{p^{*}(\omega^{\prime})}\xrightarrow{P}\mathsf{Vec}_{G/N}^{\omega^{\prime}}\,,\ \ \ \omega=p^{*}(\omega^{\prime})\,.

(IV.8)

The latter equation encodes the UV/IR anomaly matching. Thus, an exact sequence of groups can be lifted to an exact sequence of the corresponding (anomalous) fusion categories if and only if the ’t Hooft anomaly matching conditions are satisfied. Notice that while the anomaly must trivialize on
N
,
\omega|_{N}=0
, this condition is not sufficient to ensure the existence of the exact sequence.

Cyclic Groups with Anomalies.

Consider anomalous cyclic groups,
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{N}}^{\omega}
, with
\omega\in H^{3}(\mathbb{Z}_{N},U(1))
its group co-homology anomaly, to wit:

\omega(a,b,c)=\exp\left(\frac{2\pi ik}{N^{2}}a(b+c-[b+c]_{N})\right)\,,

(IV.9)

where
k\in\{0,1,\cdots,N-1\}
, and
[\quad]_{N}
denotes reduction modulo
N
. Its SymTFT is described by a twisted
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
Dijkgraaf-Witten theory, with twist
\omega
. Its spectrum consists of
N^{2}
invertible lines

\bm{Q}_{n_{e},n_{m}}=e^{n_{e}}m^{n_{m}}\,,

(IV.10)

with spins
Barkeshli:2014cna
:

\theta_{n_{e},n_{m}}=\exp\left(-\frac{2\pi i}{N}\left(n_{e}n_{m}-\frac{k}{N}n_{m}^{2}\right)\right)\,,

(IV.11)

and identifications

(n_{e},n_{m})\sim(n_{e}+N,n_{m})\sim(n_{e}+2k,n_{m}+N)\ .

(IV.12)

Any subgroup
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
of
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
,
N=n\ell
, is a normal subgroup and sits inside a short exact sequence of groups:

1\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{n}\overset{\iota}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{Z}_{N}\overset{\pi}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}\longrightarrow 1\,.

(IV.13)

This does not, however, always lift into a short exact sequence of categories.
A necessary condition is obviously that
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
is anomaly-free, that is:

\iota^{*}(\omega)=0\,,\quad\text{or}\quad k=k^{\prime}n\,.

(IV.14)

Contrary to expectations, this is not sufficient to make
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
into a normal subcategory, as discussed in the example of
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}^{\omega=2}
in the previous section.
This example, as well as several ones we will present below, highlight a striking physical consequence of the concept of normal subcategory. An anomaly free subcategory
\mathcal{N}
, if not normal,
cannot
be gapped out while preserving the quotient symmetry
\mathcal{S}
. This highlights the usefulness of the notion of short exact sequences of tensor functors, and the notion of normal subcategories in the context of symmetric RG-flows.

Another important property of ASCies, is that one can in general associate several of them to a given symmetry. This already happens in the case of cyclic groups, as we now show via an example.

Example:
{\mathbb{Z}}_{8}^{\omega=4}
.

The simplest example is given by
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{8}}^{\omega}
with anomaly
\omega=4
. The largest normal subcategory is now
\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
, which fits in two different short exact sequences:

\displaystyle\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\overset{I}{\longrightarrow}\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{8}}^{\omega=4}\overset{P_{1}}{\longrightarrow}\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}^{\omega=1}\,,

(IV.15)

\displaystyle\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\overset{I}{\longrightarrow}\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{8}}^{\omega=4}\overset{P_{-1}}{\longrightarrow}\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}^{\omega=-1}\,,

with the functors
P_{\pm 1}
defined in equation (
IV.22
).
Leading to

\mathfrak{A}(\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{8}}^{\omega=4})=\left\{\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}^{\omega=1},\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}^{\omega=3}\right\}\,.

(IV.16)

At the level of SymTFT, the embedding functor
I:\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\rightarrow\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{8}}^{\omega=4}
can be obtained from the condensation of the electric algebra

\mathcal{A}_{I}=1\oplus e^{2}\oplus e^{4}\oplus e^{6}
in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{8}}^{\omega=4})
, which gives the reduced topological order
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}})
. To work out the possible surjective normal functors and the ASCies, we look for magnetic algebras in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{8}}^{\omega=4})
. It turns out that there are two (maximal) magnetic algebras, which implement the functors
P_{\pm 1}
:

\mathcal{A}_{P_{1}}=1\oplus m^{4}\,,\qquad\mathcal{A}_{P_{-1}}=1\oplus e^{4}m^{4}\,,

(IV.17)

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mathbb{Z}_{8}^{\omega=4})/\mathcal{A}_{P_{1}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Z}(\mathbb{Z}_{4}^{\omega=1})\,,

(IV.18)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mathbb{Z}_{8}^{\omega=4})/\mathcal{A}_{P_{-1}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Z}(\mathbb{Z}_{4}^{\omega=-1})\,.

The situation can be summarized succinctly via the RG-quiche:

\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{8}}^{\omega=4}
\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}^{\omega=\pm 1}
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}})
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{8}}^{\omega=4})
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}^{\omega=\pm 1})
\mathcal{I}_{I}
\mathcal{I}_{P_{\pm 1}}

(IV.19)

Notice that the difference between the two IR anomalies, which is
2\mod 4
, pulls back to the trivial anomaly for
\mathbb{Z}_{8}
: thus, some of the IR anomalies must be emergent. There is however no canonical way to assign which of the two ASCies has an emergent anomaly, contrary to what happens in the case of an anomaly-free UV symmetry. We believe that this observation deserves further study.

Finally let us comment that, in this case, the two ASCies are exchanged by an automorphism of
\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{8}}^{\omega=4}
. To see this, notice that the center of
\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{8}}^{\omega=4}
has a time-reversal symmetry
Delmastro:2019vnj
– which is also a symmetry of
\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{8}}^{\omega=4}
– implemented by:

\Theta\,\left(\begin{array}[]{c}n_{e}\\
n_{m}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&3\\
0&-1\end{array}\right)\,\left(\begin{array}[]{c}n_{e}\\
n_{m}\end{array}\right)\,.

(IV.20)

This is broken by the choice of algebra
\mathcal{A}_{P_{\pm 1}}
, as
\Theta\mathcal{A}_{P_{1}}=\mathcal{A}_{P_{-1}}
and leads to different ASCies
\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}^{\omega=\pm 1}
. It is important to stress that this is not the general case: as we will see in examples below, a given categorical symmetry can have several different ASCies not related by automorphisms.

A more general sequence.

The previous example fits into a larger family of short exact sequence of abelian categories:

\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{n}}\ \overset{I}{\longrightarrow}\ \mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{N}}^{\omega=kn^{2}}\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle P_{k}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\ \mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}}^{\omega=k}\,,\quad N=n\ell\,.

(IV.21)

The projection
P_{k}
acts by
P_{k}(a)=[a]_{\ell}
, but has a nontrivial action on junctions:

\displaystyle P_{k}\left(\hbox to47.95pt{\vbox to39.32pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 24.47234pt\lower-13.91841pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\displaystyle=J_{k}(a,b)\,\hbox to66.76pt{\vbox to46.54pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 33.87517pt\lower-15.4462pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-6.86519pt}{20.25938pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -17.05 -9.84}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}{}}}{{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-30.54216pt}{-9.61319pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 17.05 -9.84}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{15.85344pt}{-9.61319pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.97 0 C 1.97 1.09 1.09 1.97 0 1.97 C -1.09 1.97 -1.97 1.09 -1.97 0 C -1.97 -1.09 -1.09 -1.97 0 -1.97 C 1.09 -1.97 1.97 -1.09 1.97 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,,

(IV.22)

\displaystyle J_{k}(a,b)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\frac{2\pi ik}{\ell^{2}}a(b-[b]_{\ell})\right)\,.

The same anomaly
\omega=kn^{2}
can be represented by different values of
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}
, provided the short exact sequence of groups does not split. This is related to an automorphism of the
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
algebra. More precisely,
kn^{2}
and
k^{\prime}n^{2}
give the same anomaly if
(k-k^{\prime})=\ell/\gcd(n,\ell)\mod\ell
. Which gives
\gcd(n,\ell)
different short exact sequences. This also shows that split sequences of cyclic groups give rise to unique short exact sequences of tensor categories.

We conclude that the set of ASCies for this category is

\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}}^{\nu=k}\in\mathfrak{A}(\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}}^{\omega})\,,

(IV.23)

for
N=n\ell
,
\omega=kn^{2}
, and for all
m>1
such that
m\mid\ell
,
m^{2}\nmid k
.
At the level of SymTFT, this is translated into the existence of several magnetic algebras of the same quantum dimension. This is simple to understand: the normal subcategory
\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{n}}
is described by condensation of the electric algebra:

\mathcal{A}_{I}=\bigoplus_{a=0}^{\ell}\,e^{na}\,.

(IV.24)

We now want to find the possible magnetic algebras which are mutually local with respect to
\mathcal{A}_{I}
. These must be spanned by anyons of the form
e^{a}\,m^{\ell b}
, and are bosons provided that
a=nc
. Now fix a value
c
, the algebra
\mathcal{A}_{P_{c}}
generated by
e^{nc}m^{\ell}
is a magnetic algebra describing an exact sequence of fusion categories. Some of these algebras are identified, this happens if
(c-c^{\prime})\equiv nq\mod\ell
, for some integer
q
, leaving only
\gcd(n,\ell)
distinct magnetic algebras. This matches the counting of the short exact sequences. It is also possible to check that the IR topological orders are
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}}^{k^{\prime}})
where
k^{\prime}=k+r\ell/\gcd(n,\ell)
, for some integer
r
, and the magnetic generator descends from
me^{-rn/\gcd(n,\ell)}
in the UV.

IV.3
Example: Tambara-Yamagami Categories

Tambara-Yamagami categories
\mathsf{TY}(A,\chi,\epsilon)

TambaYama
are a prime candidate to test our ideas in the realm of non-invertible symmetries. Recall that a
\mathsf{TY}(A,\chi,\epsilon)
category is described by an abelian group
A
and a duality defect
\mathcal{D}
satisfying:

\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{D}=\bigoplus_{a\,\in\,A}a\,.

(IV.25)

The associator between two
a
defects and
\mathcal{D}
is encoded in a symmetric bicharacter
\chi
on
A
, while
\epsilon
is the Frobenius-Schur indicator of the self-dual defect
\mathcal{D}
.
The mathematical structure of their anomalies – in the sense of the obstruction theory to the existence of a fiber functor – is well understood
meir2012module
;
Thorngren:2019iar
;
Zhang:2023wlu
;
Antinucci:2023ezl
. The obstruction to the existence of a fiber functor comes into two layers:

1.

A
first obstruction
encoded in the existence of a duality-invariant
A
-SPT. This is described by a Lagrangian subgroup of
A
with respect to the pairing
\chi
. A vanishing first obstruction means that the invertible part of
\mathsf{TY}(A,\chi,\epsilon)
can be trivially gapped.

2.

A
second obstruction
, which is akin to a pure anomaly for
\mathcal{D}
. This vanishes if and only if the Frobenius-Schur indicator can be trivialized in the
A
-invariant SPT. The precise mathematical formulation of such trivialization can be found in
meir2012module
;
Thorngren:2019iar
;
Antinucci:2023ezl
.

The structure of categorical exact sequences involving
\mathsf{TY}
is known
bruguieres2014central
: normal subcategories of
\mathsf{TY}
are either subgroups of its invertible symmetry, or are the full
\mathsf{TY}
itself. We now elucidate some interesting aspects of the interplay between the aforementioned obstruction theory and the structure of surjective tensor functors.

Example: Ising Fusion Category.
The Ising fusion category, that is
\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{2},+)
is by itself an ASCy. This can be immediately seen from the non-existence of magnetic condensable algebras in the Drinfeld center
2009arXiv0905.3117G
. There is, however, an electric condensable algebra

\mathcal{A}_{I}=1\oplus X_{0,-1}\subset\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{2},+)}\,,

(IV.26)

where
X_{0,-1}\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{2},+))
is a simple object of quantum dimension 1 (we follow the notation used in
2009arXiv0905.3117G
to label the simple objects), that corresponds to the embedding
I:\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\rightarrow\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{2},+)
. Indeed while
\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
is an anomaly free subsymmetry, is not a normal one. This extremely simple fact alone predicts a universal fact: there cannot be
\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{2},+)
symmetric relevant deformations that trivialize
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. This is obviously true for instance in the Ising CFT, where the
\varepsilon_{(1/2,1/2)}
deformation, that gaps
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, breaks the duality symmetry.

Example:
\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{4},+)
.
We examine an example of a
\mathsf{TY}
symmetry where the duality defect can be gauged, but the full symmetry still has a ’t Hooft anomaly. We will characterize its anomalies by analyzing its ASCies.
To do so, consider the Drinfeld center
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{4},+))

2009arXiv0905.3117G
. It is composed of the anyons as shown in Table
1
.

X_{0,\pm 1}

X_{1,\pm\zeta_{8}}

X_{2,\pm 1}

X_{3,\pm\zeta_{8}}

Y_{1,0}

Y_{2,0}

Y_{3,0}

Table 1:
Spins and quantum dimensions of the lines of
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{4}))
, where
\zeta_{8}=\exp(\frac{2\pi i}{8})
and
\zeta_{16}=\exp(\frac{2\pi i}{16})
, and we name the anyons following notations in
2009arXiv0905.3117G
. Note that
X_{0,+1}=1
.

We refer the reader to
2009arXiv0905.3117G
for the relevant fusion rules.
The canonical Dirichlet boundary for
\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{4},+)
has Lagrangian algebra:

\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{4},+)}=1\oplus X_{0,-1}\oplus Y_{1,0}\oplus Y_{2,0}\oplus Y_{3,0}\,.

(IV.27)

Non-maximal algebras for this center have been classified in
Bhardwaj:2023bbf
.
There are only two magnetic algebras, both of which are two dimensional:

\mathcal{A}_{P_{+}}=1\oplus X_{2,+1}\,,\quad\mathcal{A}_{P_{-}}=1\oplus X_{2,-1}\,.

(IV.28)

We find, using standard techniques of anyon condensation, that:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{4},+))/\mathcal{A}_{P_{+}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}^{\omega=1})\,,

(IV.29)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{4},+))/\mathcal{A}_{P_{-}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}})\,.

The boundaries
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{IR}\pm}
are determined using (
III.13
):

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}_{\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{4},+)}\times\mathcal{I}_{P_{+}}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{B}_{\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}^{\omega=1}},

(IV.30)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}_{\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{4},+)}\times\mathcal{I}_{P_{-}}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{B}_{\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}^{\omega}}\,,

They both have the same trivially-braiding electric algebra (see (
III.43
)), that is:

\mathcal{A}_{I}=1\oplus X_{2,-1}\oplus Y_{2,0}\,.

(IV.31)

Its condensation corresponds to the injective functor
\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\overset{I}{\longrightarrow}\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{4},+)
.
We are thus led to the RG-quiches:

\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{4},+)
\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}^{\omega=1}
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}})
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{4},+))
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}^{\omega=1})
\mathcal{I}_{I}
\mathcal{I}_{P_{+}}

(IV.32)

\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{4},+)
\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}^{\omega}
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}})
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{4},+))
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}})
\mathcal{I}_{I}
\mathcal{I}_{P_{-}}

(IV.33)

We conclude that
\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{4},+)
has two ASCies associated to it, via the exact sequences:

\hbox to210.29pt{\vbox to88.04pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 105.1459pt\lower-44.02145pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

(IV.34)

From the folded Lagrangian algebras corresponding to the interfaces
\mathcal{I}_{P_{\pm}}
we can also exhibit explicitly the map on objects:

\displaystyle P_{+}(\mathcal{D})=\eta\oplus\eta^{3}\,,\,

\displaystyle P_{+}(a)=\eta^{2}\,,\,

\displaystyle P_{+}(a^{2})=1\,,

(IV.35)

\displaystyle P_{-}(\mathcal{D})=\eta_{1}\oplus\eta_{2}\,,\,

\displaystyle P_{-}(a)=\eta_{1}\eta_{2}\,,\,

\displaystyle P_{-}(a^{2})=1\,,

where
\eta\in\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}^{\omega=1}
and
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}^{\omega}
denote the generators of each symmetry.
Let us comment on this result.

1.

As in previous examples, there is no unique ASCy. Furthermore,
P_{+}
and
P_{-}
cannot be related by any automorphism of
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{4},+))
. Indeed any automorphism relating
X_{2,1}
with
X_{2,-1}
must also exchange even and odd
\Sigma_{a}^{\pm}
, but this is forbidden by their spins.

2.

Again, as in the
\mathsf{Ising}
example, while the invertible symmetry
\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}
is anomaly-free, it does not correspond to a normal subcategory: it can only be preserved at the price of breaking the duality symmetry
\mathcal{D}
.

3.

On the other hand, it is very instructive to consider the fate of the generator of the
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
symmetry after the normal subcategory
\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
has been quotiented out.
It can be explicitly checked the tensor functors
P_{\pm}
are only consistent if the corresponding object in the IR has a nontrivial
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
anomaly.
While in other cases – such as the
\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}\to\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}^{\omega}
RG map – we had interpreted this as an emergent anomaly, let us stress that in the present example this is not quite correct.
As the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
quotient must be broken in all of the gapped phases of
\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{4},+)
, it was anomalous already in the full UV
\mathsf{TY}
symmetry, due to its nontrivial interplay with the duality symmetry.
Its description in the ASCies is however much simpler, and it pulls back through
P^{*}_{\pm}
to a – significantly more complex – mixed anomaly involving the non-invertible duality symmetry.

4.

It is also instructive to study the map between gapped phases of
\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{4},+)
and those of its ASCies. The UV symmetry has three distinct phases
Thorngren:2019iar
;
Bhardwaj:2023idu
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{4},+):

where dots of the same color denote gapped vacua with the same Euler counterterm and we have indicated in black/red the action of the invertible and non-invertible symmetry generators, respectively. The first phase is present in all
\mathsf{TY}
categories and corresponds to the maximal breaking of the
\mathsf{TY}
symmetry – i.e. the regular module category – the other two instead preserve the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
subgroup of
\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{4},+)
. The two ASCies associated to
\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{4},+)
have, respectively, one and three gapped phases:

It is clear that they describe (all) the non-maximal symmetry-breaking phases of
\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{4},+)
: we conclude that those
symmetry-breaking patterns are anomaly-enforced
.

\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2},\chi_{d},-)
.
Another interesting example is that of a
\mathsf{TY}
category whose anomaly strictly lies in the “second obstruction”, that is in a nontrivial Frobenius-Schur indicator. To this end we consider the
A=\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
Tambara-Yamagami category that does not admit a fiber functor, i.e., the one with diagonal bicharacter
\chi_{d}
and nontrivial FS indicator
\epsilon=-1
. We will henceforth drop
\chi_{d}
for ease of notation.
The anyon composition of its center is shown in Table
2
.

Table 2:
Spins and quantum dimensions of the simple lines of
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}))
. Here
\zeta_{8}=\exp(\frac{2\pi i}{8})
and
X_{(0,0),+1}=1
.

The symmetry boundary condition is described by the Lagrangian algebra:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2},-)}

\displaystyle=1\oplus X_{(0,0),-1}\oplus Y_{(0,0),(1,0)}

\displaystyle\quad\oplus Y_{(0,0),(0,1)}\oplus Y_{(0,0),(1,1)}\,.

(IV.38)

There is only a single largest magnetic algebra in this case, which has dimension 4
GaiSchaferNamekiWarman
:

\mathcal{A}_{P}=1\oplus X_{(1,1),+1}\oplus Y_{(1,0),(0,1)}\,.

(IV.39)

Anyon condensation leads to the double-semion theory as the reduced TO:

\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2},-))/\mathcal{A}_{P}=\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}^{\omega=1})\,,

(IV.40)

The map
\phi_{P}
that defines this functor is

\phi_{P}:\quad\left\{\begin{aligned} 1\oplus X_{(1,1),+1}\oplus Y_{(1,0),(0,1)}&\mapsto 1\cr X_{(0,0),-1}\oplus X_{(1,1),-1}\oplus Y_{(0,0),(1,0)}&\mapsto e\cr(Z_{\rho_{3},i}\oplus Z_{\rho_{4},i})&\mapsto s\cr(Z_{\rho_{3},-i}\oplus Z_{\rho_{4},-i})&\mapsto\bar{s}\,.\end{aligned}\right.

(IV.41)

By using the projections on the boundary categories we find the action of
P:\mathsf{TY}({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2},-)\to\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}^{\omega=1}
on objects:

P(\mathcal{D})=1\oplus\eta\,,\ P(a_{1})=P(a_{2})=1\,.

(IV.42)

The electric algebra satisfying (
III.43
) and braiding with
\mathcal{A}_{P}
trivially is:

\mathcal{A}_{I}=1\oplus X_{(0,0),-1}\,.

(IV.43)

It leads to the embedding
\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\overset{I}{\rightarrow}\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2},-)
giving rise to the RG-quiche:

\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2},-)
\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}^{\omega=1}
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}})
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2},-))
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}^{\omega=1})
\mathcal{I}_{I}
\mathcal{I}_{P}

(IV.44)

The categorical exact sequence:

\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\overset{I}{\longrightarrow}\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2},-)\overset{P}{\longrightarrow}\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}^{\omega=1}\,,

(IV.45)

conforms to our intuition: the
\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2},-)
symmetry has trivial first obstruction to a fiber functor (a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
SPT which is invariant under gauging exists), but the duality symmetry still suffers from a “group-cohomology” ’t Hooft anomaly, reflected in the nontrivial Frobenius-Schur indicator
Antinucci:2023ezl
. The
\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}^{\omega=1}
SSB phase pulls back to the minimal SSB phase for our TY category:

(IV.46)

while the SSB of the invertible symmetry is not anomaly-enforced. Similar lessons can be drawn whenever the first obstruction for the
\mathsf{TY}
category vanishes, and resonate with our physical picture of surjective tensor functors as anomaly quantifiers. We will discuss a similar example in (3+1)d in section
V.5
.

V
Emergent Higher-Form Symmetries and Fractionalization

In this section we study anomaly matching in space-time dimension
d>2
, where a key new ingredient compared to (1+1)d is the presence of higher-form symmetries
Gaiotto:2014kfa
.
The higher-dimensional generalization of tensor functors and short exact sequences for categories remains largely unexplored mathematical territory. However, by employing the SymTFT approach, we are able to navigate around some of these challenges and nevertheless formulate a precise quantification of anomalies in higher dimensions. We believe that all of these concepts should ultimately possess a rigorous mathematical foundation, and we support this perspective with a detailed discussion for the 2+1d case in appendix
A.6
.

Although they often emerge only at long distances, higher-form symmetries can play a crucial role in matching the anomalies of microscopic 0-form symmetries. The main concept here is
symmetry fractionalization
, as developed originally in the condensed matter literature
Senthil:1999czm
;
Essin:2013rca
;
Barkeshli:2014cna
;
Chen:2014wse
, while its manifestation in field theoretic RG-flows is known as
symmetry transmutation

Seiberg:2025bqy
(see
Delmastro:2022pfo
;
Brennan:2022tyl
;
Antinucci:2024bcm
;
Brennan:2025acl
for previous studies).
At the level of tensor functors, this is implemented by the fact that 0-form symmetry defects can map in the IR to composite defects built from emergent higher-form symmetries. In section
V.2
, we show how to incorporate such functors into the SymTFT interface framework. Sections
V.3
and
V.4
address symmetry-preserving gapped realizations, connecting with
Wang:2017loc
, and we comment on non-invertible examples in section
V.5
.

V.1
Fractionalization and Transmutation

Before starting the SymTFT interface analysis, let us briefly review what are the type of phenomena that we want to describe. A distinct feature of RG-flow in space-time dimension
d>2
is that, the background field
A_{1}^{(\text{UV})}
for a
UV
0-form symmetry can sometimes force a non-trivial background field

B_{p+1}^{(\text{IR})}=\mathcal{F}(A_{1}^{(\text{UV})})

(V.1)

for a
p
-form symmetry that emerges at long distance, in the sense that its selection rules are only valid below some energy scale.

To understand the physics behind relations like (
V.1
), recall that the typical way in which
p
-form symmetries arise at long distance is that some
p
-dimensional defects
W_{p}
, which are endable in the UV, become unbreakable at long distances.
Letting

\partial W_{p}=\mathcal{O}_{p-1}\,,

(V.2)

with
\mathcal{O}_{p-1}
a non-genuine operator
5
5

5

Often non-genuine operators refer to those attached to topological operators of one dimension higher. Here we use it also in the context of not necessarily topological operators.
,
W_{p}
becomes unbreakable at long distance if
\mathcal{O}_{p}
becomes extremely heavy and decouples from the theory in the IR. In this limit,
W_{p}
can carry a nontrivial emergent
p
-form symmetry charge at low energies.

If the system has a UV 0-form symmetry
G_{\text{UV}}
, all gauge invariant local operators transform in linear representations of
G_{\text{UV}}
. On the other hand non-genuine operators
\mathcal{O}_{p-1}
, which are not gauge invariant, can transform in projective (higher) representations of
G_{\text{UV}}

Brennan:2022tyl
;
Delmastro:2022pfo
;
Bhardwaj:2023wzd
. At low energies, even if
W_{p}
becomes unbreakable, it must transform in the same projective representation as
\mathcal{O}_{p-1}
see figure
10
. Such a projectivity can be understood as a manifestation of a defect anomaly
Antinucci:2024izg
.

W_{p}
g_{1}
g_{2}
W_{p}
g_{1}g_{2}
\mathcal{F}(g_{1},g_{2})
=
W_{p}
g_{1}g_{2}
\gamma_{W}(g_{1},g_{2})\,\times

Figure 10:
The projective action of
G_{\text{UV}}
on
W_{p}
can be understood in terms of symmetry fractionalization. In this case
\gamma_{W}(g_{1},g_{2})=\mathcal{F}(g_{1},g_{2})[W_{p}]
is matched by the topological charge of
W_{p}
under the fractionalized higher form symmetry.

Put differently, in the IR,
W_{p}
has to be charged under the 0-form symmetry
G_{\text{UV}}
, i.e. a network of
p
-form symmetry defects – under which the
p
-dimensional operator is charged – is induced at junctions of the
G_{\text{UV}}
symmetry defects, see figure
10
. The precise relation between the backgrounds is (
V.1
).

Example.

As a typical example consider
SU(N)
QCD with massive quarks. The baryon symmetry
U(1)_{B}
assigns unit charge to baryonic operators constructed out of
N
quarks. Thus the (non gauge invariant) quark operator
q(x)
has fractional
1/N
charge under
U(1)_{B}
. Below the mass scale of the quarks, the Wilson lines
W_{R}
become unbreakable and are charged under the emergent
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{(1)}
1-form center symmetry: the fundamental Wilson line is the world-line of
q(x)
, and carries
1/N
charge under
U(1)_{B}
, implying that a background
A_{1}^{(\text{UV})}
activates a background for
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{(1)}
:

B_{2}^{(\text{IR})}\equiv c_{1}\left(A_{1}^{(\text{UV})}\right)\,(\text{mod }N)\in H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{N})\,,

(V.3)

where
c_{1}\left(A_{1}^{(\text{UV})}\right)
denotes the first Chern-class.

For our discussion, it will be crucial to understand the role played by this phenomenon for anomaly matching
Delmastro:2022pfo
;
Brennan:2022tyl
;
Antinucci:2024bcm
;
Seiberg:2025bqy
: the inflow action of the UV symmetry is reproduced in the IR by an anomaly of the emergent higher-form symmetries through relations as (
V.1
). Indeed the UV anomaly for
G_{\text{UV}}
can be matched, in the IR, by a symmetry
G
that either does not act on local operators, or it does but in a non-anomalous way.
This is due to the fact that the emergent higher-form symmetries can come with an anomaly, which may or may not involve
G
.
Plugging (
V.1
) into the IR anomaly inflow action, we can match the UV one. This type of matching by higher-form symmetries is crucial in the discussion of symmetry preserving gapped phases for anomalous symmetries, such as the Wang-Wen-Witten construction
Wang:2017loc
.
Let us illustrate this through a few elementary examples.

U(1)

in Even Dimensions.

An elementary example (see
Antinucci:2024bcm
for more details) is a
U(1)
symmetry in
d\in 2\mathbb{Z}
space-time dimensions with perturbative anomaly

S_{\text{inflow}}^{(\text{UV})}=\frac{ik}{(2\pi)^{d/2}\left(d/2+1\right)!}\int_{X_{d+1}}A_{1}^{(\text{UV})}\wedge\Big(dA_{1}^{(\text{UV})}\Big)^{d/2}\ .

(V.4)

If the symmetry is spontaneously broken, then the IR is a compact Goldstone boson
\phi\sim\phi+2\pi
where the
U(1)
symmetry acts non-linearly, but it is (naively) non-anomalous, as there is no
U(1)
WZW term. However the IR also has an emergent
U(1)^{(d-2)}

(d-2)
-form symmetry corresponding to the conservation of the winding number of
\phi
. The two symmetries have a mixed anomaly

S_{\text{inflow}}^{(\text{IR})}=\frac{i}{2\pi}\int_{X_{d+1}}B^{(\text{IR})}_{d-1}\wedge dA^{(\text{IR})}_{1}\ .

(V.5)

This matches the UV anomaly in the IR only if
A_{1}^{(\text{IR})}=A_{1}^{(\text{UV})}
and if
A_{1}^{(\text{UV})}
induces a background for a
(d-2)
-form symmetry

B^{(\text{IR})}_{d-1}=\frac{k}{(2\pi)^{d/2-1}\left(d/2+1\right)!}A_{1}^{(\text{UV})}\wedge\left(dA_{1}^{(\text{UV})}\right)^{d/2-1}\,.

(V.6)

U(1)_{0}
in (2+1)d with Fermions.

Another interesting example arises in
(2+1)
d
U(1)
gauge theory with
N_{f}\in 2\mathbb{Z}
fermions
\psi_{i}
and trivial total Chern-Simons level. The flavor symmetry is
G_{\text{UV}}=U(N_{f})/\mathbb{Z}_{\frac{N_{f}}{2}}
, and if
N_{f}>2
it has a self-anomaly
Hsin:2024wki

S_{\text{inflow}}^{(\text{UV})}=\frac{2\pi i}{2N_{f}/2}\int_{X_{4}}w_{2}(A_{1}^{(\text{UV})})\cup w_{2}(A_{1}^{(\text{UV})})\,,

(V.7)

where
w_{2}(A_{1}^{(\text{UV})})\in H^{2}(X_{4},\mathbb{Z}_{N_{f}/2})
represents the obstruction of lifting the
G_{\text{UV}}
bundle to a
U(N_{f})
bundle. Notice that the fermions
\psi_{i}
, that are not gauge invariant, transform linearly under
U(N_{f})
but projectively under
G_{\text{UV}}
. Adding a mass
M>0
for the fermions, the theory flows in the IR to a pure Chern-Simons theory
U(1)_{N_{f}/2}
. The Wilson line of charge
1
, being the world-line of
\psi_{i}
, becomes unbreakable and charged under and an emergent
\mathbb{Z}_{N_{f}/2}^{(1)}
1-form symmetry. The fractional charge of
\psi_{i}
then implies that
A_{1}^{(\text{UV})}
forces a background

B_{2}^{(\text{IR})}=w_{2}(A_{1}^{(\text{UV})})

(V.8)

for
\mathbb{Z}_{N_{f}/2}^{(1)}
. While the flavor symmetry does not act on local operators in the IR, its anomaly is matched by the anomaly of the 1-form symmetry

S_{\text{inflow}}=\frac{2\pi i}{2N_{f}/2}\int_{X_{4}}B_{2}^{(\text{IR})}\cup B_{2}^{(\text{IR})}

(V.9)

using (
V.8
).

While all relations like (
V.1
) have an interpretation in terms of symmetry fractionalization
Barkeshli:2014cna
of the UV 0-form symmetry with the emergent higher-form symmetry, we will see in the next subsections how to phrase them – when the symmetries involved are finite – as precise statements on the functor
F:\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
that we will derive from SymTFT interfaces.

V.2
Anomaly Matching with Emergent Symmetries

We now present two scenarios how an anomalous 0-form symmetry in the UV, can be matched with an emergent higher-form symmetry. In the current section we will construct a functor from

\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}=G_{\text{UV}}^{(0),\omega}

(V.10)

to the IR symmetry, which involves a 0-form symmetry
G=G_{\text{UV}}
in terms of groups, but with trivial anomaly, and a
(d-2)
-form symmetry
\widehat{K}^{(d-2)}
, which have a mixed anomaly
\nu
:

\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}=G^{(0),\omega=0}\times^{\nu}\widehat{K}^{(d-2)}\,.

(V.11)

In section
V.3
we furthermore construct a gapped phase, which is
G^{(0)}
-symmetric, which has an emergent
K^{(1)}
1-form symmetry, and no 0-form symmetry acting non-trivially on local operators.

V.2.1
General Analysis Starting with
G^{(0),\omega}_{\text{UV}}

We present now a simple higher dimensional implementation of the RG-interfaces realized in the SymTFT construction. A related discussion also appeared recently in
Perez-Lona:2025ncg
. The starting point is an anomalous invertible symmetry in
d
spacetime dimensions given by a 0-form symmetry
G_{\text{UV}}\cong G
with anomaly
\omega\in H^{d+1}(BG_{\text{UV}},U(1))
. We denote this symmetry by

\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}=G^{(0),\omega}_{\text{UV}}\equiv(d-1)\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega}\,,

(V.12)

and its generators by
\mathsf{D}_{d-1}^{g}
. We will denote
p
-form symmetries
A
(whenever the degree is specified) by
A^{(p)}
.
We want to use the SymTFT interfaces to produce a tensor functor
F:\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
, where,
in
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
the UV symmetry
G^{(0),\omega}_{\text{UV}}
acts in a non-anomalous fashion. By this, we mean that the UV self-anomaly is matched by an emergent higher-form symmetry, and
G^{(0)}
may act non-faithfully on local operators in the IR. Inspired by
Wang:2017loc
, we construct
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
in two steps:

1.

First, we find a group
\widetilde{G}
which extends
G_{\text{UV}}
while trivializing the anomaly
\omega
, i.e. there is a short exact sequence

1\longrightarrow K\overset{\iota}{\longrightarrow}\widetilde{G}\overset{p}{\longrightarrow}G_{\text{UV}}\longrightarrow 1

(V.13)

such that the pull-back
p^{*}\omega
of the
G_{\text{UV}}
anomaly is trivial in
\widetilde{G}
:
p^{*}(\omega)=0
. We will assume that the extension is central, so that
K
is abelian, and the extension data is determined by a class

\nu\in H^{2}(G_{\text{UV}},K)\,.

(V.14)

The SymTFT for this non-anomalous symmetry
\widetilde{G}
will have an interface with
\mathcal{Z}(G^{(0),\omega}_{\text{UV}})
obtained by condensing defects in
\mathcal{Z}(\widetilde{G}^{(0)})
that form an algebra
\mathcal{A}
6
6

6

When the bulk has dimension
\geq 4
we use the terminology of condensable (and Lagrangian) algebras in a loose sense. For us they will be a collection of mutually local objects that can simultaneously terminate on an interface/boundary. Notice that they often involve defects of different dimensionality, tied together in a consistent manner. Lagrangian algebras are still maximal, in the sense that all topological operators outside the algebra braid nontrivially with some of its generators.

\mathfrak{Z}(G^{(0),\omega})=\mathfrak{Z}(\widetilde{G}^{(0)})/\mathcal{A}\,,

(V.15)

which in the SymTFT takes the following form with the interface
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}}
defined by the condensable algebra
\mathcal{A}
:

(V.16)

2.

Next, we construct an appropriate gapped BC
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{IR}}
for
\mathcal{Z}(\widetilde{G}^{(0)})
, described by a Lagrangian algebra
\mathcal{L}_{\text{IR}}
. This will correspond to the IR symmetry
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
to which
G_{\text{UV}}^{(0),\omega}
admits a tensor functor.
As we have pointed out in section
III.3.1
, given a Lagrangian algebra
\mathcal{L}_{\text{IR}}
that contains
\mathcal{A}

\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{L}_{\text{IR}}\,,

(V.17)

then the associated symmetries
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
automatically satisfy the (ME) (
III.13
), defining an injective tensor functor:

I:\quad G^{(0),\omega}_{\text{UV}}\to\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}\,.

(V.18)

From our construction of
\widetilde{G}
(
V.13
), the gapped boundary condition
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{IR}}
can be reached from the canonical Dirichlet boundary condition in
\mathcal{Z}(\widetilde{G})
by gauging the normal subgroup
K\subset\widetilde{G}
.

In
d
spacetime dimensions, this means that
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
will include a 0-form symmetry
G^{(0)}=\widetilde{G}/K
as well as a dual
\widehat{K}^{(d-2)}

(d-2)
-form symmetry.
Notably, while
G
is isomorphic to
G_{\text{UV}}
as a group, it has a trivial self ’t Hooft anomaly inside
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
. On the other hand it has a mixed anomaly with
\widehat{K}^{(d-2)}
determined by the extension (
V.13
)
Tachikawa:2017gyf
:

S_{\text{inflow}}=2\pi i\int\beta\left(A_{1}^{(\text{IR})}\right)\cup C^{(\text{IR})}_{d-1}\,,

(V.19)

where
A_{1}^{(\text{IR})}
is the
G
-gauge field,
C^{(\text{IR})}_{d-1}
the gauge field for the emergent
(d-2)
-form symmetry
\widehat{K}
and
\beta
the Bockstein map associated to the short exact sequence of groups.
We conclude that the IR symmetry then is

\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}=G^{(0),\omega=0}\times^{\nu}\widehat{K}^{(d-2)}\,,

(V.20)

where
\times^{\nu}
denotes the mixed ’t Hooft anomaly
Tachikawa:2017gyf
. In the SymTFT our discussion leads to the following setup:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{105.27702pt}{-12.41193pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{split}

(V.21)

The anomaly
\omega
of the UV symmetry group
G_{\text{UV}}^{(0),\omega}
is non-trivially realized in the IR by
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
, which contains an anomaly-free 0-form symmetry
G
: while the topological defects of
G_{\text{UV}}^{(0),\omega}
are mapped into those of the anomaly-free IR subgroup
G\subset\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
, the mapping of their morphisms is non-trivial. Specifically certain configuration of UV defects that intersect on a line, are mapped into configuration of IR symmetry defects where the line is dressed with a generator of the 2-form symmetry
\widehat{K}^{(d-2)}
. The UV anomaly is matched by a nontrivial symmetry fractionalization for the IR 0-form symmetry and encoded in this non-trivial modification of the junctions. We will show this very explicitly through examples.

V.2.2
Example: (3+1)d with
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{(0),k}

Let us illustrate the previous discussion in
(3+1)
d space-time dimensions for
G_{\text{UV}}=\mathbb{Z}_{N}
. The group-cohomology anomaly
k
takes value in
k\in H^{5}(B\mathbb{Z}_{N},U(1))=\mathbb{Z}_{N}
, i.e.

\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}={\mathbb{Z}}_{N}^{(0),\omega=k}\,,

(V.22)

and is encoded in the anomaly-inflow action

S_{\text{inflow}}^{(\text{UV})}=\frac{2\pi ik}{N}\int_{X_{5}}A_{1}^{(\text{UV})}\cup\beta\left(A_{1}^{(\text{UV})}\right)\cup\beta\left(A_{1}^{(\text{UV})}\right)\,,

(V.23)

where
\beta
is the Bockstein homomorphism.
Such an anomaly can be trivialized by the extension

1\rightarrow{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}\rightarrow{\mathbb{Z}}_{N^{2}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}\rightarrow 1\ ,

(V.24)

from the previous discussion, the
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
anomaly can be matched by an IR symmetry
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{(0)}\times\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{(2)}
with a mixed anomaly

S_{\text{inflow}}^{(\text{IR})}=\frac{2\pi i}{N}\int_{X_{5}}B_{3}\cup\beta\left(A_{1}^{(\text{IR})}\right)\ .

(V.25)

We now show how to obtain this result within the framework of the SymTFT.

The SymTFT for the UV symmetry is the (4+1)d twisted DW theory, with action (see
Kaidi:2023maf
for a discussion of its salient properties)

S_{\text{SymTFT}}=\frac{2\pi i}{N}\int_{X_{5}}\Big(a\cup db+k\,a\cup\beta(a)\cup\beta(a)\Big)\ .

(V.26)

Here
a\in C^{1}(X_{5},\mathbb{Z}_{N}),b\in C^{3}(X_{5},\mathbb{Z}_{N})
. The relevant topological operators are:

1.

Line operators

\bm{Q}_{1}^{(n_{e})}
labeled by
n_{e}\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}

\bm{Q}_{1}^{(n_{e})}(\gamma_{1})=\exp{\left(\frac{2\pi in_{e}}{N}\int_{\gamma_{1}}a\right)}\ .

(V.27)

2.

3-surface operators

\bm{Q}_{3}^{(n_{m},l)}
labeled by
n_{m},\,l\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}

\bm{Q}_{3}^{(n_{m},l)}(\Sigma_{3})=\exp{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{N}\int_{\Sigma_{3}}n_{m}\,b+l\,a\cup\beta(a)\right)}\ .

(V.28)

The defects
\bm{Q}_{3}^{(0,l)}
can be interpreted as theta defects
Bhardwaj:2022kot
;
Bhardwaj:2022lsg
characterized by a gauged SPT
l\in H^{3}(B\mathbb{Z}_{N},U(1))\cong\mathbb{Z}_{N}
on their worldvolume.

Electric and magnetic defects,
\bm{Q}_{1}^{(n_{e})}(\gamma_{1})
and
\bm{Q}_{3}^{(n_{m},l)}(\Sigma_{3})
, have canonical (ordinary) linking given by

\langle\bm{Q}_{3}^{(n_{m},l)}(\Sigma_{3})\,\bm{Q}_{1}^{n_{e}}(\gamma_{1})\rangle=\exp{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{N}n_{e}n_{m}\text{Lk}(\gamma_{1},\Sigma_{3})\right)}\ .

(V.29)

Furthermore, there is a nontrivial (type 2) triple linking between
\bm{Q}_{3}^{(n_{m}^{i},l^{i})}(\Sigma^{i}_{3})
defects:
7
7

7

The geometric triple linking invariant
\text{Lk}(\Sigma_{3}^{1},\Sigma_{3}^{2},\Sigma_{3}^{3})_{2}
can be described as the ordinary linking number between
\Sigma_{3}^{1}
and the line
\Sigma_{3}^{2}\cap\Sigma_{3}^{3}
.

\displaystyle\langle\bm{Q}_{3}^{(n_{m}^{1},l^{1})}(\Sigma_{1})\bm{Q}_{3}^{(n_{m}^{2},l^{2})}(\Sigma_{2})\bm{Q}_{3}^{(n_{m}^{3},l^{3})}(\Sigma_{3})\rangle

(V.30)

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\exp\left(\frac{4\pi i\text{Lk}(\Sigma_{3}^{1},\Sigma_{3}^{2},\Sigma_{3}^{3})_{2}}{N^{2}}\Big(n_{m}^{1}n_{m}^{2}l^{3}+n_{m}^{1}l^{2}n_{m}^{3}+\right.

\displaystyle\left.+l^{1}n_{m}^{2}n_{m}^{3}-\frac{3k}{N}n_{m}^{1}n_{m}^{2}n_{m}^{3}\Big)\right)\,.

This is particularly relevant for us, as it is the simplest bulk observable detecting the anomaly.
We give a derivation of this equation and more details which are needed to understand algebras in appendix
A.7
.

The canonical Dirichlet boundary
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{UV}}
with
G_{\text{UV}}
symmetry corresponds to a Lagrangian algebra:

\mathcal{L}_{\text{UV}}=\Big\langle\bm{Q}_{1}^{(1)},\bm{Q}_{3}^{(0,1)}\Big\rangle=\left\{\bm{Q}_{1}^{(n_{e})},\bm{Q}_{3}^{(0,l)},\ n_{e},l\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}\right\}\ .

(V.31)

Notice that all of the theta-defects
\bm{Q}_{3}^{(0,l)}
must be added to get a Lagrangian algebra, as they braid trivially with
\bm{Q}_{1}^{(n_{e})}
. Once this is done, the set becomes maximal: all the other defects have non-trivial braiding with at least one element of
\mathcal{L}_{\text{UV}}
.
The symmetry generators are the projection of
\bm{Q}_{3}^{(n_{m},l)}
onto the boundary:

\mathsf{D}_{3}^{(n_{m})}=\pi_{\text{UV}}\left(\bm{Q}_{3}^{(n_{m},l)}\right)\ .

(V.32)

The RG-quiche.
We will now present an explicit derivation of the RG-quiche leading to the enhanced
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
symmetry. For simplicity we will restrict our attention to the case of odd
N
and
\gcd(N,k)=1
. The more general case is similar, but requires further sophistication.
8
8

8

The case of
N
even is technically more subtle, as the interfaces are not obtained by simple condensation, but by condensation with discrete torsion valued in
H^{5}(B^{2}\mathbb{Z}_{N},U(1))\cong\mathbb{Z}_{\text{gcd}(N,2)}
.

To satisfy (
V.16
) we take
K=\mathbb{Z}_{N}
and
\widetilde{G}=\mathbb{Z}_{N^{2}}
. We will denote topological defects in
\mathfrak{Z}(\widetilde{G})
by
\widetilde{\bm{Q}}
.
The IR gapped boundary condition corresponding to

\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}=\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}^{(2)}\times^{\nu}\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{(0)}

(V.33)

is implemented by the Lagrangian algebra:

\mathcal{L}_{\text{IR}}=\left\{\widetilde{\bm{Q}_{1}}^{Nr_{e}}\,,\widetilde{\bm{Q}_{3}}^{Nr_{m},Ns}\ |\ r_{e},r_{m},s=0,...,N-1\right\}\,,

(V.34)

the algebra multiplication map is nontrivial and assigns to a codimension-two intersection
\gamma_{ij}=\Sigma_{i}\cap\Sigma_{j}
a dressing by the line

\widetilde{\bm{Q}_{1}}^{m_{e}^{ij}}\,,\quad m_{e}^{ij}=-(r_{m}^{i}s^{j}+r_{m}^{j}s^{i})+N\widetilde{k}r_{m}^{i}r_{m}^{j}\,,

(V.35)

where
\widetilde{k}
will be related to the possible symmetry fractionalization classes.
This ensures that the triple linking between generators is always trivial. It can be checked that the triple linking of no other 3-surface operator can be trivialized by the same token. The IR symmetry operators are:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}_{3}^{(n_{m})}=\pi_{\text{IR}}\left(\widetilde{\bm{Q}}_{3}^{(n_{m}+Nr_{m},Ns)}\right)\,,\ \

\displaystyle n_{m}=0,...,N-1

(V.36)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}_{1}^{(n_{e})}\ =\pi_{\text{IR}}\left(\widetilde{\bm{Q}}_{1}^{(n_{e}+Nr_{e})}\right)\,,\ \

\displaystyle n_{e}=0,...,N-1\ .

together with the generalized theta-defects constructed from the 2-form symmetry:

\widetilde{\Theta}_{3}^{(l)}=\pi_{\text{IR}}\left(\widetilde{\bm{Q}}_{3}^{(0,l+Ns)}\right)\ ,\ \ \ \ \ l=0,...,N-1\ .

(V.37)

We now want to construct the interface algebra
\mathcal{A}_{k}
landing us on
\mathfrak{Z}(G^{(0),\omega=k})
. As per our previous discussion, such algebra should be
electric
in order to give rise to a tensor functor.
The correct algebra is:

\mathcal{A}_{k}=\Big\langle\widetilde{\bm{Q}}_{3}^{(Nr_{m},0)}\Big\rangle\,,\ \ \ \ m_{e}^{ij}=N\widetilde{k}r_{m}^{i}r_{m}^{j}\,,

(V.38)

with
\widetilde{k}=-2^{-1}k^{-1}
. Notice that this is an electric algebra, that can be completed into
\mathcal{L}_{\text{IR}}
.
The condensation map identifies:

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{Q}}_{1}^{(Nn_{e})}

\displaystyle\mapsto\quad\bm{Q}_{1}^{(n_{e})}\,,

(V.39)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{Q}}_{3}^{(n_{m},-Nkn_{m})}

\displaystyle\mapsto\quad\bm{Q}_{3}^{(n_{m},0)}\,.

By computing the triple linking of
\widetilde{\bm{Q}}_{3}^{(n_{m},-Nkn_{m})}
, it is straightforward to check that they reproduce the cubic anomaly
k\in H^{5}(\mathbb{Z}_{N},U(1))
.
This is compatible with the tensor functor:

I(\mathsf{D}_{3}^{(n_{m})})=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}_{3}^{(n_{m})}\,.

(V.40)

More interestingly, the symmetry fractionalization class in the IR can be detected via the dressing of certain junctions of 0-form symmetry generators. To see this, consider a boundary UV defect
\mathsf{D}_{3}^{(1)}(\Sigma_{3})
on a three-manifold
\Sigma_{3}
for which (
PD
denotes the Poincare dual):

\text{PD}(\Sigma_{3})\wedge d\text{PD}(\Sigma_{3})=N\text{PD}(\gamma_{1})\,.

(V.41)

Using (
V.39
), we see that the tensor functor maps it into
\pi_{\text{IR}}(\widetilde{\bm{Q}}_{3}^{(1,-Nk)})
in the IR. The surface
\Sigma_{3}
turns on the symmetry fractionalization background we want to detect.
Lifting the setup slightly into the bulk, this is encoded in the action:

\displaystyle S[\Sigma]

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi i}{N^{2}}\int_{X_{5}}(da_{\text{IR}}+d\text{PD}(\Sigma))\,b_{\text{IR}}

(V.42)

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle-\frac{2\pi ik}{N^{3}}\int_{X_{5}}d\text{PD}(\Sigma)a_{\text{IR}}da_{\text{IR}}\,,

shifting
a_{\text{IR}}\to a_{\text{IR}}-\text{PD}(\Sigma)
we find:

S[\Sigma]=S+\frac{2\pi i}{N^{2}}\int_{\partial X_{5}}\text{PD}(\gamma)\,a_{\text{IR}}\,.

(V.43)

Which corresponds to the insertion of an emergent symmetry defect
\pi_{\text{IR}}(\widetilde{\bm{Q}}_{1}^{k})=\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}_{1}^{k}
along
\gamma
.
This result is consistent with the symmetry fractionalization of backgrounds:

A_{1}^{(\text{IR})}=A_{1}^{(\text{UV})}\,,\quad B_{3}^{(\text{IR})}=kA_{1}^{(\text{UV})}\cup\beta(A_{1}^{(\text{UV})})\,,

(V.44)

and we have recovered it geometrically in the SymTFT.

RG-Quiche from Gauge Fields.
We conclude by presenting an alternative perspective on the RG-quiche interface using the bulk action.
The SymTFT actions in the UV and IR are, respectively:

\displaystyle S_{\text{UV}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi i}{N}\int a_{\text{UV}}\cup db_{\text{UV}}+\frac{k}{N}a_{\text{UV}}\cup\beta(a_{\text{UV}})^{2}\,,

(V.45)

\displaystyle S_{\text{IR}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi i}{N}\int a^{\prime}\cup d\widetilde{b}+\widetilde{a}\cup db^{\prime}+\frac{2\pi i}{N^{2}}\int a^{\prime}\cup db^{\prime}\,,

where we have decomposed the IR gauge fields in
a_{\text{IR}}=a^{\prime}+N\widetilde{a}
and
b_{\text{IR}}=b^{\prime}+N\widetilde{b}
, respectively. We now consider an interface
\mathcal{I}_{I}
between the two theories. Gauge invariance requires:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi i}{N}\int_{\mathcal{I}_{I}}\biggl(d\lambda^{\prime}\cup\left[\widetilde{b}+\frac{1}{N}b^{\prime}\right]+d\widetilde{\lambda}\cup b^{\prime}\biggr.

(V.46)

\displaystyle\biggr.\qquad-d\lambda_{\text{UV}}\cup\left[b_{\text{UV}}+\frac{k}{N}a_{\text{UV}}\cup\beta(a_{\text{UV}})\right]\biggl)\,.

To cancel the gauge variation we identify
a^{\prime}=a_{\text{UV}}
at the interface, together with:

\widetilde{b}=b_{\text{UV}}\,,\quad b^{\prime}=ka_{\text{UV}}\cup\beta(a_{\text{UV}})\,,

(V.47)

furthermore
\widetilde{a}
remains dynamical on the interface, and the gauge variation is compensated by an interface term
\widetilde{a}\cup b^{\prime}
. This corresponds to a Neumann boundary condition for
\widetilde{a}
.
A UV symmetry defect:

\bm{Q}_{3}^{(n_{m},0)}=\exp\left(\frac{2\pi in_{m}}{N}\int_{\Sigma}b_{\text{UV}}\right)\,,

(V.48)

is mapped into:
9
9

9

We write
\frac{1}{N}\widetilde{b}
as
\frac{1}{N^{2}}(b^{\prime}+N\widetilde{b})-\frac{1}{N^{2}}b^{\prime}
and use the gluing conditions.

\displaystyle\phi\left(\bm{Q}_{3}^{(n_{m},0)}\right)=

\displaystyle\exp\left(\frac{2\pi in_{m}}{N^{2}}\int b_{\text{IR}}-kNa_{\text{IR}}\cup\beta(a_{\text{IR}})\right)

(V.49)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{Q}}_{3}^{(n_{m},-kNn_{m})}\,,

matching our previous discussion. Notice that, above,
\beta(a_{\text{IR}})=da_{\text{IR}}/N^{2}
is the IR Bockstein map.
The IR boundary conditions are simply Dirichlet boundary conditions for
a^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime}
. The matching equation (
III.13
) requires that we should impose the same gapped boundary condition by first acting with
\mathcal{I}_{I}
and then giving Dirichlet boundary condition for
a_{\text{UV}}
. This follows immediately from our discussion above.

The symmetry fractionalization can also be seen right away. Consider equation (
V.47
) in the presence of the
\mathcal{L}_{\text{IR}}
boundary conditions
a^{\prime}=A_{1}^{(\text{UV})}
,
b^{\prime}=B_{3}^{(\text{IR})}
, it reads:

B_{3}^{(\text{IR})}=kA_{1}^{(\text{UV})}\cup\beta(A_{1}^{(\text{UV})})\,,

(V.50)

implementing anomaly matching via the fractionalization of the emergent two-form symmetry.

This example shows clearly how the related phenomena of symmetry transmutation and symmetry fractionalization are described in our general framework of SymTFT interfaces.

V.3
SETs and Relation to Wang-Wen-Witten

In section
V.2
we considered an anomalous
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}=G^{(0),\omega}
and constructed an embedding into
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
, that contains a non-anomalous
G_{\text{UV}}^{(0),\omega=0}
sub-symmetry.

In this section we will furthermore trivialize the symmetry
G^{(0),\omega=0}
. Specifically, we will consider a symmetric phase for this non-anomalous sub-symmetry. In this phase, the higher-form symmetry will be broken, corresponding to a
symmetry-enriched topological order (SETO)
.

V.3.1
General Analysis Starting with
G_{\text{UV}}^{(0),\omega}

We will now discuss IR gapped phases preserving the non-anomalous 0-form symmetry, in other words the Wang-Wen-Witten construction
Wang:2017loc
.
Building on the construction of the RG-quiche in the last subsection, we construct symmetry preserving gapped phases for
G
by choosing suitable (gapped) physical boundary conditions in
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}})
.
Requiring the 0-form symmetry
G^{(0)}
to be unbroken in such a phase will give rise to a SETO – namely a nontrivial topological order.

We start our analysis with the setup in section
V.2
, where
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
includes

G^{(0),\omega=0}\times^{\nu}\widehat{K}^{(d-2)}\,,

(V.51)

as well as the theta defects and condensates constructed out of
\widehat{K}^{(d-2)}
.
The mixed anomaly
\nu
follows from the extension

1\rightarrow K\rightarrow\widetilde{G}\xrightarrow{p}G\rightarrow 1\,.

(V.52)

Recall that
K
is abelian.
Notice, that according to our SymTFT criterion in section
III.3.4
,
G^{(0),\omega=0}\subset\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
is an anomaly-free but non-normal subcategory.
Hence, a
G^{(0)}
-symmetric gapped phase necessarily requires the spontaneous breaking of further emergent symmetries.

To study the symmetric gapped phase, we close the SymTFT sandwich and insert a gapped physical boundary
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{phys}}
:

\begin{split}\hbox to186.15pt{\vbox to115.92pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 14.82637pt\lower-15.19933pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{92.51653pt}{39.48969pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{80.35132pt}{90.39128pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{split}

(V.53)

We would like to make the sub-symmetry
G^{(0),\omega=0}\subset\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
act trivially in this phase. To this end, we need
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{phys}}
to be a magnetic boundary condition with respect to
\widetilde{G}
, i.e. condensing the fluxes (labeled by conjugacy classes of
\widetilde{G}
)
10
10

10

For a non-abelian
G
, genuine elements of the center are labeled by conjugacy classes
\bm{Q}_{3}^{[g]}
and irreducible representations
\bm{Q}_{1}^{\rho}
. The full categorical structure is encoded in
\mathcal{Z}(n\mathsf{Vec}_{G})=\displaystyle\boxplus_{[g]\in G}n\mathsf{Rep}(H_{g})
, where
H_{g}
is the centralizer of
[g]
. This structure generalizes the
H^{3}(G,U(1))
labels
l
we have encountered in the previous discussion. We will not need these subtleties in what follows.
:

\mathcal{L}_{\text{phys}}=\mathcal{L}_{\text{mag},\widetilde{G}^{(0)}}=\bigoplus_{\widetilde{g}\in\widetilde{G}}\widetilde{\bm{Q}}_{d-1}^{[\widetilde{g}]}\,.

(V.54)

With this choice, the UV
G_{\text{UV}}^{(0),\omega}
-symmetry generators
\bm{Q}_{d-1}^{[g]}
,
g\in G
, can cross the interface and end on
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{phys}}
. On the other hand the defects
\widetilde{\bm{Q}}_{d-1}^{k}
,
k\in K
, can end both on the physical and
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{IR}}
boundaries:

\begin{split}\hbox to229.22pt{\vbox to115.92pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 15.02638pt\lower-15.19933pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
