(V.55)

The boundary of
\widetilde{\bm{Q}}_{d-1}^{k}
, for
k\in K
, produces a
(d-2)
-dimensional topological defect

\mathsf{U}_{d-2}^{k}=\partial\widetilde{\bm{Q}}_{d-1}^{k}\,,\quad k\in K\,,

(V.56)

which generates an
emergent
1
-form symmetry

K^{(1)}
in the gapped phase. The full symmetry
\mathcal{D}_{\text{IR}}
of this phase does not have 0-form symmetries acting on local operators (it is a
G^{(0)}
-preserving phase) but a 1-form symmetry and
(d-2)
-form symmetry

\mathcal{D}_{\text{IR}}\supset K^{(1)}\times\widehat{K}^{(d-2)}\,,

(V.57)

with a mixed anomaly

S^{(\text{IR})}_{\text{inflow}}=2\pi i\int_{X_{d+1}}C_{2}^{(\text{IR})}\cup B_{d-1}^{(\text{IR})}

(V.58)

with
C_{2}^{(\text{IR})}\in H^{2}(X,\widehat{K})
,
B_{d-1}^{(\text{IR})}\in H^{d-1}(X,K)
.
The IR phase is a
Symmetry Enriched Topological Order (SETO)
.
The effective field theory for this phase is the standard, untwisted,
(d+1)
-dimensional Dijkgraaf-Witten theory
Dijkgraaf:1989pz
;
Kapustin:2014gua
with gauge group
K
. As we will see very explicitly in an example below, the UV symmetry enforces that this theory is enriched with a symmetry
G
, in the sense that a background field
A_{1}^{(\text{UV})}
for
G
activates backgrounds for
K^{(1)}
and
\widehat{K}^{(d-2)}
. This matches the anomaly without breaking the symmetry, and reproduces the general mechanism due to Wang-Wen-Witten
Wang:2017loc
.

V.3.2
Example:
(3+1)
d with
G_{\text{UV}}^{(0),\omega}=\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{(0),k}

Let us concretely consider again
(3+1)
d, with
G_{\text{UV}}^{(0),\omega}=\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{(0),k}
and the anomaly (
V.23
).
The physical boundary condition that makes the
G^{(0),\omega=0}\subset\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
act trivially is the magnetic boundary condition with respect to
\widetilde{G}={\mathbb{Z}}_{N^{2}}
,

\mathcal{L}_{\text{phys}}=\bigoplus_{\widetilde{n}_{m}\in\widetilde{G}={\mathbb{Z}}_{N^{2}}}\widetilde{\bm{Q}}_{3}^{(\widetilde{n}_{m},0)}\,.

(V.59)

The boundary of
\widetilde{\bm{Q}}_{3}^{(\widetilde{n}_{m}\in K,0)}
produces a 2-dimensional topological defect

\mathsf{U}_{2}^{(\widetilde{n}_{m})}=\partial\widetilde{\bm{Q}}_{3}^{(\widetilde{n}_{m},0)}\ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ \widetilde{n}_{m}\in K

(V.60)

living on the boundary, that generates the
emergent 1-form symmetry

K^{(1)}
in the gapped phase. The symmetry of this phase is
\mathcal{D}_{\text{IR}}=K^{(1)}\times\widehat{K}^{(2)}

with a mixed anomaly

S^{(\text{IR})}_{\text{inflow}}=2\pi i\int_{X_{5}}C_{2}^{(\text{IR})}\cup B_{3}^{(\text{IR})}

(V.61)

with
B_{3}^{(\text{IR})}\in H^{3}(X,\widehat{K})
,
C_{2}^{(\text{IR})}\in H^{2}(X,K)
.
The effective theory is again the untwisted,
(3+1)d
Dijkgraaf-Witten theory with gauge group
K
.

To understand how the UV anomaly is reproduced in this gapped phase, we need again to inspect how the
G_{\text{UV}}
-symmetry defects
\mathsf{D}_{3}^{(n_{m})}
are mapped in the IR, equivalently how the backgrounds
B_{3}^{(\text{IR})},C_{2}^{(\text{IR})}
are related with
A_{1}^{(\text{UV})}
. The second identification of (
V.44
) remains valid. On the other hand, as
G
is extended by
K
in
\mathcal{Z}(\widetilde{G})
, fusing
\mathsf{D}_{3}^{(n_{m})}
and
\mathsf{D}_{3}^{(n_{m}^{\prime})}
we get
\mathsf{D}_{3}^{(n_{m}+n_{m}^{\prime})}
dressed with
\widetilde{\bm{Q}}_{3}^{(\nu(n_{m},n_{m}^{\prime}),0)}
:

\mathsf{D}_{3}^{(n_{m})}
\mathsf{D}_{3}^{(n_{m}^{\prime})}
\mathsf{D}_{3}^{(n_{m}+n_{m}^{\prime})}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\times\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\widetilde{\bm{Q}}_{3}^{(\nu(n_{m},n_{m}^{\prime}),0)}

(V.62)

with
\nu\in H^{2}(BG_{\text{UV}},K)
the extension class. But
\widetilde{\bm{Q}}_{3}^{(\nu(n_{m},n_{m}^{\prime}),0)}
becomes trivial and leaves the 1-form symmetry defect
\mathsf{U}_{2}^{(\nu(n_{m},n_{m}^{\prime}))}
on its boundary, that lives at the junction:

\mathsf{D}_{3}^{(n_{m})}
\mathsf{D}_{3}^{(n_{m}^{\prime})}
\mathsf{D}_{3}^{(n_{m}+n_{m}^{\prime})}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
\mathsf{U}_{2}^{(\nu(n_{m},n_{m}^{\prime}))}

(V.63)

This is the the statement that
G_{\text{UV}}
, while does not act on local operators, has symmetry fractionalization with
K^{(1)}
controlled by the class
\nu\in H^{2}(G_{\text{UV}},K)
.
This implies that the background
A_{1}^{(\text{UV})}
activates a nontrivial background field for the emergent one-form symmetry:

C_{2}^{(\text{IR})}=A_{1}^{(\text{UV})\,*}(\nu)=\beta\left(A_{1}^{(\text{UV})}\right)\ .

(V.64)

Plugging this back into (
V.61
) together with the second equation in (
V.44
) we immediately reproduce the UV anomaly (
V.23
).

V.4
Non-Minimal Interfaces

As we have seen in the last section, the symmetry
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
, that is the target of the functor
I:G_{\text{UV}}^{(0),k}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
can be realized in a
G
-preserving gapped phase which includes an emergent one-form symmetry. If we denote the symmetry of this phase by
\mathcal{D}_{\text{IR}}
, this suggests that there must also exist a different functor

F:G_{\text{UV}}^{(0),k}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\text{IR}}\,.

(V.65)

To come full circle, we show how to describe this functor directly, without invoking the presence of the IR physical (gapped) boundary condition or the symmetry
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
.
In other words, we will construct the corresponding SymTFT interface
\mathcal{I}_{F}
between
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})
and
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{D}_{\text{IR}})
.
The construction is quite general, and makes use of recent results about
non-minimal
gapped boundary conditions for higher-dimensional topological orders
Bhardwaj:2024qiv
;
Wen:2024qsg
;
Bhardwaj:2025piv
.

The first observation is that
\mathcal{D}_{\text{IR}}
is modular: any faithfully acting topological defect braids non-trivially with at least one other such defect.
In our case the defects
\mathsf{U}_{2}^{(\widetilde{n}_{m})},\mathsf{L}_{1}^{(n_{e})}
generate
K^{(1)}
and
\widehat{K}^{(d-2)}
respectively, and have non-trivial braiding. The linking corresponds to the canonical action of
\widehat{K}
onto
K
.

If a symmetry
\mathcal{C}
is modular, then the category of genuine topological defects of its SymTFT
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C})
is trivial
Decoppet:2023uoy
. In our case:

\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{D}_{\text{IR}})=(d-1)\mathsf{Vec}\,.

(V.66)

This means that, as a TQFT,
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}})
must be an invertible theory, see e.g.
Argurio:2024oym
.

An interface
\mathcal{I}_{F}
between
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})
and an invertible TQFT is a boundary condition for the former.
This appears puzzling at first sight: to satisfy the Matching Equation (
III.13
), the boundary condition
\mathcal{I}_{F}
needs to be magnetic. However, the absence of a magnetic boundary condition is precisely the way in which a ’t Hooft anomaly for the
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
symmetry is detected via the SymTFT.
The key point is that, although
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{D}_{\text{IR}})
is invertible, it may still correspond to a nontrivial SPT phase.

The boundary condition
\mathcal{I}_{F}
can then be a
non-minimal
one
Bhardwaj:2024qiv
;
Wen:2024qsg
;
Bhardwaj:2025piv
.
To construct it we start from the canonical Dirichlet boundary condition
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{UV}}
of
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})
and stack it with a decoupled
d
-dimensional TQFT
\mathcal{T}
. The theory
\mathcal{T}
has two important properties:

1.

First, it admits a
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
action. For
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}=G^{(0),\,\omega}_{\text{UV}}
this means that it can be coupled to
G_{\text{UV}}^{(0)}
gauge fields consistently.

2.

Second, it is a gapped boundary condition for the invertible theory
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{D}_{\text{IR}})
. Thus
\mathcal{T}
is modular, and the nontrivial braiding between its objects is encoded in the SPT
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{D}_{\text{IR}})
.

This setup describes a factorized interface
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{UV}}\boxtimes\mathcal{T}
between
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})
and
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{D}_{\text{IR}})
.

Precisely when the Wang-Wen-Witten construction applies, we can furthermore choose the
G_{\text{UV}}^{(0),\,\omega}
action on
\mathcal{T}
as to cancel the ’t Hooft anomaly stemming from the Dirichlet boundary condition
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}
. We denote this theory by
\mathcal{T}^{G}
. In practice, constructing
\mathcal{T}^{G}
from
\mathcal{T}
corresponds to the choice of appropriate symmetry fractionalization classes.

This setup provides us with an anomaly-free diagonal
G_{\text{UV}}^{(0)}
symmetry action on
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{phys}}\boxtimes\mathcal{T}^{G}
, which we can then gauge. The interface
\mathcal{I}_{F}=\left(\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{UV}}\boxtimes\mathcal{T}^{G}\right)/G
is precisely the magnetic boundary condition we are after:

\begin{split}\hbox to223.13pt{\vbox to134.41pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 17.6716pt\lower-15.19933pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0.154,0.664,0.742}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -23.62 0 L -23.62 141.73 L 141.73 141.73 L 141.73 0 L -23.62 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{1,1,1}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 141.73 0 L 141.73 141.73 L 283.46 141.73 L 283.46 0 L 141.73 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{split}

(V.67)

Notice that in this picture, the IR theory does not need to be gapped. In fact the IR physical boundary can very well be gapless, and the modular symmetry
\mathcal{D}_{\text{IR}}
can be realized in a CFT. However we do not gain much out of this observation, as a modular symmetry can always be decoupled from the rest of the theory
muger2003structure
;
muger2003subfactors
;
Hsin:2018vcg
;
Cordova:2025eim
.

The lesson that we learn from this, is that in dimension
d>2
it becomes natural to consider topological interfaces that are non-minimal, in the sense explained above. Including them in the analysis of SymTFT interfaces satisfying the Matching Equation can considerably enlarge the landscape of possible UV/IR functors, making the study in
d>2
much richer and complicated, but nevertheless under control.

Example:
(3+1)
d with
G_{\text{UV}}^{(0),\omega}=\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{(0),k}
.

For concreteness, consider the construction above in the case of an anomalous
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
symmetry in
(3+1)d
. We take the
(3+1)d
TQFT
\mathcal{T}
as the
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
DW theory:

\mathcal{T}:\quad\frac{2\pi i}{N}\int_{X_{4}}x_{2}\cup dy_{1}.

(V.68)

We denote by
C_{2}
and
B_{3}
the background field for
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{(1)}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{(2)}
respectively, that act on
e^{\frac{2\pi i}{N}n_{e}\int y_{1}}
and
e^{\frac{2\pi i}{N}n_{m}\int x_{2}}
.
The corresponding center is an invertible theory, with action:

S_{\text{SymTFT}}=\frac{2\pi i}{N}\int b_{2}\cup dc_{2}+c_{1}\cup db_{3}+c_{2}\cup b_{3}\,.

(V.69)

The theory
\mathcal{T}
is simply its Dirichlet boundary condition
b_{3}=B_{3},\,c_{2}=C_{2}
.

The enrichment
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{Z}_{N}}
with the 0-form symmetry is achieved as follows. We declare that the theory has a
0
-form symmetry
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
that does not permute lines and surfaces, but such that a background field
A_{1}
activates

C_{2}=\beta(A_{1})\ ,\ \ \ \ B_{3}=-kA_{1}\cup\beta(A_{1})\ .

(V.70)

As we discussed above, the
0
-form symmetry defined in this way is anomalous. Its ’t Hooft anomaly precisely cancels the one arising from the Dirichlet boundary of
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{(0),k})
. Thus the stacking

\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{UV}}\boxtimes\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{Z}_{N}}

(V.71)

has an anomaly free diagonal
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
symmetry, which we can gauge. This produces the non-minimal interface corresponding to the functor
F:\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{(0),k}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\text{IR}}
.

V.5
Non-Invertible Generalizations

Using the mechanism described in the previous sections it is possible to discuss generalizations of the Wang-Wen-Witten mechanism in which an anomalous non-invertible symmetry is preserved in gapped phase. This provides a non-invertible analogue of the Wang-Wen-Witten construction.

As an example, we consider triality defects in (3+1)d
Choi:2022zal
. These arise when a theory
\mathcal{T}
with
\mathbb{Z}_{n}^{(1)}
1-form symmetry is self-dual under the operation
ST
:

Z_{\mathcal{T}}[B_{2}]=\sum_{b_{2}^{\prime}}\exp{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{n}\int_{X_{4}}B_{2}\cup b_{2}^{\prime}+\frac{\mathfrak{P}(b_{2}^{\prime})}{2}\right)}\,Z_{\mathcal{T}}[b_{2}^{\prime}]

(V.72)

corresponding to gauging with discrete torsion. Above
\mathfrak{P}:H^{2}(X_{4},\mathbb{Z}_{n})\to H^{4}(X_{4},\mathbb{Z}_{\gcd(2,n)n})
is the Pontryagin square operation.
The relevant data characterizing the triality symmetry have been discussed in the literature. On top of the 1-form symmetry group
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
, they are
Antinucci:2023ezl
;
Cordova:2023bja
:

1.

A non-degenerate symmetric bicharacter on the 1-form symmetry. In the
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
under consideration this is a number
p
coprime with
n
. We will take
p=1
without loss of generality.

2.

A generalized Frobenius-Schur indicator labeled by
\epsilon\in H^{5}(\mathbb{Z}_{3},U(1))

=\mathbb{Z}_{3}
, describing a cubic anomaly for the triality defect.

This category can be referred to as a generalized 3-Tambara-Yamagami category

\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}=3\mathsf{TY}^{{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}}({\mathbb{Z}}_{n}^{(1)},\chi,\epsilon)\,,

(V.73)

where the superscript
{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}
indicates that we are considering triality
{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}
.
We take this categorical symmetry as our
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
. The SymTFT
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})
has been constructed in
Kaidi:2022cpf
;
Antinucci:2022vyk
;
Antinucci:2023ezl
;
Cordova:2023bja
;
Bhardwaj:2024xcx
. It can be described starting with the SymTFT for the 1-form symmetry
\mathbb{Z}_{n}^{(1)}
alone, namely the (4+1)d 1-form
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
Dijkgraaf-Witten theory

S=\frac{2\pi i}{n}\int_{X_{5}}b_{2}\cup dc_{2}\,,

(V.74)

and then gauging the
\mathbb{Z}_{3}^{(0)}
triality symmetry

\mathsf{ST}:\ \ \ b_{2}\mapsto-b_{2}-c_{2}\ ,\ \ \ \ \ c_{2}\mapsto b_{2}\,,

(V.75)

while adding a discrete torsion
\tau\in H^{5}(B\mathbb{Z}_{3},U(1))
in the bulk, with
\tau=\epsilon
.

The symmetry
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
can be anomalous (lacks a fiber functor), depending on
n
and
\epsilon\in\mathbb{Z}_{3}
. Again, as in the case of standard TY categories, the obstruction theory to a fiber functor stems follows a two-level structure
Antinucci:2023ezl
;
Cordova:2023bja
:

1.

First obstruction:
If the original Dijkgraaf-Witten theory (
V.74
) does not have any
\mathbb{Z}_{3}^{(0)}
-invariant topological boundary condition
\mathfrak{B}_{\text{invariant}}
, then there is no triality-invariant SPT phase for the one-form symmetry. This obstruction cancels only if the equation
r^{2}+r+1\equiv 0\pmod{n}
admits a solution
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
. A triality symmetry with a first obstruction anomaly is either spontaneously broken, or must be realized in a gapless phase
Apte:2022xtu
;
Damia:2023ses
.

2.

Second obstruction:
When the first obstruction vanishes, we still have a second obstruction anomaly controlled by
\epsilon
. In the case that we will discuss the second obstruction only vanishes if
\epsilon\equiv 0\mod 3
.
11
11

11

More generally, symmetry fractionalization can cancel some anomalies. The condition then takes the form
\epsilon+Y\neq 0
for all allowed
Y\in\mathbb{Z}_{3}
computed in
Antinucci:2023ezl
.

\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(1)}}
trivial
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(1)})
\text{trivial }\mathbb{Z}_{3}^{(0)}\text{ SPT}
\mathfrak{B}_{\text{invariant}}
\leadsto
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{UV}}
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{3}}
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})
\mathfrak{Z}(3\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{3}}^{\epsilon=1})
\mathcal{I}_{P}

Figure 11:
Top figure: the triality invariant boundary
\mathfrak{B}_{\text{invariant}}
separates the 1-form SymTFT
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(1)})
, enriched with
\mathbb{Z}_{3}^{(0)}
from a trivial
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
-SPT. Gauging
\mathbb{Z}_{3}^{(0)}
with discrete torsion
\epsilon=1
produces an interface
\mathcal{I}_{P}
between
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})
and
\mathfrak{Z}(3\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{3}}^{1})
.

We want to show that if the first obstruction anomaly vanishes but the second does not, the non-invertible triality symmetry can be realized in a SET phase that does not break it.

We focus on the simplest example
n=2
,
\epsilon=1
. The first obstruction anomaly vanishes for
r=1
, while we have a second obstruction anomaly. The key observation is that
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
is not an ASCy: the 1-form symmetry
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(1)}
is a normal subcategory that is the kernel of a surjective functor

P:\ \mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\ \rightarrow\ 3\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{3}}^{(0),\epsilon=1}\ .

(V.76)

The SymTFT interface associated with
P
is built as follows.
We start from the
\mathbb{Z}_{3}^{(0)}
-invariant gapped boundary condition
\mathfrak{B}_{\text{invariant}}
of (
V.74
), corresponding to
r=1
and to the condensation of the
(n_{e},n_{m})=(1,1)
dyonic surface.

This boundary condition can be viewed as an interface between a
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
-enriched
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(1)})
and an invertible
\mathbb{Z}_{3}^{(0)}
-symmetric topological order. As the whole setup is triality-invariant, we then gauge
\mathbb{Z}_{3}^{(0)}
with discrete torsion
\epsilon=1
throughout. On the left, this yields the SymTFT
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})
for triality symmetry; on the right, it gives the gauged
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
SPT with
\epsilon=1
, i.e., the twisted (4+1)d
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
DW theory discussed in section
V.2
, which is the SymTFT for
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
with anomaly 1.
The interface between them is
\mathcal{I}_{P}
, as shown in figure
11
, while the boundary condition
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{3}}
of
\mathfrak{Z}(3\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{3}})
is the canonical Dirichlet boundary with
\mathbb{Z}_{3}^{(0)}
symmetry.

The important fact is that
\mathfrak{B}_{\text{invariant}}
is a magnetic boundary condition of
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(1)})
with respect to
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(1)}}
, as it corresponds to the condensation of dyonic surfaces.
Therefore
\mathcal{I}_{P}

defines a surjective functor
P:\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\rightarrow 3\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{3}}^{\epsilon=1}
.

After we apply the functor
P
we are precisely in the same situation as at the beginning of section
V.2
: we have a
\mathbb{Z}_{3}^{(0)}
with anomaly
1
. Following the construction of section
V.3
, and its reformulation in terms of a single functor as in section
V.4
, we can further apply the invertible Wang-Wen-Witten construction:

\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{UV}}
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{3}}
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{D}_{\text{IR}}}
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})
\mathfrak{Z}(3\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{3}}^{\epsilon=1})
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{D}_{\text{IR}})
\mathcal{I}_{P}
\mathcal{I}_{F}

(V.77)

The composition

P^{\prime}=F\circ P:\quad\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}^{\prime}\supset\left(\mathbb{Z}_{3}^{(1)}\times\mathbb{Z}_{3}^{(2)}\right)^{\text{anomaly}}

(V.78)

describes the realization of the anomalous non-invertible triality symmetry in a symmetry preserving gapped phase, in the same spirit as Wang-Wen-Witten
Wang:2017loc
.

VI
Symmetry Enriched SymTFT and LSM Anomaly-Matching

As a final application of our construction, let us discuss how discrete spacetime symmetries – e.g. lattice translations – can be matched in the continuum by emanant global symmetries. We focus on the IR matching of Lieb-Schultz-Mattis (LSM) anomalies
Lieb:1961fr
;
Affleck:1986pq
in the continuum limit by ’t Hooft anomalies for (emanant) global symmetries
Cheng:2022sgb
and the constraints that the matching conditions impose on their structure.

In this section, we define an LSM anomaly as an obstruction to the realization of a symmetric, translationally invariant trivially-gapped phase on the lattice.
The natural setup is that of a lattice system with a symmetry
\mathcal{C}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{T}
, where
\mathbb{Z}_{T}
are the lattice translations and
\mathcal{C}
is an anomaly-free symmetry, in the sense that it admits a fiber functor.
12
12

12

For simplicity, we will work in the infinite chain, so that the translation group is formally
\mathbb{Z}
. This is inconsequential to our conclusions, as they concern the continuum limit.

The notation
\mathcal{C}\rtimes\mathcal{G}
stands for a
\mathcal{G}
-crossed extension of
\mathcal{C}
, called a crossed product (whose definition can be found in appendix
A.5
), that is, the extension of a fusion category
\mathcal{C}
by the action of an outer automorphism group
\mathcal{G}
. This encodes the fact that the translation symmetry acts nontrivially on the flavor symmetry, although the action is not necessarily a semidirect product of groups.
The LSM anomaly can then be interpreted as a sort of mixed ’t Hooft anomaly between lattice translations
T
and the global symmetry
\mathcal{C}
of the UV lattice system
Cheng:2015kce
;
Seifnashri:2023dpa
.
It can be shown
Seifnashri:2023dpa
in
(1+1)
d systems, that for
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Vec}_{G}
the LSM anomaly takes values in
H^{2}(G,U(1))
, which also describes bosonic SPT for the
G
symmetry.
This resonates with the crystalline equivalence principle
Thorngren:2016hdm
, according to which we would describe lattice translations as a
\mathbb{Z}
background gauge field
A_{T}
, and the LSM anomaly becomes:

\omega_{\text{LSM}}=2\pi i\int A_{T}\cup\omega(A)\,,\ \ \ \omega\,\in H^{2}(G,U(1))\,.

(VI.1)

In this setup, the twisting by lattice translations provides a map between
G
-SPTs resulting in the LSM anomaly. This perspective will be useful in generalizing our construction to non-invertible symmetries.

In the thermodynamic limit lattice translations act trivially on the low lying states and it is natural to expect that the LSM obstruction should be matched by an internal ’t Hooft anomaly for an emergent symmetry.
In such a case, we say
Cheng:2022sgb
that the global symmetry “emanates” from lattice translations and is consistent with our proposed picture of ’t Hooft anomaly matching between UV and IR (see also
Seiberg:2025bqy
).

In this section we use our SymTFT picture for UV/IR anomaly matching, together with the recent proposal
Pace:2024acq
;
Pace:2025hpb
for the description of (1+1)d lattice translations (and other spacetime symmetries) via a
Symmetry Enrichment
of the SymTFT construction (also known as a Symmetry Enriched Topological Order – SETO – in the Condensed Matter community), to derive an exact categorical sequence describing the LSM anomaly matching.
Our construction is conceptually simple, and gives a potent tool generalizing LSM anomaly matching to previously unknown setups involving non-invertible global symmetries, of which we provide the simplest example. We also expect many interesting examples to arise in higher dimensional setups, but do not analyze them in this work.

VI.1
LSM Anomaly from SESymTFT

We will start by reviewing the construction of the Symmetry-Enriched SymTFT (SESymTFT) describing lattice spacetime symmetries proposed in
Pace:2024acq
;
Pace:2025hpb
.
We will focus for concreteness on enrichment by lattice translations, and encourage the reader to consult
Pace:2024acq
;
Pace:2025hpb
for a more thorough presentation. Generalizations to orientation reversal symmetries should be possible.
We caution the reader that the construction of
Pace:2024acq
;
Pace:2025hpb
differs in spirit from the standard SymTFT mantra: the lattice translation symmetry will not be gauged in the bulk, as this is not a natural operation to be performed on a lattice system, but it will rather be implemented as a background structure encoding its interplay with the remaining global symmetry.

As we will be dealing with
(2+1)
d non-isotropic – in this case foliated – systems, we will denote by
x,t
the boundary coordinates and by
y
the internal SymTFT direction.
The enriched SymTFT for lattice translations is a foliated theory, which means it is non-trivially coupled to a foliation structure
\varphi
along the
x
direction:

\varphi=a^{-1}dx\,,

(VI.2)

where
a
is the lattice spacing. The foliation will act as a type of background gauge field for the lattice translations, allowing the enrichment of the SymTFT at the cost of a mild loss of topological invariance.

The construction starts from an un-enriched theory
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C})
, describing the global (internal or flavor) symmetry of the lattice system. As remarked before, we will take
\mathcal{C}
to be anomaly-free. The
\mathcal{C}
symmetry thus admits several inequivalent fiber functors,
f_{i}
, describing the possible
\mathcal{C}
-symmetric SPTs. As a matter of notation, we will take the trivially gapped phase to be one of these SPTs.

In order to have a non-trivial enrichment by lattice translations, we will further take
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C})
to have nontrivial braided outer automorphisms,
\mathcal{U}\in\Aut\left(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C})\right)
. These describe the action of 0-form symmetries on the anyonic content of the SymTFT.
The insight of
Pace:2024acq
;
Pace:2025hpb
is that the LSM anomaly can be described by appropriately coupling the foliation structure
\varphi
to an appropriately chosen
\mathcal{U}
.
We will denote the associated enriched SymTFT by

\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C})^{\mathcal{U}T}\,,

(VI.3)

and will use the notation
\mathcal{U}T
to remind us of the choice of bulk automorphism which twists the translation symmetry.

Coupling the SymTFT to a nontrivial background foliation has the effect of enriching the SymTFT with an array of
\mathcal{U}
defects, stretching in the
(t,y)
directions:

(t,x)
y
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C})
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}
\leadsto
\mathcal{U}T
\mathcal{U}T
\mathcal{U}T
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C})^{\mathcal{U}T}
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}

(VI.4)

Importantly, for this construction to be well-defined, we will need the symmetry boundary condition to be invariant under the action of
\mathcal{U}
:

\mathcal{U}\times\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}=\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}\,.

(VI.5)

In this case,
\mathcal{U}
descends to an automorphism of the global symmetry
\mathcal{C}
itself, and comes with a well-defined action on its structures, notably the space of
\mathcal{C}
-symmetric gapped phases
\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathcal{C}}
.

LSM Anomaly.

We are now ready to discuss how the LSM anomaly arises in this setup. As
\mathcal{C}
is anomaly-free, it can be trivially realized via (several) distinct SPTs, which we label by their fiber functors
f_{i}
,
i=1,...,M
.
These are in one-to-one correspondence with magnetic Lagrangian algebras,
\mathcal{L}_{i}
in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C})
with
\mathcal{L}_{i}\cap\mathcal{L}_{\text{sym}}=\{1\}
. The SymTFT sandwich between
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{phys}}
and
\mathfrak{B}_{\mathcal{L}_{i}}
describes the
i
-th SPT phase.
The bulk automorphism
\mathcal{U}
acts on the
\mathcal{L}_{i}
by a permutation
\sigma
as the number of ground states is preserved by the
\mathcal{U}
action using (
VI.5
):

\mathcal{U}\left(\mathcal{L}_{i}\right)=\mathcal{L}_{\sigma(i)}\,,\ \ \ \sigma\in S_{M}\,.

(VI.6)

Consequently, in the enriched setup, a translationally-invariant SPT corresponds to a fixed point
i^{*}
under
\sigma
:
\sigma(i^{*})=i^{*}
. Thus we find:

The symmetry
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{\mathcal{U}T}
has an LSM anomaly if no magnetic Lagrangian algebra (i.e. SPT) is fixed under the
\mathcal{U}
action.

Let us give a clarifying example.

Example
(\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{T}
.
To see the construction explicitly at work (and set the stage for our subsequent applications) let us consider
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
.
We will take
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}
to be described by electric condensation:

\mathcal{L}_{\text{sym}}=1\oplus e_{1}\oplus e_{2}\oplus e_{1}e_{2}\,.

(VI.7)

The
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
theory has two trivially gapped phases – the trivial one and the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
SPT – described by the Lagrangian algebras:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{triv}}

\displaystyle=1\oplus m_{1}\oplus m_{2}\oplus m_{1}m_{2}

(VI.8)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{SPT}}

\displaystyle=1\oplus e_{1}m_{2}\oplus e_{2}m_{1}\oplus e_{1}e_{2}m_{1}m_{2}\,.

The symmetry boundary condition
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}
is left invariant under the following automorphism
\mathcal{U}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}(e_{1})=e_{1}\,,\ \

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}(e_{2})=e_{2}\,,

(VI.9)

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}(m_{1})=m_{1}e_{2}\,,\ \

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}(m_{2})=m_{2}e_{1}\,.

As
\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{L}_{\text{triv}})=\mathcal{L}_{\text{SPT}}
,
\mathcal{U}
exchanges the trivial and SPT phases:

\hbox to113.72pt{\vbox to114.74pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 13.48236pt\lower-13.69933pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}

(VI.10)

This leads to an LSM anomaly for the combined symmetry, as no
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
SPT is lattice-translation invariant. The
\mathcal{U}
automorphism of
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}})
can also be described explicitly as:

\mathcal{U}=\exp\left(\pi i\int a_{1}\,a_{2}\right)\,,

(VI.11)

where
a_{1},\,a_{2}
are the electric gauge fields of the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
untwisted DW theory. Coupling
\mathcal{U}
to the foliation structure simply leads to the following action for the SymTFT:

S=\pi i\int\left(\sum_{i=1,2}a_{i}db_{i}+\varphi\cup a_{1}\cup a_{2}\right)\,.

(VI.12)

VI.2
Categorical Exact Sequences and LSM Anomaly-Matching

We will now move on to the description of the exact sequence encoding the continuum LSM anomaly matching by an emanant symmetry using the SymTFT.
We will focus on examples in which
\mathcal{U}
is a cyclic automorphism of order
N
, namely
\mathcal{U}^{N}=1
for some
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
13
13

13

The structure of the automorphisms of a given topological order has been studied in detail in several works
Kapustin:2010if
;
Fuchs:2012dt
;
Barkeshli:2014cna
;
Roumpedakis:2022aik
;
Buican:2023bzl

Our aim is to construct a categorical exact sequence of the form:

N\mathbb{Z}_{T}\overset{I}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{\mathcal{U}T}\overset{P}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}\,,

(VI.13)

where
\mathbb{Z}_{T}
stands for lattice translations and
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
is the IR symmetry matching the LSM anomaly. The
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
symmetry is generally larger than the
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
symmetry, due to the presence of an emanant subsymmetry.
Graphically, this corresponds to an enriched RG-quiche:

T^{N}
T^{N}
T^{N}
\mathcal{U}T
\mathcal{U}T
\mathcal{U}T
N\mathbb{Z}_{T}
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{T}
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathsf{Vec})^{T^{N}}
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})^{\mathcal{U}T}
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{IR})

(VI.14)

The leftmost part is a trivial yet enriched topological order, it describes the subgroup of lattice translations which act trivially on the continuum Hilbert space, and thus are in the kernel of the UV/IR map.
The rightmost segment instead describes the IR continuum symmetry, where the LSM anomaly for
\mathcal{U}T
is matched by an emanant symmetry, so generically
\text{dim}(\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}))<\text{dim}(\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}))
.
This is not in contradiction with the fact that the total quantum dimension should
decrease
along a surjective RG-flow, as the UV dimension should be taken to be the one of the enriched SymTFT, which is formally infinite.
In order to describe the sequence we must provide the appropriate interfaces between the three topological orders.

We start with the description of the left interface. As the corresponding center is
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec})=\mathsf{Vec}
the interface must correspond to the condensation of a Lagrangian algebra in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})
. Condition (
III.13
) forces this to be
\mathcal{L}_{\text{UV}}
, the Lagrangian algebra corresponding to the UV symmetry boundary:

T^{N}
T^{N}
T^{N}
\mathcal{U}T
\mathcal{U}T
\mathcal{U}T
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{UV}}
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathsf{Vec})^{T^{N}}
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})^{\mathcal{U}T}
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{UV}}

(VI.15)

Notice that this is compatible with the trivialization of the SymTFT enrichment by
\mathcal{U}T
, as
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{UV}}
must be
\mathcal{U}
-invariant by (
VI.5
).

The rightmost interface instead implements a map between an enriched topological order and a standard one. The natural physical mechanism implementing for such a map corresponds to gauging the 0-form symmetry
\mathcal{G}
generated by
\mathcal{U}
.
In mathematical terms, the SESymTFT is described by a
\mathcal{G}
-crossed braided tensor category
Barkeshli:2014cna
and the gauging procedure corresponds to equivariantization. We refer the reader to
Barkeshli:2014cna
for an in depth review. For our current purposes we will only require some of the results summarized in appendix
A.5
.
As the
\mathcal{G}
automorphism group leaves
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}
invariant, it corresponds to an automorphism of
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
itself. In these cases it is known, see
2009arXiv0905.3117G
, that the center after gauging the 0-form symmetry is simply:

\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}})=\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\rtimes\mathcal{G})\,.

(VI.16)

Alternatively, the interface
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{G}}
can be described starting from
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\rtimes\mathcal{G})
and gauging the dual
\mathsf{Rep}(\mathcal{G})
symmetry.

As the starting symmetry boundary is
\mathcal{G}
-invariant, it is naturally mapped to a corresponding boundary condition in
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\rtimes\mathcal{G})
, described by enhancing the symmetric algebra
\mathcal{L}_{\text{UV}}
by the generators of the dual symmetry
\mathsf{Rep}(\mathcal{G})
. This boundary condition encodes a
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\rtimes\mathcal{G}
symmetry, of which the
\mathcal{G}
subgroup is emanant.

Also in this case the Matching Equation is satisfied, as the action of the
\mathcal{G}
-gauging interface on the invariant algebra
\mathcal{L}_{\text{UV}}

provides the dressing by the
\mathsf{Rep}(\mathcal{G})
generators.
The final quiche is thus:

\mathcal{U}T
\mathcal{U}T
\mathcal{U}T
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{G}}
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{T}
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\rtimes\mathcal{G}
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})^{\mathcal{U}T}
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{UV}\rtimes\mathcal{G})

(VI.17)

In the UV, the enrichment by the lattice translation symmetry was implemented by the twist defect
Z^{\mathcal{U}}
of the
\mathcal{U}
symmetry. After gauging the
\mathcal{G}
symmetry the twist defects are genuine and become Gukov-Witten defects for the
\mathsf{Rep}(\mathcal{G})
symmetry.
As they carry
\mathsf{Rep}(\mathcal{G})
charge, they survive as nontrivial topological
\mathcal{G}
defects on the
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\rtimes\mathcal{G}
boundary condition, describing the action of the emanant symmetry
z\in\mathcal{G}
on the continuum degrees of freedom:

\hbox to113.55pt{\vbox to116.24pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-28.58498pt\lower-15.27827pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}

VI.3
Invertible and Non-Invertible Examples

The construction up to this point has been rather abstract. Let us conclude by providing two key examples: the simplest LSM anomaly matching involving
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}=\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
and the simplest non-invertible example:
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}=\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8})
twisted by its triality automorphism.
As far as we are aware this is a novel result
14
14

14

The LSM anomaly of an
\mathsf{Rep}(G)\times Z(G)
–
Z
here being the standard center of the group
G
– has instead been discussed in
Pace:2024acq
.
.

LSM Anomaly Matching:
(\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{T}\to(\mathbb{Z}_{2})^{3}_{\text{III}}
.

We start with

\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}=\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\,.

(VI.19)

We can now go back to our example of the LSM anomaly (
VI.10
) and our discussion of it.
The description of the normal subcategory is obvious and follows our prescription verbatim.

To describe the target category of the surjective functor, i.e. the IR symmetry, we perform the condensation of the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
automorphism
\mathcal{U}
.
The gauging of a bulk 0-form symmetry is known to lead to the center of a Tambara-Yamagami category
Kaidi:2022cpf
;
Antinucci:2022vyk
;
Antinucci:2023ezl
,
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2},\chi,\epsilon))
. The choice of
\chi
can be read off from the action of the duality symmetry on electric and magnetic lines
Antinucci:2023ezl
, while the FS indicator is described by gauging twisted by the discrete torsion
\epsilon\,\in\,H^{3}(\mathbb{Z}_{2},U(1))
.
We first perform the change of basis:

\displaystyle e^{\prime}_{1}=e_{1}m_{2}\,,\ \

\displaystyle e^{\prime}_{2}=e_{2}m_{1}\,,

(VI.20)

\displaystyle m^{\prime}_{1}=m_{1}\,,\ \

\displaystyle m^{\prime}_{2}=m_{2}\,,

so that the duality action takes the form:

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}(e^{\prime}_{1})=m^{\prime}_{2}\,,\ \

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}(e^{\prime}_{2})=m^{\prime}_{1}\,,

(VI.21)

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}(m^{\prime}_{1})=e^{\prime}_{2}\,,\ \

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}(m^{\prime}_{2})=e^{\prime}_{1}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}(e^{\prime}_{1}m^{\prime}_{2})=e^{\prime}_{1}m^{\prime}_{2}\,,\ \

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}(e^{\prime}_{2}m^{\prime}_{1})=e^{\prime}_{2}m^{\prime}_{1}\,.

This corresponds to the off-diagonal bicharacter
\chi_{o}
, leading to
\mathsf{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2},\chi_{o},+)=\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8})
. The automorphism action on the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
group is trivial on objects, but nontrivial on their three-valent junctions. Indeed the center of
\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8})
is the same as the one for the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{3}
group with a type-III anomaly:

\omega(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})=\exp\left(i\pi\int A_{1}A_{2}A_{3}\right)\,.

(VI.22)

This anomaly correctly encodes the twisted action of
\mathbb{Z}_{T}
on
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, and we recover
the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-crossed braided extension of the center in the IR.

We make this more precise by studying the induced boundary condition in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8}))
. The anyon content is given in Table
3

;

Table 3:
Spins and quantum dimensions of the simple lines of
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8}))
.

The duality-invariant Lagrangian algebra (
VI.7
) is lifted into:

\mathcal{L}_{\text{sym}}^{\text{IR}}=1\oplus e_{R}\oplus e_{G}\oplus e_{B}\oplus e_{RG}\oplus e_{RB}\oplus e_{BG}\oplus e_{RGB}\,.

(VI.23)

We can check explicitly that this is giving a tensor functor. The only lines which can traverse the interface are those that are uncharged under the dual
\mathsf{Rep}(\mathbb{Z}_{2})
, generated by
e_{RGB}
and must be duality-invariant in order to end on
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{UV}}
. These are exactly
1,e_{R},e_{G},e_{B}
and their image under fusion with
e_{RGB}
:

\mathcal{U}T
\mathcal{U}T
\mathcal{U}T
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{G}}
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{UV}}
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{IR}}
e_{R}
e_{G}
e_{B}
e_{1}
e_{2}
e_{1}e_{2}

(VI.24)

This boundary condition does not host a non-invertible symmetry, but rather the
(\mathbb{Z}_{2})^{3}
symmetry with a type III anomaly, consistent with our previous remarks.

We are thus led to the enriched RG-quiche:

T^{2}
T^{2}
T^{2}
\mathcal{U}T
\mathcal{U}T
\mathcal{U}T
N\mathbb{Z}_{T}
[\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}]\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{T}
(\mathbb{Z}_{2})^{3}_{\text{III}}
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathsf{Vec})^{T^{2}}
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})^{\mathcal{U}T}
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8}))

(VI.25)

where

\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}=\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}^{\omega_{\text{III}}}\,.

(VI.26)

We conclude this example by describing the UV/IR map between the generator of the translation symmetry
\mathcal{U}T
in the UV and the emanant
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry in the IR.

In the UV, the action of translation
\mathcal{U}T
is implemented by twist defects
Z_{\rho}
of
\mathcal{U}
, pushed on the
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{UV}}
boundary. There, the
Z_{\rho}
are mapped to invertible objects:

Z_{\rho_{(i,j)}}\mapsto 2\eta^{\mathcal{U}T}\eta_{1}^{i}\eta_{2}^{j}\,,

(VI.27)

where the superscript
\eta^{\mathcal{U}T}
enforces the fact that the (invertible) object is still non-local. Crossing the gauging interface, the tensor functor is simply:

P(\eta^{\mathcal{U}T})=\eta_{3}\,,

(VI.28)

where
\eta_{3}
generates the third (emanant)
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry of the IR description:
