As predicted by our formalism.

Non-invertible LSM Anomaly Matching.
Our SymTFT formulation gives a powerful handle on LSM anomaly-matching in cases where the underlying symmetry
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
is non-invertible. The simplest example is given by the smallest fusion category which is not group-like but has several fiber functors:

\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}=\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8})\,.

(VI.30)

The
\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8})
fusion category has an
S_{3}
outer-automorphism group permuting its three
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
subgroups.
This can be extended to an automorphism of the center
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8}))
which acts by permutation on the RGB labels in Table
3
. The
\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8})
symmetry boundary:

\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8})}=1\oplus e_{RGB}\oplus m_{GB}\oplus m_{RB}\oplus m_{RG}\,,

(VI.31)

is invariant under this automorphism, while the three
\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8})
SPTs
Bhardwaj:2024qrf
;
Warman:2024lir
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{SPT}_{R}}=1\oplus e_{B}\oplus e_{G}\oplus e_{GB}\oplus 2m_{R}\,,

(VI.32)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{SPT}_{G}}=1\oplus e_{R}\oplus e_{B}\oplus e_{RB}\oplus 2m_{G}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{SPT}_{B}}=1\oplus e_{R}\oplus e_{G}\oplus e_{RG}\oplus 2m_{B}\,,

are cyclically permuted:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{SPT}_{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{SPT}_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{SPT}_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}}

(VI.33)

by
\mathcal{U}
which generates the
\mathbb{Z}_{3}\subset S_{3}
automorphism subgroup.

Decorating the translation generator by the triality automorphism
\mathcal{U}
and considering the enriched theory
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8}))^{\mathcal{U}T}
leads to an LSM anomaly between lattice translations and the
\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8})
symmetry.
15
15

15

On the other hand, the three
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{R,G,B}
subgroups of
S_{3}
can give rise to no LSM anomaly, as they always leave the corresponding SPT fixed.

Notice that the three
\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8})
SPTs are indistinguishable by the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
invertible subcategory of
\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8})
alone: the LSM anomaly truly involves the non-invertible symmetry generator.
16
16

16

More precisely, the three SPTs differ in their
\mathcal{D}
-twisted sector
Thorngren:2019iar
. The relevant datum is a group homomorphism
\nu_{R,G,B}
:
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}
assigning a minus sign to two out of the three
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
subgroups. Clearly the permutation action cycles between
\nu_{R,G,B}
providing the map on
\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8})
SPTs.

The lattice translation symmetry acts by an invertible
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
symmetry
\eta^{\mathcal{U}T}
on
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}\subset\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8})
:

\displaystyle\eta^{\mathcal{U}T}\,\eta_{R}=\eta_{G}\,\eta^{\mathcal{U}T}\,,\ \

\displaystyle\eta^{\mathcal{U}T}\,\eta_{G}=\eta_{B}\,\eta^{\mathcal{U}T}\,,

(VI.34)

\displaystyle\eta^{\mathcal{U}T}\,\eta_{B}=\eta_{R}\,\eta^{\mathcal{U}T}\,,\ \

\displaystyle\eta^{\mathcal{U}T}\,\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}\,\eta^{\mathcal{U}T}\,.

In the continuum, the IR matching of the LSM anomalies gives rise to an emanant
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
symmetry. Indeed notice that, since the automorphism
\mathcal{G}
acts on
\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8})
by a simple permutation of the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
subgroups, the gauged SymTFT is
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8})\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{3})
, where now
\rtimes
really denotes the
crossed product category, which is in the same spirit of semi-direct product, but defined for categories.
We discuss the details of this crossed extension
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8})\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{3})
and crossed product
\rtimes
in appendix
A.5
.
The UV boundary condition corresponds to the Lagrangian algebra:

\mathcal{L}_{\text{IR}}=\left(\bigoplus_{a=0,1,2}1^{a}\oplus e_{RGB}^{a}\right)\oplus\mu_{RG}\,,

(VI.35)

with
1^{a}
the dual
\mathsf{Rep}(\mathbb{Z}_{3})
representations and
\mu_{RG}=m_{RG}\oplus m_{RB}\oplus m_{GB}
. This is precisely the Lagrangian algebra which implements the canonical Dirichlet boundary condition in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8})\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{3})
.
The SymTFT description of the whole setup is:

T^{2}
T^{2}
T^{2}
\mathcal{U}T
\mathcal{U}T
\mathcal{U}T
N\mathbb{Z}_{T}
\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8})\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{T}
\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8})\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathsf{Vec})^{T^{2}}
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8}))^{\mathcal{U}T}
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8})\!\!\rtimes\!{\mathbb{Z}}_{3})

(VI.36)

Thus, gauging the
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
symmetry generated by
\mathcal{U}T
, the continuum IR symmetry is:

\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}=\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8})\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{3}\,,

(VI.37)

with
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
generator
\eta_{\text{IR}}
emanating from the lattice translation symmetry. The UV/IR map reads:

P(\eta^{\mathcal{U}T})=\eta_{\text{IR}}\,.

(VI.38)

Due to the crossed product structure, the LSM anomaly is matched by a mixed ’t Hooft anomaly of the
\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8})\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{3}
symmetry.

It would be interesting to extend our construction further to include non-invertible lattice translation symmetries
Seiberg:2023cdc
;
Seiberg:2024gek
and various types of higher self-duality symmetries
Antinucci:2022cdi
;
Diatlyk:2023fwf
;
Choi:2023vgk
;
Lu:2024lzf
;
Lu:2025gpt
.

VII
Conclusions and Outlook

In this work, we have discussed how generalized symmetries can be matched along RG-flows via the mathematics of tensor functors.
We have shown that tensor functors have in turn a simple SymTFT realization, which has allowed us to uncover several interesting aspects of this problem: Perhaps most striking of all the idea that ’t Hooft anomalies for non-invertible symmetries can be explained and quantified using the language of short exact sequences of fusion categories, naturally giving rise to the notion of ASCies.

While we have already given several applications of our construction, we expect that several new avenues are opened by our present work:

1.

A mathematical theory of ASCies.
The theory of short exact sequences of tensor categories – and their possible higher-categorical generalizations – remains subject of current mathematical development
bruguieres2011exact
;
bruguieres2014central
;
natale2020notionexactsequencehopf
;
ETINGOF20171187
. As a result, many structural properties of Anomalous Symmetry Categories (ASCies), as well as their interrelations, are still not fully understood.
As emphasized in this work, developing this mathematical framework offers a promising path toward a deeper understanding of ’t Hooft anomalies in both conventional and categorical settings.

2.

Continuous symmetries.
The present work has focused exclusively on discrete finite symmetries. While it is widely believed that discrete symmetries can already capture the full anomaly structure of continuous ones, the language of tensor categories is inherently suited to describing symmetries with flat (i.e., topological) backgrounds.
Recently, several works have developed a SymTFT-based approach to incorporate continuous symmetries
Antinucci:2024zjp
;
Brennan:2024fgj
;
Bonetti:2024cjk
;
Apruzzi:2024htg
;
Antinucci:2024bcm
. In this context, continuous spacetime symmetries and their anomalies naturally come into play. Our formalism may offer insight into how discrete spacetime symmetries on the lattice – and their associated anomalies – leave an imprint on the structure of their continuum counterparts.

3.

LSM anomaly matching for higher categories.
Our formulation of LSM anomaly matching via the SymTFT framework naturally extends to higher dimensions. These extensions yield simple and computable predictions for LSM anomalies and their matching in the presence of higher-form symmetries.
Moreover, building on techniques from
Aasen:2020jwb
;
Bhardwaj:2024kvy
;
Inamura:2025cum
, the SymTFT may provide a direct route to formulating UV lattice models that realize higher LSM anomalies – and potentially offer insight into their IR dynamics.

4.

Anomalies of Weak and Strong Symmetries.

Recent developments have expended the study of symmetric phases for mixed states. In particular averaged or mixed state SPTs were discussed in
degroot2022
;
ma2023average
;
zhang2022strange
;
sala2024
;
lee2025
for group-symmetries.
Recently a general SymTFT formulation of mixed state phases was put forward including mixed state SPTs in
Schafer-Nameki:2025fiy
;
Luo:2025phx
;
Qi:2025tal
. In
Schafer-Nameki:2025fiy
an extension to non-invertible weak and strong symmetries is developed, and it would be interesting to quantify anomalies for such mixed states combining the approaches of the present paper with that one.

5.

Constraints on RG-interfaces.
As our main motivating example, we have argued that tensor functors are realized by RG-interfaces between CFTs connected via symmetry-preserving relevant deformations. It is natural to expect that the mathematical structure of tensor functors imposes strong constraints on the physics of such interfaces.
For instance, the induced map on generalized charges can identify UV representations that become confined on the interface. It would also be interesting to explore whether phenomena such as symmetry enhancement on conformal interfaces
Gaiotto:2012np
;
Antinucci:2025uvj
;
Choi:2025ebk
can be more naturally understood within our framework.

6.

UV/IR map in the presence of defects.
A crucial ingredient in enriching the UV/IR correspondence is the presence of unscreened dynamical defects in the low-energy effective theory. This is a natural feature in, for example, the Higgs phase of a gauge theory, where Abrikosov–Nielsen–Olesen (ANO) or center vortices appear as massive excitations.
The realization of generalized symmetries on extended dynamical defects has been studied from various perspectives in recent works
Copetti:2024onh
;
Choi:2024tri
;
Antinucci:2024izg
;
Brennan:2025acl
;
Copetti:2024rqj
;
Copetti:2024dcz
;
Cordova:2024iti
;
Gagliano:2025gwr
. These defects serve as prototypical examples of higher-charged objects or higher representations
Bhardwaj:2023ayw
;
Bartsch:2023wvv
. It would be interesting to synthesize these developments to further constrain both bulk and defect RG-flows.

We hope to come back to these and other problems in the near future.

Acknowledgments.

We thank Fabio Apruzzi, Pieter Bomans, Ho Tat Lam, Sahand Seifnashri, Alison Warman, Matt Yu, Yunqin Zheng for discussions.
We also thank the KITP for hospitality during the program GenSym25, during which this collaboration was initiated.
The work of AA, YG, and SSN is supported by the UKRI Frontier Research Grant, underwriting the ERC Advanced Grant “Generalized Symmetries in Quantum Field Theory and Quantum Gravity”. The work of CC and in part of SSN is supported by the STFC grant ST/X000761/1.
This research was supported in part by grant NSF PHY-2309135 to the Kavli Institute for Theoretical Physics (KITP).

Appendix A
Mathematical Background and Proofs

In this appendix, we collect necessary background materials on tensor functors, their properties and their generalization to (2+1)d, along with a review of
G
-crossed product categories and equivariantizations. We also provide some proofs and computations, e.g., of the equivalence between the Matching Equation and the existence of a tensor functor between symmetry categories, and of the triple linking invariance in the (4+1)d twisted Dijkgraaf-Witten theory.

A.1
Mathematics of Tensor Functors

This section summarizes some basic notions from category theory, with a focus on tensor categories and short exact sequence of tensor categories, as needed for the main text.

General Category Theory Language.
We begin with the basic notions associated with functors in category theory. See
(
Weibel_1994
, Appendix A)
for further background on general category-theoretic concepts.

A
functor

F:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
from a category
\mathcal{C}
to a category
\mathcal{D}
is a rule that assigns: an object
F(\mathsf{D}_{1})
of
\mathcal{D}
to every object
\mathsf{D}_{1}
of
\mathcal{C}
, and a morphism
F(f):F(\mathsf{D}_{1})\rightarrow F(\mathsf{D}_{2})
in
\mathcal{D}
to every morphism
f:\mathsf{D}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{D}_{2}
in
\mathcal{C}
, such that
F
preserves the identity morphisms and compositions, i.e.

\displaystyle F(\text{id}_{\mathsf{D}})

\displaystyle=\text{id}_{F(\mathsf{D})}\,,

(A.1)

\displaystyle F(g\circ f)

\displaystyle=F(g)\circ F(f)\,.

(A.2)

A functor
F:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
is faithful if, for each objects
\mathsf{D}_{1},\mathsf{D}_{2}\in\mathcal{D}
, the map between the hom-sets:

\text{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathsf{D}_{1},\mathsf{D}_{2})\rightarrow\text{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}}(F(\mathsf{D}_{1}),F(\mathsf{D}_{2}))\,,

(A.3)

is injective. A functor is called an
embedding
if it is both injective on objects and faithful.

Tensor Category Language.
We now focus on the relevant notions from tensor category theory. We refer to
EGNO
for basic notions on tensor categories, and
bruguieres2011exact
;
natale2020notionexactsequencehopf
for notions associated to exact sequences of tensor categories.

Given two tensor categories
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
, with associators
\alpha
and
\beta
, respectively, a
tensor functor
(or
monoidal functor
)

F:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}

(A.4)

is a functor equipped with a natural isomorphism
J
, called the
tensor structure
or
monoidal structure
:

J_{\mathsf{D}_{1},\mathsf{D}_{2}}:\quad F(\mathsf{D}_{1})\otimes F(\mathsf{D}_{2})\cong F(\mathsf{D}_{1}\otimes\mathsf{D}_{2})

(A.5)

for any objects
\mathsf{D}_{1},\mathsf{D}_{2}
in
\mathcal{C}
, that guarantees the compatibility condition:

\displaystyle J_{\mathsf{D}_{1},\mathsf{D}_{2}\otimes\mathsf{D}_{3}}\circ(\text{id}_{F(\mathsf{D}_{1})}\otimes J_{\mathsf{D}_{2},\mathsf{D}_{3}})\circ\beta_{F(\mathsf{D}_{1}),F(\mathsf{D}_{2}),F(\mathsf{D}_{3})}=

(A.6)

\displaystyle F(\alpha_{\mathsf{D}_{1},\mathsf{D}_{2},\mathsf{D}_{3}})\circ J_{\mathsf{D}_{1}\otimes\mathsf{D}_{2},\mathsf{D}_{3}}\circ(J_{\mathsf{D}_{1},\mathsf{D}_{2}}\otimes\text{id}_{F(\mathsf{D}_{3})})

is satisfied for all objects
\mathsf{D}_{1},\mathsf{D}_{2},\mathsf{D}_{3}
in
\mathcal{C}
. This tensor structure
J
is exactly the map between topological junctions of the UV and IR symmetry categories in figure
4
. We say that a tensor functor
F
, with tensor structure
J
, is compatible with the charge if

\displaystyle b_{n\,1,F(\mathsf{D}_{1}\otimes\mathsf{D}_{2})}^{\alpha}\circ(\text{id}_{n\,1}\otimes J_{\mathsf{D}_{1},\mathsf{D}_{2}})=

(A.7)

\displaystyle(J_{\mathsf{D}_{1},\mathsf{D}_{2}}\otimes\text{id}_{n\,1})\circ b_{n\,1,F(\mathsf{D}_{1})\otimes F(\mathsf{D}_{2})}^{\alpha}\,,

for any objects
\mathsf{D}_{1},\mathsf{D}_{2}
in
\mathcal{C}
and for any half-braiding
b_{n\,1}^{\alpha}
on
n\,1
in
\mathcal{D}
.

We now turn to properties of tensor functors that are essential for defining a short exact sequence of tensor categories. Although many of these have already appeared in the main text, we restate them here for completeness.

A tensor functor
F:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
is
normal
if for every object
\mathsf{D}
of
\mathcal{C}
, there is a subobject
\mathsf{D}_{0}\subset\mathsf{D}
such that
F(\mathsf{D}_{0})
is the largest trivial subobject of
F(\mathsf{D})
. If both
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
are fusion categories,
F
is normal if and only if, for any simple object
\mathsf{D}
in
\mathcal{C}
, if
F(\mathsf{D})
contains a copy of the tensor unit
1
, then
F(\mathsf{D})\cong n\,1
for some natural number
n
. We provide more details about the physical relevance of normal functors in Appendix
A.4
.

For a tensor functor

F:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}\,,

(A.8)

its
kernel
is the full tensor subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
:

\ker(F)=F^{-1}(\langle 1\rangle)\,,

(A.9)

i.e., objects
\mathsf{D}
of
\ker(F)
are such that
F(\mathsf{D})\cong 1^{n}
for some natural number
n
.

A tensor functor
P:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
is
dominant
(or
surjective
) if every object of
\mathcal{D}
is a subobject of
P(\mathsf{D})
for some object
\mathsf{D}
of
\mathcal{C}
. This notion should not be confused with that of an essentially surjective functor: a functor
F:\mathcal{C}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{2}
is essentially surjective if for every object
\mathsf{D}_{2}
of
\mathcal{C}_{2}
, there is object
\mathsf{D}_{1}
of
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and isomorphism
F(\mathsf{D}_{1})\cong\mathsf{D}_{2}
in
\mathcal{C}_{2}
. Throughout,
\text{im}(F)
denotes the essential image.

An
exact sequence of tensor categories
is a sequence

\mathcal{N}\xrightarrow{I}\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{P}\mathcal{S}\,,

(A.10)

where
\mathcal{N}
,
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{S}
are tensor categories,
P
is a surjective tensor functor, and
I
is a full embedding whose essential image is equivalent to
\ker(P)
.

If such an exact sequence exist, we call
\mathcal{N}
a
normal
subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
. If a tensor category
\mathcal{C}
does not have any normal subcategories other than
\mathsf{Vec}
, it is said to be
simple
. (It is also called simple with respect to rank-one module categories, if one considers exact sequences of tensor categories with respect to module categories, see
ETINGOF20171187
;
natale2020notionexactsequencehopf
.)

It was shown in
bruguieres2011exact
that a short exact sequence of fusion categories
\mathcal{N}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
is of the form

\langle A\rangle\rightarrow\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{-\otimes A}\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)\,,

(A.11)

where
A
is a connected (i.e.,
\text{dim}(\Hom(1,A))=1
) self-trivializing (i.e., for any
\mathsf{D}\subset A
,
\mathsf{D}\otimes A\cong nA
for some
n
) semisimple commutative central (i.e., admits a half-braiding, making
A
an algebra in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C})
) algebra in
\mathcal{C}
, and
\langle A\rangle
denotes the full subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
generated by objects in
A
, closed under taking direct sums, subojects and quotients.

A.2
Proof of the Matching Equation

We now prove the equivalence between the condition that an interface satisfies the Matching Equation (
III.13
) and the existence of a tensor functor
F:\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
. This statement holds both when
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
are fusion 1-categories, where
F
is a tensor functor; and when
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
are fusion 2-categories, where
F
is a tensor 2-functor. In what follows, all constructions and arguments are phrased so as to apply uniformly in either setting.

Many of the steps in the proof rely on foundational results from
EGNO
in the fusion 1-category case, and on the corresponding results from
Decoppet:2023uoy
for fusion 2-categories.

To begin, recall that the mathematical structure describing an interface between
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})
and
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}})
is a
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
-
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
-bimodule (1- or 2-)category, or equivalently – from the folded Lagrangian perspective – a
(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\boxtimes\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}^{\text{op}})
-module (1- or 2-)category. Let
\mathcal{M}
denote this
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
-
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
-bimodule category. The Matching Equation (
III.13
) translates to the equivalence of (right)
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
-module categories:

\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\boxtimes_{\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}}\mathcal{M}\cong\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}\,.

(A.12)

From Tensor Functors to Interfaces.

Given a tensor (1- or 2-)functor

F:\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}\,,

(A.13)

the (1- or 2-)category
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
carries the structure of a
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
-
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
-bimodule category, with the left
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
action given by the tensor structure in
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\times\mathcal{M}\

\displaystyle\rightarrow\ \mathcal{M}

\displaystyle(\mathsf{D},m)\

\displaystyle\mapsto\ F(\mathsf{D})\otimes m\,,

(A.14)

and module associator inherited from the associator of
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
(similarly generalize to
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
-
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
-bimodule 2-categories). The universal property of the relative Deligne product guarantees

\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\boxtimes_{\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}}\mathcal{M}\cong\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}

(A.15)

as a
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
-module category. Hence the Matching Equation (
III.13
) is satisfied, with the interface corresponding to the
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
-
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
-bimodule category
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
.

From Interfaces to Tensor Functors.

Conversely, given a
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
-
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
-bimodule category
\mathcal{M}
. In general, as a right
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
-module category,
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\boxtimes_{\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}}\mathcal{M}
decomposes into

\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\boxtimes_{\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}}\mathcal{M}\cong\bigoplus_{i}\mathcal{N}_{i}\,,

(A.16)

where the (1- or 2-)categories
\mathcal{N}_{i}
are indecomposable
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
-module categories, i.e., there are equivalences of right
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
-module categories

\displaystyle T:\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\boxtimes_{\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}}\mathcal{M}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\bigoplus_{i}\mathcal{N}_{i}

(A.17)

\displaystyle S:\bigoplus_{i}\mathcal{N}_{i}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\boxtimes_{\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}}\mathcal{M}\,,

(A.18)

with natural isomorphisms to the identity functors

\displaystyle T\circ S

\displaystyle\cong\text{id}_{\bigoplus_{i}\mathcal{N}_{i}}\,,

(A.19)

\displaystyle S\circ T

\displaystyle\cong\text{id}_{\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\boxtimes_{\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}}\mathcal{M}}\,.

(A.20)

Using the equivalences of categories, one can define the tensor functor on the dual categories

\displaystyle F:\text{Fun}_{\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}},\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})

\displaystyle\rightarrow\text{Fun}_{\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}}\left(\oplus_{i}\mathcal{N}_{i},\oplus_{i}\mathcal{N}_{i}\right)

\displaystyle\psi

\displaystyle\mapsto T\circ(\psi\boxtimes_{\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}}\text{id}_{\mathcal{M}})\circ S\,.

(A.21)

The target category is a tensor (1- or 2-)category if and only if the right
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
-module category
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\boxtimes_{\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}}\mathcal{M}
is indecomposable (see section 7.12 of
EGNO
for fusion 1-categories and
(
Decoppet:2023uoy
, Corollary 5.2.5)
for fusion 2-categories).

Moreover, together with the tensor equivalence

\text{Fun}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C})\cong\mathcal{C}

(A.22)

for any fusion category
\mathcal{C}
(valid in both the 1-categorical and 2-categorical settings), it is when the Matching Equation (
III.13
) holds, i.e.,

\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\boxtimes_{\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}}\mathcal{M}\cong\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}

(A.23)

as right
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
-module categories, we have a tensor functor (or tensor 2-functor) between symmetry categories

F:\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}\,.

(A.24)

A.3
Drinfeld Center of
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})

In this appendix we summarize some of the details of the SymTFT for
S_{3}
finite non-abelian group, its condensable algebras and folded Lagrangians obtained in
Bhardwaj:2023bbf
;
Bhardwaj:2024qrf
. This is used in the main text.
We present
S_{3}
as

S_{3}=\langle a,b\ |\ a^{3}=b^{2}=1\ ,\ \ bab=a^{2}\rangle\ .

(A.25)

The Drinfeld center
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3}))=\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{S_{3}})
is group-theoretical, and its anyons are labeled by
([g],R)
, where
[g]
is a conjugacy class of
S_{3}
and
R
an irreducible representation of its centralizer group.
There are three conjugacy classes:
[1]
with maximal centralizer, hence its representations are
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
generated by
1,P,E
, which are the trivial, sign and 2d irreducible representations, where
E\otimes P=E
, and

E\otimes E=1\oplus P\oplus E\,.

(A.26)

The two non-trivial conjugacy classes are

•

[a]=\left\{a,a^{2}\right\}
whose centralizer is
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
, hence we label the corresponding lines by
a_{\chi=0,1,2}
.

•

[b]=\left\{b,ab,a^{2}b\right\}
whose centralizer is
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, hence we label these lines by
b_{\pm}
.

The canonical
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
symmetry Lagrangian algebra is

\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})}=1\oplus a_{0}\oplus b_{+}\ .

(A.27)

The various condensable algebras (including the Lagrangian) assemble into the following Hasse diagram
Bhardwaj:2024qrf
:

1
1\oplus a_{0}
1\oplus P
1\oplus E
1\oplus a_{0}\oplus b_{+}
1\oplus P\oplus 2a_{0}
1\oplus P\oplus 2E
1\oplus b_{+}\oplus E

(A.28)

There are three (non-trivial) condensable but non-Lagrangian algebras

\mathcal{A}_{a_{0}}=1\oplus a_{0}\ ,\ \ \ \ \mathcal{A}_{P}=1\oplus P\ ,\ \ \ \ \mathcal{A}_{E}=1\oplus E\ .

(A.29)

The last two, of dimensions
2
and
3
respectively, are magnetic. Their corresponding folded Lagrangian algebras have been computed in
Bhardwaj:2023bbf
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{(E)}_{\text{folded}}

\displaystyle=1\oplus E\oplus P\,\overline{m}\oplus E\,\overline{m}\oplus b_{+}\,\overline{e}\oplus b_{-}\,\overline{em}

(A.30)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{(P)}_{\text{folded}}

\displaystyle=1\oplus P\oplus a_{0}\overline{L}_{1,0}\oplus a_{0}\overline{L}_{2,0}\oplus a_{2}\overline{L}_{1,1}\oplus a_{2}\overline{L}_{2,2}\oplus

\displaystyle\oplus E\overline{L}_{0,1}\oplus E\overline{L}_{0,2}\oplus a_{1}\overline{L}_{1,2}\oplus a_{1}\overline{L}_{2,1}

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{folded}}^{(a_{0})}

\displaystyle=1\oplus a_{0}\oplus P\,\overline{m}\oplus a_{0}\,\overline{m}\oplus b_{+}\,\overline{e}\oplus b_{-}\,\overline{em}\ .

A.4
Normal Functors and Subcategories

For non-invertible symmetries, a particularly important class of functors
F:\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
is given by normal functors (
II.10
). For invertible symmetries, every tensor functor is automatically normal. Intuitively, normal functors avoid situations where a simple non-invertible defect
D
in the UV flows to a sum of defects in the IR, where some of which act trivially and others non-trivially. Thus the kernel of a normal functor is sufficiently large to capture the entire portion of the UV symmetry that acts trivially in the IR.

A normal tensor functor has a simple characterization in terms of the interface
\mathcal{I}
. We recall that by the folding trick the interface is equivalent to a boundary of
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})\boxtimes\overline{\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}})}
, described by the folded Lagrangian

\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{I}}=\bigoplus_{i,j}n_{i,j}a^{\text{UV}}_{i}\otimes\overline{b}^{\text{IR}}_{j}\,.

(A.31)

The coefficient
n_{i,j}
is non-zero whenever the
a_{i}^{\text{UV}}
can transmute to
b_{j}^{\text{IR}}
through the interface. If
b_{j}^{\text{IR}}\subset\mathcal{L}_{\text{IR}}
, a defect
a_{i}^{\text{UV}}
with
n_{i,j}\neq 0
is the lift to the bulk of a symmetry defect that can be mapped to trivial by the tensor functor. Thus the condition for normality is

\forall b_{i}^{\text{UV}},n_{i,j}\neq 0,a_{j}^{\text{IR}}\subset\mathcal{L}_{\text{IR}},\Longrightarrow n_{i,k}=0\ \forall a_{k}^{\text{IR}}\not\subset\mathcal{L}_{\text{IR}}\ .

(A.32)

As we will see shortly, normal functors are particularly interesting if they are also surjective, as their kernel define normal subcategories.
17
17

17

One can always restrict the target category to the image of a normal functor, hence making the functor surjective.
In this case the condition for normality can be rephrased into a simpler one in terms of the condensable magnetic algebra
\mathcal{A}
of
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})
that defines the functor. The surjective functor is normal if and only if there is a subalgebra
\mathcal{A}_{I}\subset\mathcal{L}_{\text{UV}}
such that
\mathcal{A}_{I}\otimes\mathcal{A}
is a condensable algebra of
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})
, and it is Lagrangian.

An Example:
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
.
An illustrative example is given by a
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
symmetry, whose simple objects are
1,P,E
:

E\otimes P=E,\ \ P\otimes P=1,\ \ E\otimes E=1\oplus P\oplus E\ .

(A.33)

\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
has two natural surjective tensor functors given by the restriction to its
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
subgroups:

\displaystyle F_{2}:\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathsf{Rep}(\mathbb{Z}_{2})

\displaystyle F_{3}:\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathsf{Rep}(\mathbb{Z}_{3})

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle\mapsto\eta

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle\mapsto 1

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle\mapsto 1\oplus\eta\,,

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle\mapsto\omega\oplus\omega^{2}\,.

(A.34)

Here
\eta
and
\omega
are the generators of
\mathsf{Rep}(\mathbb{Z}_{2})=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and
\mathsf{Rep}(\mathbb{Z}_{3})=\mathbb{Z}_{3}
respectively.
F_{3}
is a normal functor, while
F_{2}
is not.

Let us derive these facts from the SymTFT. From some known facts on
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3}))
in Appendix
A.3
, there are two (non-Lagrangian) magnetic algebras
\mathcal{A}_{P},\mathcal{A}_{E}
of dimensions
2
and
3
respectively.

We start with
\mathcal{A}_{E}=1\oplus E
. Its condensation produces
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathbb{Z}_{2})
, whose lines we label by
1,e,m,em
. The folded Lagrangian associated with the interface
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}_{E}}
between
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3}))
and
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}})
is
Bhardwaj:2023bbf
:

\mathcal{L}^{(E)}_{\text{folded}}=1\oplus E\oplus P\,\overline{m}\oplus E\,\overline{m}\oplus b_{+}\,\overline{e}\oplus b_{-}\,\overline{em}\ .

(A.35)

This gives a map of bulk lines across the interface, from which we read that
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})}=1\oplus a_{0}\oplus b_{+}
is mapped into
1\oplus e
, thus
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}_{E}}
satisfies the Matching Equation (
III.13
). From (
A.35
) we can read that the interface maps
P\mapsto m
and
E\mapsto 1\oplus m
, and thus defines a functor that coincides with
F_{2}
of (
A.4
) (
m
becomes
\eta
on
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
via the forgetful functor).

Notice that (
A.35
) does not satisfy (
A.32
), as expected, given that
F_{2}
is not a normal functor. Moreover, the only non-trivial subalgebra of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})}
is
\mathcal{A}_{a_{0}}=1\oplus a_{0}
, but
\mathcal{A}_{a_{0}}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{E}
is not a condensable algebra, so the criterion for surjective normal functor is also not satisfied.

On the other hand, condensing
\mathcal{A}_{P}=1\oplus P
produces
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{3}})=\text{DW}(\mathbb{Z}_{3})
, whose lines we label by
L_{n,m}
,
n,m\in\{0,1,2\}
, and the folded Lagrangian is

\begin{array}[]{r}\mathcal{L}^{(P)}_{\text{folded}}=1\oplus P\oplus a_{0}\overline{L}_{1,0}\oplus a_{0}\overline{L}_{2,0}\oplus a_{2}\overline{L}_{1,1}\oplus a_{2}\overline{L}_{2,2}\oplus\\
\oplus E\overline{L}_{0,1}\oplus E\overline{L}_{0,2}\oplus a_{1}\overline{L}_{1,2}\oplus a_{1}\overline{L}_{2,1}\end{array}

(A.36)

The Matching Equation is satisfied with
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{Z}_{3}}=1\oplus L_{1,0}\oplus L_{2,0}
, and the action on
P
and
E
reproduces
F_{3}
from (
A.4
). Now the condition (
A.32
) is satisfied. The same is true for the condition about surjective normal functors, as

\mathcal{A}_{a_{0}}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{P}=1\oplus P\oplus 2a_{0}

(A.37)

is a Lagrangian algebra.

Now we return to the embedding of the anomaly-free subcategory of
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
, and see how it results in a short exact sequence of fusion categories.
Condensing
\mathcal{A}_{I}=\mathcal{A}_{a_{0}}=1\oplus a_{0}
produces
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}})
, and its corresponding folded Lagrangian in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Rep}({S_{3}}))\boxtimes\overline{\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}})}
is
Bhardwaj:2023bbf
:

\mathcal{L}_{I}^{a_{0}}=1\oplus a_{0}\oplus P\,\overline{m}\oplus a_{0}\,\overline{m}\oplus b_{+}\,\overline{e}\oplus b_{-}\,\overline{em}\ .

(A.38)

We see that (
III.13
) is satisfied if
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\text{UV}}
corresponds to
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}=1\oplus e
. Thus we get a tensor functor
I:\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\rightarrow\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
that maps the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
generator
\eta=\pi_{\text{UV}}(m)
, where
\pi_{\text{UV}}:\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}})\rightarrow\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
denotes the forgetful functor, into
I(\eta)=P
. We see explicitly that
F_{3}\circ I
is a fiber functor, and we get an exact sequence of categories

\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\rightarrow\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})\rightarrow\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{3}}\ .

(A.39)

This is a particular case of a general result: for any finite group
G
and
N\triangleleft G
normal, there is an exact sequence of categories
bruguieres2011exact

\mathsf{Rep}(G/N)\rightarrow\mathsf{Rep}(G)\rightarrow\mathsf{Rep}(N)\ .

(A.40)

Example: Fibonacci Categories.

We provide another example of a non-normal functor.
Consider the Fibonacci category
\mathsf{Fib}
with simple objects 1 and
\tau
, and the fusion rule:

\tau\otimes\tau=1\oplus\tau\,.

(A.41)

There is a surjective tensor functor

\displaystyle P:\mathsf{Fib}\boxtimes\mathsf{Fib}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathsf{Fib}

\displaystyle\tau\boxtimes 1

\displaystyle\mapsto\tau\,,

\displaystyle 1\boxtimes\tau

\displaystyle\mapsto\tau\,,

\displaystyle\tau\boxtimes\tau

\displaystyle\mapsto 1\oplus\tau\,.

(A.42)

Note that this functor is not normal and has trivial kernel

\ker(P)\cong\mathsf{Vec}\,.

(A.43)

Still, one can view the target category
\mathsf{Fib}
as the category of modules over the algebra
A=1\oplus(\tau\boxtimes\tau)
in
\mathsf{Fib}\boxtimes\mathsf{Fib}
:

\mathsf{Fib}\cong(\mathsf{Fib}\boxtimes\mathsf{Fib})_{A}\,.

(A.44)

This algebra
A
fails to define a normal functor as it is not self-trivializing:

(\tau\boxtimes\tau)\otimes A\not\cong nA

(A.45)

for any
n
. In this case, the kernel of
P
fails to agree with the full subcategory generated by
A
.
This aligns with the observation that there is no non-trivial condensable algebras in
\mathsf{Fib}^{\boxtimes n}

BOOKER2012176
;
Kong:2013aya
;
Neupert:2016pjk
.

A.5
G
-Crossed Categories and Equivariantizations

This appendix consists of the notion of a crossed product category, and its related short exact sequence of tensor categories, which are important to the study of symmetry enrichment and LSM anomaly matching. We also review a mathematical theorem and see how it computes the
G
-equivariantization of a
G
-crossed braided extension of the Drinfeld center.

Crossed Product Categories.

We first review the construction of a crossed product category following
tambara2001invariants
. In what follows we only present the categorical structure on objects, and we refer to
tambara2001invariants
for the definition on morphisms, associator and unitors.

Given any (not necessarily finite) group
G
acting on a tensor category
\mathcal{C}
,

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle\rightarrow\text{Aut}^{\otimes}(\mathcal{C})

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle\mapsto\rho_{g}\,.

(A.46)

One can define the crossed product category
\mathcal{C}\rtimes G
. As categories,

\mathcal{C}\rtimes G=\bigoplus_{g\in G}\mathcal{C}\boxtimes g\,,

(A.47)

where the notation
\mathcal{C}\boxtimes g\cong\mathcal{C}
for every
g\in G
. An object in
\mathcal{C}\rtimes G
is denoted by

\bigoplus_{g\in G}\mathsf{D}\boxtimes g

(A.48)

with
\mathsf{D}\in\mathcal{C}
, and fusion rules can be computed from

(\mathsf{D}_{1}\boxtimes g_{1})\otimes(\mathsf{D}_{2}\boxtimes g_{2})=(\mathsf{D}_{1}\otimes\rho_{g_{1}}(\mathsf{D}_{2}))\boxtimes g_{1}g_{2}\,.

(A.49)

Note that for any subgroup
H\subset G
,
\mathcal{C}\rtimes G
has a full fusion subcategory equivalent to
\mathsf{Vec}_{H}
generated by objects of the form

1\boxtimes h\,,

(A.50)

for
h\in H
.

Short Exact Sequences from Crossed Product Categories.

Given any
G
-action on a tensor category
\mathcal{C}
as before, if a normal subgroup
N\triangleleft G
acts trivially on
\mathcal{C}
, i.e., the
G
-action on
\mathcal{C}
factors through a
G/N
-action

G/N\rightarrow\text{Aut}^{\otimes}(\mathcal{C})\,,

(A.51)

then there is a short exact sequence of tensor categories

\mathsf{Vec}_{N}\xrightarrow{I}\mathcal{C}\rtimes G\xrightarrow{P}\mathcal{C}\rtimes G/N\,,

(A.52)

where
I
is the embedding of the full subcategory
\mathsf{Vec}_{N}
as discussed above, and the surjective functor
P
has trivial tensor structure, compatible with the associators as defined in
tambara2001invariants
, and maps object
\mathsf{D}\boxtimes g
in
\mathcal{C}\rtimes G
to

P(\mathsf{D}\boxtimes g)=\mathsf{D}\boxtimes gN\in\mathcal{C}\rtimes G/N\,.

(A.53)

Similarly, one can define its map on hom-sets, which we do not elaborate here.
Note that
\text{im}(I)=\ker(P)
, hence
P
is a normal functor.

Equivariantization of Crossed Extensions.

We now assume
G
to be a finite group and turn to the
G
-equivariantization of a
G
-crossed braided extension of
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C})
18
18

18

In general, there can be multiple
G
-crossed (braided) extensions of a given (braided) category.
,
which shows up in the description of the IR TO in section
VI
.

It is shown in
(
2009arXiv0905.3117G
, Theorem 3.5)
that there is an equivalence of braided fusion categories

\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{C}\rtimes G)^{G}\cong\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}\rtimes G)\,,

(A.54)

where
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{C}\rtimes G)^{G}
denotes the
G
-equivariantization of the relative center
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{C}\rtimes G)
, which is a braided
G
-crossed fusion category

\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{C}\rtimes G)=\bigoplus_{g\in G}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{C}\boxtimes g)\,,

(A.55)

with objects of
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{C}\boxtimes g)
being pairs
(\mathsf{D}\boxtimes g,\gamma)
, where
\mathsf{D}\in\mathcal{C}
and
\gamma
the natural isomorphisms

\gamma_{\mathsf{D}^{\prime}}:\mathsf{D}^{\prime}\otimes\mathsf{D}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathsf{D}\otimes g(\mathsf{D}^{\prime})\,,

(A.56)

for each
\mathsf{D}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
, satisfying natural compatibility conditions. Note that the trivially-graded component
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{C}\boxtimes e)=\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C})
, where
e\in G
is the identity element. One can conclude that
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{C}\rtimes G)
is indeed a
G
-crossed braided extension of
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C})
.

Applying the above result with
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8})
and
G=\mathbb{Z}_{3}
, we conclude the
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
-equivariantization of the
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
-crossed extension of
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8}))
is

\mathcal{Z}_{\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8})}(\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8})\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{3})^{\mathbb{Z}_{3}}\cong\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8})\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{3})\,.

(A.57)

Explicitly, the trivially-graded component of the
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
-crossed extension
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8})}(\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8})\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{3})
is simply
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8}))
itself, and for
a=1,2
, the
a
-graded component contains objects

\begin{split}&1_{a}\,,\,(e_{\text{RGB}})_{a}\,,\,(s_{\text{RGB}})_{a}\,,\,(\bar{s}_{\text{RGB}})_{a}\,,\\
&(e_{\text{R}}\oplus e_{\text{G}}\oplus e_{\text{B}})_{a}\,,\,(e_{\text{RG}}\oplus e_{\text{GB}}\oplus e_{\text{RB}})_{a}\,,\\
&(m_{\text{R}}\oplus m_{\text{G}}\oplus m_{\text{B}})_{a}\,,\,(m_{\text{RG}}\oplus m_{\text{GB}}\oplus m_{\text{RB}})_{a}\,,\\
&(f_{\text{R}}\oplus f_{\text{G}}\oplus f_{\text{B}})_{a}\,,(f_{\text{RG}}\oplus f_{\text{GB}}\oplus f_{\text{RB}})_{a}\,.\end{split}

(A.58)

The fusion rules are induced from the fusion rules of
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8}))
and the group law of the grading.

A.6
Tensor Functors and ASCies in (2+1)d

As suggested earlier, we would like to use the SymTFT approach to study tensor functors and ASCies, in order to formulate a higer-dimensional version of these notions – without having to revert to higher tensor functors. This is physically extremely well-motivated. In the case of (2+1)d, i.e. fusion 2-categories, we can make some of this very precise as the SymTFT for such symmetries is extremely well-developed.

The first important point is that all so-called bosonic fusion 2-categories have been classified
decoppet2022drinfeld
and their Drinfeld center is given by
\mathfrak{Z}(2\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
for a finite 0-form symmetry group
G
with anomaly
\omega\in H^{4}(G,U(1))
.

First we would like to generalize the notion of tensor functors to fusion 2-categories, i.e. define a functor

F:\ \mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}\to\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}\,,

(A.59)

where the symmetry categories in the UV and IR are now 2-categories, describes symmetries of
(2+1)
d systems. Examples are 0-form and 1-form symmetries, as well as 2-group symmetries.
Although tensor 2-functors have been defined in
baez1996higher
, there is not too much detailed study of properties of 2-functors in the literature. Instead, we use the alternative definition in terms of SymTFT interfaces, developed in section
III
.
Gapped boundary conditions and interfaces for the SymTFTs of any bosonic fusion 2-category have been classified in
Bhardwaj:2024qiv
;
Bhardwaj:2025piv
;
Bhardwaj:2025jtf
;
Wen:2025thg
.

Tensor 2-Functors and SymTFT Interfaces.

A tensor 2-functor (
A.59
)
is equivalent to an RG-interface
\mathcal{I}_{F}
that satisfies the Matching Equation (
III.13
), i.e., an interface that maps the
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
symmetry boundary Lagrangian algebra
\mathcal{L}_{\text{UV}}
to the
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
symmetry boundary Lagrangian algebra
\mathcal{L}_{\text{IR}}
. This is proven in appendix
A.2
using general results from
Decoppet:2023uoy
. Thus, although the properties of tensor 2-functor may be hard to check, the SymTFT formulation is extremely useful.

In order to quantify anomalies, we also need to define the short exact sequence of fusion 2-categories

\mathcal{N}\xrightarrow{I}\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{P}\mathcal{S}\,.

(A.60)

Neither injective nor surjective 2-functors are defined in the literature. We use our SymTFT setup as a starting point:
instead of defining 2-functor
I:\mathcal{N}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
to be an embedding, we consider an interface
\mathcal{I}_{I}
such that any topological defect that ends on the symmetry boundary
\mathcal{C}
has to end on the interface
\mathcal{I}_{I}
, i.e., the interface corresponds to condensation of a subalgebra of the symmetry Lagrangian algebra, similar to (
III.30
).

Likewise, the placeholder for a 2-functor
P:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
to be surjective (dominant), is an interface where only topological defect that can end on both the
\mathcal{C}
-symmetry boundary and the interface
\mathcal{I}_{P}
to be the trivial defects, analogous to (
III.33
).

The exactness of (
A.60
)
can again be formulated as follows: any topological defect in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{N})
gets mapped, after passing through the interfaces
\mathcal{I}_{I}
and
\mathcal{I}_{P}
, to a topological defect in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{S})
that ends on the symmetry boundary; and conversely, any topological defect in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{S})
that can end on the symmetry boundary arises from a topological defect in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{N})
. This is similar to figure
9
.

UV-IR Symmetries and ASCies.

In
(2+1)
d, fixing symmetry 2-categories
\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}}
for the UV and IR theories, an interface between the corresponding SymTFT
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})
and
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}})
is described by a Lagrangian algebra in the folded theory

\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{UV}})\boxtimes\overline{\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{IR}})}\,.

(A.61)

As the center of all (bosonic) fusion 2-categories are group-theoretical, the SymTFTs for the UV and IR symmetries are described by

\mathfrak{Z}(2\mathsf{Vec}_{G_{\text{UV}}}^{\omega_{\text{UV}}})\,,\quad\mathfrak{Z}(2\mathsf{Vec}_{G_{\text{IR}}}^{\omega_{\text{IR}}})\,,

(A.62)

for some finite groups
G_{\text{UV}}
,
G_{\text{IR}}
and cocycles
\omega_{\text{UV}}\in H^{4}(G_{\text{UV}},U(1))
,
\omega_{\text{IR}}\in H^{4}(G_{\text{IR}},U(1))
. Furthermore, a Lagrangian algebra in

\mathcal{Z}(2\mathsf{Vec}_{G_{\text{UV}}\times G_{\text{IR}}}^{\omega_{\text{UV}}\overline{\omega_{\text{IR}}}})\,,

(A.63)

corresponds to an interface between the two topological orders.

Examples.

As an example, consider a short exact sequence of finite groups

1\rightarrow N\rightarrow G\xrightarrow{p}G/N\rightarrow 1\,.

(A.64)

Associated to this, there is a short exact sequence of fusion 2-categories

2\mathsf{Vec}_{N}\rightarrow 2\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{p^{*}\omega}\xrightarrow{P}2\mathsf{Vec}_{G/N}^{\omega}\,,

(A.65)

for
\omega\in H^{4}(G/N,U(1))
, where
P
is induced from the projection
p:G\rightarrow G/N
. The interface corresponding to
P
is the condensation interface labeled by
\mathcal{I}_{(G,N,\omega,\mathsf{Vec}_{G^{\text{diag}}})}
following the notation in
Bhardwaj:2025jtf
.

Likewise, for any short exact sequence of 2-groups (in terms of fiber sequences, or crossed-modules)

1\rightarrow\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}\xrightarrow{p}\mathcal{K}\rightarrow 1\,,

(A.66)

and anomaly
\omega\in H^{4}(B\mathcal{K},U(1))
, we propose a short exact sequence of fusion 2-categories

2\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathcal{H}}\rightarrow 2\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathcal{G}}^{p^{*}\omega}\rightarrow 2\mathsf{Vec}_{\mathcal{K}}^{\omega}\,.

(A.67)

The bosonic fusion 2-categories are classified
Decoppet:2024htz
in terms of group-theoretical data and fusion 1-categories. This would open up a way to classification of ASCies for symmetry 2-categories to be possible by combining short exact sequences of groups and fusion 1-categories, and it would be very interesting to develop this direction in the future.

What this appendix shows is that although not all the higher categorical structures may be known by mathematicians, the physical approach using the SymTFT reformulation is powerful and provides a useful way to extend our results to higher dimensions. We have sees this at work in section
V
.

A.7
Link Invariants and Algebras in (4+1)d

In this appendix we briefly detail the computation of (triple) link invariants for the (4+1) dimensional twisted Dijkgraaf-Witten theory. Our methods follow closely
Putrov:2016qdo
and are a slight generalization of
Kaidi:2023maf
. These are used in section
V
.

Link invariants.
First we write down the action in the presence of multiple
\bm{Q}_{3}^{(n_{m},l)}
defects:

\displaystyle S_{\text{SymTFT}}[\Sigma_{i},(n_{m}^{i},l^{i})]

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi i}{N}\int\biggl[(da+\sum_{i}\,n_{m}^{i}\text{PD}(\Sigma_{i}))\,b

(A.68)

\displaystyle+\left(\sum_{i}l^{i}\text{PD}(\Sigma_{i})+k\beta(a)\right)a\,\beta(a)\biggr]\,,

where
PD
denotes the Poincare dual. Let
\widehat{\Sigma}_{i}
be a surface filling
\Sigma_{i}
, then
d\text{PD}(\widehat{\Sigma_{i}})=\text{PD}(\Sigma_{i})
. After the change variables
a\to a-\sum_{i}\,n_{m}^{i}\text{PD}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{i})
, the action picks up a phase (we assume that all the surfaces have trivial self-intersection):

\displaystyle S_{\text{SymTFT}}[\Sigma_{i},(n_{m}^{i},l^{i})]=S_{\text{SymTFT}}

(A.69)

\displaystyle+\frac{2\pi i}{N^{2}}\sum_{i>j,k}\int 2n_{m}^{i}n_{m}^{j}l^{k}\int\text{PD}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{i})d\text{PD}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{j})d\text{PD}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{k})

\displaystyle-\frac{2\pi i}{N^{3}}\sum_{i>j>k}6kn_{m}^{i}n_{m}^{j}n_{m}^{k}\int\text{PD}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{i})d\text{PD}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{j})d\text{PD}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{k})\,.

The term inside the integral is the type-2 linking number:

\text{Lk}(\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2},\Sigma_{3})_{2}=\int\text{PD}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{1})d\text{PD}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{2})d\text{PD}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{3})\,.

(A.70)

We conclude that the triple linking between the magnetic operators
\bm{Q}_{3}^{(n_{m},l)}
is

\displaystyle\langle\bm{Q}_{3}^{(n_{m}^{1},l^{1})}\bm{Q}_{3}^{(n_{m}^{2},l^{2})}\bm{Q}_{3}^{(n_{m}^{3},l^{3})}\rangle=

(A.71)

\displaystyle\exp\biggl[\frac{4\pi i\text{Lk}(\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2},\Sigma_{3})_{2}}{N^{2}}\biggl(n_{m}^{1}n_{m}^{2}l^{3}+n_{m}^{1}n_{m}^{3}l^{2}\biggr.

\displaystyle+n_{m}^{2}n_{m}^{2}l^{1}-\frac{3k}{N}n_{m}^{1}n_{m}^{2}n_{m}^{3}\biggr)\biggr]\,.

Furthermore, if we shift
n_{m}\to n_{m}+N
, changing variables to
a-N\,\text{PD}(\widehat{\Sigma})
imposes the identification:

\bm{Q}_{3}^{N,0}\sim\bm{Q}_{3}^{0,3k}\,,

(A.72)

under which the triple linking is left invariant.
In order to discuss the triple linking more carefully, we can also consider decoration of one-dimensional intersections
\gamma_{ij}=\Sigma_{i}\cap\Sigma_{j}
by electric lines
\bm{Q}_{1}^{n_{e}^{ij}}
. The decorated triple linking is:

\displaystyle\langle\bm{Q}_{3}^{(n_{m}^{1},l^{1})}\bm{Q}_{3}^{(n_{m}^{2},l^{2})}\bm{Q}_{3}^{(n_{m}^{3},l^{3})}\rangle_{n_{e}^{ij}}=

(A.73)

\displaystyle\exp\biggl[\frac{4\pi i\text{Lk}(\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2},\Sigma_{3})_{2}}{N^{2}}\biggl(n_{m}^{1}n_{m}^{2}l^{3}+n_{m}^{1}n_{m}^{3}l^{2}

\displaystyle+n_{m}^{2}n_{m}^{2}l^{1}-\frac{3k}{N}n_{m}^{1}n_{m}^{2}n_{m}^{3}\biggr)

\displaystyle+\frac{2\pi i}{N}(n_{m}^{1}n_{e}^{23}+n_{m}^{2}n_{e}^{13}+n_{m}^{3}n_{e}^{12})\biggr]\,.

The decorated linking is invariant under the redefinitions:

\displaystyle n_{m}^{1}

\displaystyle\to n_{m}^{1}+N\,,

(A.74)

\displaystyle l^{1}

\displaystyle\to l^{1}+3k\,,

\displaystyle n_{e}^{13}

\displaystyle\to n_{e}^{13}-2l^{3}\,,

\displaystyle n_{e}^{12}

\displaystyle\to n_{e}^{12}-2l^{2}\,,

and similarly for the other labels.

Condensable algebras.

We now discuss condensable algebras in the (4+1)d Dijkgraaf-Witten theory with trivial twist
k=0
. These are described by a set of objects, together with the assignment of electric lines
\widetilde{\bm{Q}}_{1}^{m_{e}^{ij}}
on codimension-2 intersections of the algebra generators. These lines are needed to ensure mutual locality.

Apart from electric and magnetic Lagrangian algebras:

\mathcal{L}_{e}=\Big\langle\widetilde{\bm{Q}}_{1}^{(n_{e})}\Big\rangle\,,\quad\mathcal{L}_{m}=\Big\langle\widetilde{\bm{Q}}_{3}^{(n_{m},0)}\Big\rangle\,,

(A.75)

with
m_{e}^{ij}=0
, dyonic algebras are also present.
The algebra describing the
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{(2)}\times^{\nu}\mathbb{Z}_{n}^{(0)}
boundary condition is:

\mathcal{L}_{\text{IR}}=\Big\langle\widetilde{\bm{Q}}_{1}^{(Nr_{e})}\,,\ \widetilde{\bm{Q}}_{3}^{(Nr_{m},Ns)}\Big\rangle\,,

(A.76)

together with the assignment of lines at codimension-2 junctions:

m_{e}^{ij}=-(r_{m}^{i}s^{j}+r_{m}^{j}s^{i})+N\eta^{ij}_{e}\,.

(A.77)

The triple linking is:

\displaystyle\Big\langle\widetilde{\bm{Q}}_{3}^{(Nr_{m}^{1},Ns^{1})}\widetilde{\bm{Q}}_{3}^{(Nr_{m}^{2},Ns^{2})}\widetilde{\bm{Q}}_{3}^{(Nr_{m}^{3},Ns^{3})}\Big\rangle=

(A.78)

\displaystyle\exp\biggl[\frac{4\pi i}{N}\left[r_{m}^{1}r_{m}^{2}s^{3}+r_{m}^{1}r_{m}^{3}s^{2}+r_{m}^{3}r_{m}^{2}s^{1}\right]\biggr]\,,

and we can immediately verify that the choice of dressing is the correct one.

The non-maximal condensable algebras which are relevant for our discussions are variations of
\mathcal{L}_{m}
:

\mathcal{A}_{k}=\Big\langle\widetilde{Q}_{3}^{(Nr_{m},0)}\Big\rangle\,,\quad m_{e}^{ij}=N\widetilde{k}r_{m}^{i}r_{m}^{j}\,,

(A.79)

where
\widetilde{k}=-2^{-1}k^{-1}
. Let us consider in detail the reduced TO
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{(0)})/\mathcal{A}_{k}
. Clearly we have electric lines:

\bm{Q}_{1}^{n_{e}}\equiv\widetilde{\bm{Q}}_{1}^{Nn_{e}}\,.

(A.80)

We now study the spectrum of surface operators. Consider the triple linking:

\displaystyle\Big\langle\widetilde{\bm{Q}}_{3}^{(Nr_{m},0)}\widetilde{\bm{Q}}_{3}^{(Nr_{m}^{\prime},0)}\widetilde{\bm{Q}}_{3}^{(n,l)}\Big\rangle=

(A.81)

\displaystyle\exp\biggl[\frac{2\pi i}{N^{2}}\Bigl(r_{m}r_{m}^{\prime}(2l+N\widetilde{k}n)+N(r_{m}\mu^{\prime}+r_{m}^{\prime}\mu)\Bigr)\biggl]\,,

where
\mu
denotes the dressing of the junction between
\widetilde{\bm{Q}}_{3}^{(Nr_{m},0)}
and
\widetilde{\bm{Q}}_{3}^{(n,l)}
.
The equation is solved by
l=Ns
,
\mu=r_{m}\theta
and
2s+\widetilde{k}n+2\theta\equiv 0
(mod
N
). Next we must consider a triple linking process involving only a single algebra object:

\displaystyle\Big\langle\widetilde{\bm{Q}}_{3}^{(Nr_{m},0)}\widetilde{\bm{Q}}_{3}^{(n,Ns)}\widetilde{\bm{Q}}_{3}^{(n^{\prime},Ns^{\prime})}\Big\rangle=

(A.82)

\displaystyle\exp\biggl[\frac{2\pi i}{N^{2}}\bigl(2r_{m}(sn^{\prime}+ns^{\prime})+r_{m}(n\theta^{\prime}+n^{\prime}\theta)\bigr)\biggl]\,,

eliminating
\theta
we find:

ns^{\prime}+n^{\prime}s-\widetilde{k}^{-1}nn^{\prime}\equiv 0\mod N^{2}\,,

(A.83)

which implies that
s=-kn
. Thus the remaining surface operators are:

\bm{Q}_{3}^{(n_{m},0)}=\widetilde{\bm{Q}}_{3}^{(n_{m},-kNn)}\,.

(A.84)

Their triple linking is consistent with
k
units of
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
anomaly.
